Summary

Federal support for reproductive health services—preventive, diagnostic, and treatment services related to reproductive systems, functions, and processes—is administered in different ways, largely because federal agencies, departments, and programs have different purposes.

The payment for and provision of reproductive health services remains of ongoing interest to Congress, which regularly considers bills and enacts laws related to various aspects of reproductive health care. This includes policies that expand or restrict the types of reproductive health services available, how they are paid for or provided, and the restrictions in place on paying for or providing certain types of reproductive health services.

This report provides answers to frequently asked questions concerning the coverage, funding, and provision of reproductive health services in the United States. Specifically, it discusses six categories of reproductive health services with regard to whether the federal government pays for these services, requires certain health insurance plans to cover them, or provides them. The six categories addressed in this report are

* 1. contraception and contraception counseling;
* 2. abortion and abortion counseling;
* 3. infertility-related services;
* 4. maternity services;
* 5. reproductive health preventive and treatment services; and
* 6. gender-affirming services.

After providing an overview of the reproductive health services, the report

* describes the services that federal payment programs will cover when provided to enrolled beneficiaries;
* answers questions about federal requirements for private health insurance coverage of reproductive health services;
* describes whether and how federal programs that provide health services directly to a set of beneficiaries deliver or pay for the six types of reproductive health services; and
* provides short summaries of various federal programs that administer grants to entities to provide specific types of reproductive health services.

---

Introduction

Reproductive health services are preventive, diagnostic, and treatment services related to reproductive systems, functions, and processes. Federal support for these services is administered in different ways because federal agencies, departments, and programs have different purposes and requirements under federal law.

This report first defines six different types of reproductive health services that may receive federal support, noting restrictions where relevant. The six types of reproductive health services discussed in this report are

* 1. contraception and contraception counseling;
* 2. abortion and abortion counseling;
* 3. infertility-related services;
* 4. maternity services;
* 5. reproductive health preventive services, and treatment; and
* 6. gender-affirming services.[1](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn1 "Gender- a ffirming s ervices are medical and surgical interventions designed to help align an individuals' primary and secondary sex characteristics with their gender identity. Services include, but are not limited to , hormone therapy and surgical procedures. For more information, see \" What Are Gender-Affirming Services? \" in this report.")

The report next describes the federal government's role in paying for services provided to beneficiaries enrolled in federal health insurance programs, requiring payment for services by certain private health insurance plans, and providing domestic reproductive health services through federal agencies and programs.[2](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn2 "The enrollment estimates provided for federal programs covered in this report are not additive, as individuals can have multiple types of health coverage. In addition, the methodological approaches used to determine the estimates may limit comparisons between different coverage types.") The report then discusses grant programs that focus on one or more specific reproductive health topics (e.g., breast cancer screening) and grant programs that have a broader focus but may provide or pay for some types of reproductive health services. The report concludes with two appendixes: [**Appendix A**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref65840896) identifies acronyms used in this report; [**Appendix B**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref65841102) lists CRS experts on the various reproductive health topics discussed in this report.

Throughout this report, CRS has taken the primary approach of using terms in the same manner as the terms are used in the statute, rules, regulations, and guidance of specific agencies and grant programs.[3](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn3 "For example, gendered terms such as \"woman,\" \"man,\" \"female,\" \"male,\" \"pregnant women,\" \" individuals who are pregnant\" in each section are consistent with terminology used by each federal agency or grant program.")

General Questions

What Are Reproductive Health Services?

Reproductive health services are preventive, diagnostic, and treatment services related to reproductive systems, functions, and processes. These services include, but are not exclusive to, those related to family planning; abortion; maternal health; sexually transmitted infections (STIs)/sexually transmitted diseases (STDs);[4](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn4 "The federal programs described in this report use the terms sexually transmitted infections (STIs) and sexually transmitted diseases (STDs) interchangeably. As a result, this report presents either term as it is used in the program being discussed, without suggesting a distinction between the terms. The clinical difference between the two is that an STI is a \"virus, bacteria, fungus, or parasite people can get through sexual contact.\" An STD develops following an infection with the manifestati...") screening and treatment for diseases, including cancers of the reproductive organs and breast tissues; and gender-affirming services.

Family planning services, which are a subset of reproductive health services, include health-promoting preventive, diagnostic, and treatment services that help individuals and/or families decide on whether or when to become pregnant. Such services may include using contraceptives, infertility treatments, preconception care, pregnancy counseling, and counseling on healthy sexual behaviors.[5](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn5 "Kathryn M. Curtis et al., Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), \"U.S. Selected Practice Recommendations for Contraceptive Use, 2024,\" Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report Recommendations and Reports , vol. 73, no. 3 (August 8, 2024). Adoption is not discussed in this report because, although it is included as a family planning service, it is not an explicit health service.")

What Are Contraceptive and Contraceptive Counseling Services?

Contraceptive services include contraceptive counseling services and use of contraceptive products or methods to prevent or delay pregnancy. *Contraceptive counseling* includes a health care provider's assessment of a client in determining which methods are safe, effective, and available for the client; counseling of the client in determining whether a contraceptive method will meet the client's need and, if so, which method to choose; education of the client on how to use the method consistently and correctly; the provision of the contraceptive product or method; and follow-up care.[6](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn6 "Kathryn M. Curtis et al., Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), \"U.S. Selected Practice Recommendations for Contraceptive Use, 2024,\" Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report Recommendations and Reports , vol. 73, no. 3 (August 8, 2024).") Some contraceptive methods are available without contraceptive counseling or prescription by a health care provider.

A *contraceptive* is a product or method intended to lower the possibility of becoming pregnant.[7](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn7 "Some types of contraceptives may also reduce risk of contracting certain STIs. STIs are discussed in the \" What Are Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services? \" section of this report.") Contraceptive products vary in type and include drugs (e.g., oral contraceptives) and medical devices (e.g., internal condom). Some contraceptive products have both a drug and device component (e.g., contraceptive patch). Prior to commercial availability in the United States, contraceptive products are reviewed by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA), an operating division of the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS).

For contraceptive drugs, FDA approves those products that demonstrate substantial evidencethat the drug is safe and effective for the purpose stated in the new drug application.[8](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn8 "For more information, see CRS Report R41983, How FDA Approves Drugs and Regulates Their Safety and Effectiveness , and CRS In Focus IF11083, Medical Product Regulation: Drugs, Biologics, and Devices .") For high-risk (class III) contraceptive devices, FDA *approves* those products that demonstrate *reasonable assurance* of safety and effectiveness.[9](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn9 "CRS Report R42130, FDA Regulation of Medical Devices . Examples of contraceptive devices that are class III (high risk) include some intrauterine devices (IUDs), tubal occlusion devices (such as Essure, which was discontinued by Bayer in 2018), and the female condom. For IUD regulation, see 21 C.F.R. §884.5360; for tubal occlusion device regulation, see 21 C.F.R. §884.5380.") For moderate-risk (class II) contraceptive devices, FDA *clears* those products that demonstrate substantial equivalence to a device already on the market (a predicate device).[10](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn10 "CRS Report R42130, FDA Regulation of Medical Devices . Examples of contraceptive devices that are class II (moderate risk) include the diaphragm and the male condom. For female condom regulation, see 21 C.F.R. §884.5330; for diaphragm regulation, see 21 C.F.R. §884.5350; and for condom regulation, see 21 C.F.R. §884.5300.") Contraceptive methods also include fertility awareness-based methods. These methods monitor symptoms and biological markers to determine periods during the menstrual cycle when the possibility of pregnancy is lowest.[11](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn11 "CDC, \"Contraception and Birth Control Methods,\" August 6, 2024, https://www.cdc.gov/contraception/about/ . Fertility awareness tracking can also be used to indicate when during someone's menstrual cycle they are most likely to become pregnant.") FDA has cleared mobile medical applications (apps) for users to track biological markers, such as body temperature, to prevent pregnancy. Fertility-awareness tracking can also be tracked manually, although the FDA has not cleared this method.

Federal funding or payment for contraceptives is generally limited to products that are FDA-approved, cleared, or authorized for marketing and certain medical or surgical procedures.

FDA has approved or cleared a variety of contraceptive methods.[12](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn12 "Note that FDA approves, clears, and grants marketing authorization for individual contraceptive products , not methods . For purposes of this report, which includes birth control options broader than products, such as sterilization, the term methods is used.") Adapted from FDA's *Birth Control Guide*, [**Table 1**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref173940442) lists those methods from those most effective at preventing pregnancy to those least effective, and provides a description for each method.[13](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn13 "FDA, Birth Control Guide , May 2024, https://www.fda.gov/media/150299/download . Effectiveness estimates are the number of women per 100 who become pregnant over the course of one year using the same method given typical use of that method .") For each of the first five methods listed, according to FDA, less than one pregnancy per 100 women per year would be expected, in contrast to the last method listed (spermicide alone), up to 28 pregnancies per 100 women per year would be expected under typical use.[14](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn14 "Ibid.") Long-acting reversible contraceptives (LARCs), which include intrauterine devices (IUDs) and implants, are highly effective in preventing pregnancy and can last for several years. FDA has approved emergency contraceptives (EC), which may be used if the regular form of birth control fails (e.g., condom breakages). FDA states that although EC is effective at preventing pregnancy if it is taken soon after unprotected sex or birth control failure, EC "should not be used as a regular form of birth control."[15](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn15 "FDA, \"Birth Control,\" May 10, 2024, https://www.fda.gov/consumers/womens-health-topics/birth-control .") FDA-approved contraceptive methods do not cause abortion.[16](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn16 "Abortifacient drugs are those for which the main or side effect is a medical abortion. Emergency contraception (EC), for instance, prevents pregnancy by delaying ovulation and will not affect an existing pregnancy. For an example of how EC works, see, for example, FDA, \" Plan B One-Step (1.5 mg levonorgestrel) Information ,\" December 23, 2022, https://www.fda.gov/drugs/postmarket-drug-safety-information-patients-and-providers/plan-b-one-step-15-mg-levonorgestrel-information . Plan B One-Step i...") These contraceptive products, including EC, are not effective if the patient is already pregnant (*pregnancy* "encompasses the period of time from implantation until delivery").[17](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn17 "45 C.F.R. §46.202(f).")

Some contraceptives need to be prescribed by a health care provider, in the case of some oral contraceptives. Other contraceptives additionally require a health care provider to physically place them, such as contraceptive implants and IUDs. Contraceptives including condoms, ECs, and a recently approved oral contraceptive are available without a prescription at pharmacies, online vendors, or other retailers. In July 2023, FDA approved the first over-the-counter (OTC) birth control pill, Opill (norgestrel).[18](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn18 "FDA, \"FDA Approves First Nonprescription Daily Oral Contraceptive,\" press release, July 13, 2023, https://www.fda.gov/  news-events/  press-announcements/  fda-approves-first-nonprescription-daily-oral-contraceptive .") In order for a product to be approved for OTC use, FDA requires the manufacturer to demonstrate the product can be used safely and effectively in a nonprescription setting and without supervision from a health care professional.[19](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn19 "For more on OTCs, see CRS Report R46985, FDA Regulation of Over-the-Counter (OTC) Drugs: Overview and Issues for Congress") In a decisional memorandum, FDA found "the potential benefits of an increase in the ability for consumers to prevent unintended pregnancy (with its attendant medical, economic, and societal harms) outweigh the potential risks of the product in the nonprescription setting" and ultimately approved Opill to be available OTC.[20](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn20 "FDA, \"Decisional Memorandum, New Drug Application 17031 Supplement 41 Application for Full Prescription-to-Nonprescription Switch of Norgestrel Tablets 0.075 mg,\" decisional memo, July 13, 2023, https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/nda/2023/017031Orig1s041SumR.pdf .")

Sterilization surgeries, including tubal ligation and vasectomy procedures, are the most effective form of contraception and are considered permanent.[21](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn21 "FDA, \"Birth Control,\" May 10, 2024, https://www.fda.gov/consumers/womens-health-topics/birth-control .") Though not listed in FDA's *Birth Control Guide*, other surgeries may be used as a primary form of contraception. These procedures include hysterectomy (removal of uterus)[22](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn22 "U.S. National Library of Medicine, \"Hysterectomy,\" January 26, 2021, https://medlineplus.gov/hysterectomy.html .") and bilateral salpingectomy (removal of fallopian tubes), often with bilateral oophorectomy (removal of both ovaries).[23](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn23 "Harvard Health Publishing, \"Will removing your fallopian tubes reduce your risk of ovarian cancer?,\" October 13, 2020, https://www.harvardhealth.org/womens-health/will-removing-your-fallopian-tubes-reduce-your-risk-of-ovarian-cancer . Salpingectomy and oophorectomy may also be used to prevent or treat certain reproductive cancers.") These surgeries are also commonly used to treat medical conditions, such as reproductive cancers.

Table 1. Contraceptive Methods: Effectiveness and Descriptions

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Method | Number of Pregnancies Expected  (per 100 women over one year) | Description |
| Sterilization surgery for women | Less than 1 | Tubal ligation (cutting or tying of fallopian tubes); sealing of fallopian tubes with clips, clamps, rings, or with an instrument that uses electric current. |
| Sterilization surgery for men | Less than 1 | Vasectomy; blocking of vas deferens (tubes that carry seminal fluid). |
| Intrauterine device (IUD) copper | Less than 1 | T-shaped copper device inserted into the uterus; prevents sperm from reaching the egg and may prevent implantation. Can be used for a maximum of 10 years. |
| IUD with progestin | Less than 1 | T-shaped device containing the hormone progestin inserted into the uterus; prevents sperm from reaching the egg and thins the lining of the uterus. Can last eight years or more depending on the type. |
| Implantable rod | Less than 1 | Small progestin-containing rod placed under the skin of the upper arm; stops ovaries from releasing eggs; thickens cervical mucus (preventing sperm from reaching the egg). Can be used for up to three years. |
| Shot/Injection | 4 | Intramuscular or subcutaneous injection of the hormone progestin; one shot is needed every three months. |
| Oral contraceptive (combined pill) | 7 | Daily pill containing estrogen and progestin hormones; prevents ovaries from releasing eggs; thickens cervical mucus (preventing sperm from reaching the egg). Taken for three weeks with a week break in between. |
| Oral contraceptive (progestin only) | 7 | Daily pill containing progestin hormones; thickens cervical mucus (preventing sperm from reaching the egg); some types may prevent ovaries from releasing eggs, but these types are less common. Some types are taken continuously, while others are taken for three weeks with a week break in between. |
| Patch | 7 | Skin patch containing estrogen and progestin hormones that is worn on the upper arm, upper back, lower abdomen, or buttocks; prevents ovaries from releasing eggs; thickens cervical mucus (preventing sperm from reaching the egg). Each new patch is worn for three weeks at a time, with a week break in between. |
| Vaginal contraceptive ring | 7 | Flexible ring worn intravaginally that releases progestin and estrogen hormones; prevents ovaries from releasing eggs; thickens cervical mucus (preventing sperm from reaching the egg). Each new ring is worn for three weeks at a time, with a week break in between. |
| Software application for contraception | 7-8 | Medical software application (app) that can be used as a method of contraception to prevent pregnancy by predicting fertile days using information entered by the user such as daily basal body temperature and menstrual cycle information; recommends when to refrain from unprotected sex or to use contraception. |
| Male condom | 13 | Thin film sheath placed over the penis; over-the-counter barrier method that prevents sperm from reaching the egg. |
| Diaphragm with spermicide | 17 | Dome-shaped flexible disk worn intravaginally to cover the cervix, with spermicide foam, cream, or jelly inside of it; barrier method that prevents sperm from reaching the egg; spermicide kills sperm cells. Worn for a maximum of 24 hours. |
| Sponge with spermicide | 17 | Disk-shaped sponge-like device worn intravaginally, with spermicide foam, cream, or jelly inside of it; barrier method that prevents sperm from reaching the egg; spermicide kills sperm cells. Worn for a maximum of 30 hours. |
| Cervical cap with spermicide | 22-23 | Latex or silicon cup that covers the cervix, with spermicide foam, cream, or jelly inside of it; barrier method that prevents sperm from reaching the egg; spermicide kills sperm cells. Worn for a maximum of 48 hours. |
| Internal (female) condom | 21 | Thin lubricated pouch placed inside the vagina; over-the-counter barrier method that prevents sperm from reaching the egg. |
| Spermicide alone | 21-28 | Sperm cell killing foam, cream, jelly, film, or tablet placed intravaginally; over-the-counter product. |
| Emergency Contraceptives (EC) | Pregnancies Prevented | Description |
| EC (Levonorgestrel 1.5mg [one pill] or  Levonorgestrel 0.75mg [two pills]) | 7 out of every 8 women who would have gotten pregnant will not become pregnant after taking this EC. | Progestin hormone pill(s); should be taken within 72 hours of birth control failure or unprotected sex; primarily works to stop or delay ovaries from releasing eggs.  Levonorgestrel 1.5mg (one pill) is available over-the-counter for patients without age restrictions (e.g., Plan B One Step, Next Choice One Dose)  Levonorgestrel 0.75mg (two pills) is available over-the-counter for patients 17 years old or older, and by prescription for patients under age 17. |
| EC (Ulipristal Acetate) | 6 or 7 out of every 10 women who would have gotten pregnant will not become pregnant after taking this EC. | Pill that blocks progesterone hormone; should be taken within 120 hours of birth control failure or unprotected sex; works primarily by stopping or delaying ovaries from releasing eggs; may also work by changing the lining of the uterus that may affect implantation; available by prescription (e.g., Ella). |

**Sources:** FDA, "Birth Control," May 10, 2024, <https://www.fda.gov/consumers/womens-health-topics/birth-control>. FDA, "Plan B One-Step (1.5 mg levonorgestrel) Information," December 23, 2022, <https://www.fda.gov/drugs/postmarket-drug-safety-information-patients-and-providers/plan-b-one-step-15-mg-levonorgestrel-information>. FDA, "ella (ulipristal acetate) tablet," full prescribing information, August, 2010, <https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/label/2010/022474s000lbl.pdf>.

**Notes:** Table language reflects that of the FDA Birth Control resources and chart. It is organized from most to least effective contraceptive if used continuously (sterilization is most effective; spermicide used alone is least effective). Number of pregnancies prevented per 100 people using that method over the course of a year is not available for EC because EC is not meant for routine or continuous use like other methods included in this table.

What Are Abortions and Abortion Counseling Services?

An abortion, which is used to terminate a pregnancy, may be medically induced or surgically performed. A medically induced abortion (also called a medical abortion) is a nonsurgical intervention that FDA has approved for pregnant patients within the first 10 weeks of a pregnancy.[24](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn24 "FDA, \"Information about Mifepristone for Medical Termination of Pregnancy Through Ten Weeks Gestation,\" January 17, 2025, https://www.fda.gov/drugs/postmarket-drug-safety-information-patients-and-providers/information-about-mifepristone-medical-termination-pregnancy-through-ten-weeks-gestation . Katherine Kortsmit et al., \"Abortion Surveillance—United States, 2021,\" Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report , vol. 72, no. SS-9 (November 24, 2023), pp. 1-29.") To terminate a pregnancy with medication, the drugs mifepristone and misoprostol are prescribed in combination.[25](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn25 "FDA, \"Information about Mifepristone for Medical Termination of Pregnancy Through Ten Weeks Gestation,\" January 17, 2025, https://www.fda.gov/drugs/postmarket-drug-safety-information-patients-and-providers/information-about-mifepristone-medical-termination-pregnancy-through-ten-weeks-gestation .") Mifepristone is a progesterone hormone blocker and is the only FDA-approved drug for the termination of pregnancy.[26](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn26 "Mifepristone is the generic form of Mifeprex, the first form of mifepristone approved by the FDA. FDA, \"Questions and Answers on Mifeprex,\" September 1, 2023 , https://www.fda.gov/drugs/postmarket-drug-safety-information-patients-and-providers/questions-and-answers-mifeprex .") Misoprostol is used off-label to induce uterine contractions, though its approved use is to prevent stomach ulcers.[27](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn27 "American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG), Practice Bulletin Number 225, Medication Abortion Up to 70 Days of Gestation, October 2020, https://www.acog.org/clinical/clinical-guidance/practice-bulletin/articles/2020/10/medication-abortion-up-to-70-days-of-gestation .") Using misoprostol alone can be an alternative if mifepristone is unavailable, although it is less effective than the combination mifepristone-misoprostol method.[28](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn28 "Ibid .") Using medication as an abortion method can include a follow-up appointment with a health care provider to confirm termination of the pregnancy.

Mifepristone was first approved by the FDA in 2000 under specified restrictions on its use. [29](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn29 "FDA, Center for Drug Evaluation and Research (CDER), Approval Letter MIFEPREX , NDA 20-687, September 28, 2000, https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/appletter/2000/20687appltr.htm . These requirements included providing the medication by or under the supervision of a physician who met certain qualifications, obtaining a patient's and provider's signatures on a Patient Agreement form, reporting any hospitalizations and any other serious events to the drug's sponsor, and recordin...") In 2007, the Food and Drug Administration Amendments Act ([P.L. 110-85](https://www.congress.gov/law/110/pub/85)) expanded FDA's authority to require Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS) for certain drugs under specified conditions.[30](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn30 "See CRS Report R44810, FDA Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS): Description and Effect on Generic Drug Development .") Since 2011, mifepristone has been subject to restricted distribution pursuant to the drug's REMS program. FDA has changed the REMS requirements for mifepristone over time. Among these changes, in 2021, FDA reviewed the mifepristone REMS program and determined that certain elements of the program would be updated. Subsequently, the mifepristone REMS program was changed to remove an in-person dispensing requirement and add a new pharmacy certification process, which enabled retail pharmacies that meet certain qualifications to dispense mifepristone directly to patients who have a prescription from a certified prescriber.[31](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn31 "FDA, \"Information about Mifepristone for Medical Termination of Pregnancy Through Ten Weeks Gestation,\" January 17, 2025, https://www.fda.gov/drugs/postmarket-drug-safety-information-patients-and-providers/information-about-mifepristone-medical-termination-pregnancy-through-ten-weeks-gestation .") These changes allowed mifepristone to be available via telehealth prescribing for the first time. Changes to the REMs have been subject to litigation.[32](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn32 "See CRS Legal Sidebar LSB10919, FDA Regulation of Medication Abortion: Recent Legal Developments .")

Surgical abortion procedures vary depending on gestational age. These procedures can include aspiration of the uterus, dilation of the cervix, and evacuation procedures.[33](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn33 "Stephanie Ramer et al., \"Abortion Surveillance — United States, 2022,\" Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report Surveillance Series , vol. 73, no. 7 (November 28, 2024), pp. 1-31.")

Abortion counseling, sometimes called *all-options pregnancy counseling*, is, in general, a discussion between a clinician and a patient about abortion as a potential option in pregnancy decisionmaking in addition to discussions on parenting and adoption.[34](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn34 "American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists, Pregnancy Choices: Raising the Baby, Adoption, and Abortion , FAQ, April 2024, https://www.acog.org/womens-health/faqs/pregnancy-choices-raising-the-baby-adoption-and-abortion .") Clinical guidelines on all-options counselling recommend providers offer nondirective counseling and give patients neutral, factual information.[35](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn35 "Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6 (December 2024).") For patients interested in an abortion, counseling may also include providing information about their available options based on gestational age and legal restrictions that vary from state to state.[36](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn36 "CRS Report R47595, State Laws Restricting or Prohibiting Abortion .")

The U.S. Supreme Court's opinion in *Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization* concluded that the U.S. Constitution does not confer a right to an abortion.[37](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn37 "CRS Legal Sidebar LSB10768, Supreme Court Rules No Constitutional Right to Abortion in Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization .") In light of this decision and existing state laws, the availability of abortion services vary by state. Abortion services may be affected by state laws limiting abortion based on gestational age, requiring certain information to be shared prior to obtaining an abortion, or other conditions. For a survey of state laws restricting or prohibiting abortion, see CRS Report R47595, [*State Laws Restricting or Prohibiting Abortion*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/R47595). Additionally, litigation related to abortion access under federal and state law remains ongoing.[38](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn38 "See KFF, State and Federal Reproductive Rights and Abortion Litigation Tracker , August 26, 2026, https://www.kff.org/womens-health-policy/litigation-involving-reproductive-health-and-rights-in-the-federal-courts/ .")

Can Federal Funds Be Used to Pay for Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

Federal funds are available under limited circumstances to pay for abortion. Specifically, under federal law, certain federal funds may only be used to pay for abortions in cases of rape, incest, or endangerment of a pregnant woman's life. This restriction is the result of statutory and legislative provisions such as the Hyde Amendment (see **text box**), a funding restriction which has been added to the annual appropriations measure for the Departments of Labor, HHS, and Education, and Related Agencies (LHHS) since 1976.[39](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn39 "See P.L. 94-439 , §209, 90 Stat. 1418, 1434 (1976).") LHHS appropriations for FY2026 include the restriction that no funds appropriated in the act shall be used for an abortion, except if the pregnancy was the result of rape or incest or the woman suffers from a physical disorder, injury, or illness arising from the pregnancy that a physician certifies places her in danger of death unless an abortion is performed.[40](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn40 "See Division B, Title II, Sections 506 and 507 of P.L. 119-75") Similar provisions exist in the appropriations measures for foreign operations, the District of Columbia, the Treasury, and the Department of Justice (DOJ).[41](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn41 "For additional discussion of abortion funding restrictions, see CRS Report RL33467, Abortion: Judicial History and Legislative Response , and CRS In Focus IF12167, The Hyde Amendment: An Overview .") Other codified restrictions limit the use of funds made available to the Department of Defense (DOD), the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA), and the Indian Health Service (IHS), and the use of federal subsidies for health insurance plans offered on the health insurance exchanges.[42](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn42 "See, for example, 10 U.S.C. §1093(a) . \"Funds available to the Department of Defense may not be used to perform abortions except where the life of the mother would be endangered if the fetus were carried to term or in a case in which the pregnancy is the result of an act of rape or incest.\" Regarding the federal subsidies for plans on the exchanges, see \" Can Federal Funds Be Used to Pay for Abortion in Private Health Insurance Plans? \"")

These provisions and agency guidance may additionally specify conditions under which abortion counseling may or may not be offered by federal agencies and grant programs.[43](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn43 "See, for example, 42 C.F.R. §59.5 . (\"Each project supported under [Part 59] must: ... not provide abortion as a method of family planning. A project must: offer pregnant clients the opportunity to be provided information and counseling regarding each of the following options: prenatal care and delivery; infant care, foster care, or adoption, and pregnancy termination.\")")

|  |
| --- |
| Hyde Amendment  Following the Supreme Court's *Roe v. Wade* decision, some of the first federal legislative responses involved restrictions on the use of federal funds to pay for abortions. In 1976, Representative Henry J. Hyde offered an amendment to the Departments of Labor and Health, Education, and Welfare Appropriation Act, 1977, that restricted the use of funds appropriated in the act to pay for abortions. In 1980, the U.S. Supreme Court upheld the validity of the Hyde Amendment, concluding that the funding restriction was constitutional. The original provision stated, "None of the funds contained in this Act shall be used to perform abortions except where the life of the mother would be endangered if the fetus were carried to term."    **Sources:** [P.L. 94-439](https://www.congress.gov/law/94/pub/439), §209, 90 Stat. 1418, 1434 (1976).  **Note:** For additional discussion of abortion funding restrictions, see CRS Report RL33467, [*Abortion: Judicial History and Legislative Response*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/RL33467). |

What Are Infertility Services?

Infertility is a reproductive health disorder generally defined as the inability to become pregnant after a certain amount of time attempting to conceive.[44](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn44 "Infertility is generally defined as not being able to become pregnant after one year of unprotected sex. For women 35 years and older, they may be evaluated for infertility following a period of six month. CDC, \"Infertility: Frequently Asked Questions,' May 15, 2024, https://www.cdc.gov/reproductive-health/infertility-faq/ .") Infertility affects people of all genders and can be caused by reproductive organ damage, hormone imbalance, genetic disorders, or certain medical treatments.[45](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn45 "Ibid.") Infertility caused by medical treatments is called *iatrogenic infertility*, such as chemotherapy treatment for cancer.[46](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn46 "American Society for Reproductive Medicine, \"Fertility preservation in patients with medical indications: a committee opinion,\" Fertility and Sterility , vol. 125, no. 2 (February 2026), pp. 247-259.")

Treatments for infertility thus may involve surgery, hormone/medication therapy, genetic counseling, or medical procedures such as intrauterine insemination (IUI).[47](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn47 "CDC, \"Infertility: Frequently Asked Questions,' May 15, 2024, https://www.cdc.gov/reproductive-health/infertility-faq/ . Intrauterine insemination (IUI) , sometimes called artificial insemination , is an infertility treatment . In this procedure, specially prepared sperm are transferred into the uterus.") Treatment may also involve Assisted Reproductive Technologies (ARTs), which are generally defined as all fertility treatments in which either eggs or embryos are handled outside the body.[48](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn48 "See CRS In Focus IF13144, Assisted Reproductive Technology Regulation and Oversight . IVF is t ypically administered in \"cycles.\" In a single cycle, one egg or many eggs are retrieved from an ovary and externally fertilized. The embryo or embryos are transferred into the uterus and monitored for development. More than one cycle may be necessary to achieve pregnancy.") In vitro fertilization (IVF), the most commonly used ART, is a set of procedures designed to help initiate a pregnancy via transfer of an embryo into a uterus.[49](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn49 "American Society for Reproductive Medicine, Assisted Reproductive Technology: A Guide for Patients , 2018, https://www.reproductivefacts.org/globalassets/_rf/news-and-publications/bookletsfact-sheets/english-pdf/art-booklet.pdf .") Other ARTs include gamete and zygote intrafallopian transfer.[50](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn50 "CDC, \"20 21 Assisted Reproductive Technology: Fertility and National Summary Report,\" 20 23 , https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/154438 . Gamete and zygote intrafallopian transfers are procedures in which gametes (eggs and sperm) or zygotes (fertilized egg before it begins to divide) are transferred into the fallopian tubes rather than the uterus.")

IVF consists of several steps and can be referred to as an *ART cycle*. The basic procedures of an ART cycle typically include the following:

* **Ovarian Stimulation****.** A female patient is prescribed medications that stimulate her ovaries to prepare to produce multiple oocytes (eggs).
* **Oocyte Retrieval****.** The resulting oocytes are removed from the ovaries before ovulation occurs during a surgical procedure at a fertility clinic. These retrieved oocytes might then be frozen for future use.
* **Fertilization of the Oocytes****.** The retrieved oocytes are either thawed and combined with sperm or, shortly after retrieval, combined with sperm in an embryology laboratory with the purpose of fertilizing the oocytes to develop into an embryo.
* **Embryo Culture****.** The fertilized oocytes are cultured in the laboratory to develop into embryos. Embryos that develop appropriately are then prepared for transfer or frozen for later use.
* **Embryo Transfer****.** An embryo, either fresh or frozen, can then be transferred into the uterine cavity. This may result in a pregnancy if the embryo successfully attaches to the uterine lining.[51](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn51 "CDC, \"20 21 Assisted Reproductive Technology: Fertility and National Summary Report,\" 20 23 , https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/154438 .")

ART cycles can involve other procedures or variations of the procedures briefly summarized above, depending on factors like an infertility diagnosis or whether donated material is being used. Multiple treatment cycles may be needed to achieve a successful pregnancy.

The provision of ART services involves multiple entities, which the federal government regulates in various capacities. These include fertility clinics, embryology and clinical laboratories, and health care professionals providing ART services. These entities are subject to federal oversight and regulations specific to the ART services they provide. For information, see CRS In Focus IF13144, [*Assisted Reproductive Technology Regulation and Oversight*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/IF13144).

What Are Maternity Services?

Maternity services, also referred to in this report as maternal health services, encompass a range of preventive, diagnostic, and treatment services to monitor the health of the pregnant individual and the fetus. These services include interventions provided during pregnancy (i.e., prenatal or antepartum care), services provided during labor and delivery (i.e., intrapartum care), and services provided after birth (i.e., postpartum or postnatal care).[52](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn52 "Although not formally part of maternity care, preconception care , defined as a woman's health before she becomes pregnant, is considered the first step in planning a healthy pregnancy. The goal of preconception care (also referred to as pre-pregnancy care ), is to identify health conditions, lifestyle factors, and other risk factors that may affect the health of a future pregnancy. For more information, see Office of the Assistant Secretary for Health (OASH): Office on Women's Health, Prec...") Other maternity services may include support provided by entities such as doulas[53](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn53 "A doula is a nonmedical , trained professional who \"provides continuous physical, emotional and informational support to their client before, during and shortly after childbirth.\" See DONA International, \"What is a Doula,\" https://dona.org/what-is-a-doula/ .") or lactation specialists, as well as other care coordination and educational services (e.g., childbirth preparation classes). Specific services and the timing and frequency of visits can vary based on the needs of the pregnant individual and the fetus, as well as the maternity care setting.

*Prenatal care* services monitor the pregnant individual's physical and psychological health as well as the overall health of the fetus. Prenatal services typically include the routine monitoring of vital signs such as maternal blood pressure and temperature, as well as physical and laboratory assessments to screen, diagnose, and manage maternal or fetal risks, conditions, disorders, or infections.[54](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn54 "Sharon Murray et al., \"Antepartum Assessment, Care, and Education,\" in Foundations of Maternal-Newborn and Women's Health Nursing , 8 th ed. (St. Louis, MI: Elsevier, 2023), pp. 123-139. Health Resources and Services Administration (HRSA), \"Women's Preventive Services Guidelines,\" December 2025, https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines .") Patient education is provided on a range of topics, including nutrition and the use of prenatal vitamins (e.g., folic acid supplementation[55](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn55 "U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) , \" Folic Acid Supplementation to Prevent Neural Tube Defects: Preventive Medication ,\" August 1, 2023, https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/  uspstf/  recommendation/  folic-  acid-  for-  the-  prevention-  of-  neural-  tube-  defects-  preventive-  medication . See text box in the section \" What Are Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services? \" below for more information about the U SPSTF.")), counseling against the use of harmful substances, and referrals to relevant medical or social services.[56](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn56 "Russell S. Kirby and Sarah Verbiest, \"The Reproductive and Perinatal Health of Women, Pregnant Persons, and Infants,\" in Kotch's Maternal and Child Health: Problems, Programs, and Policy in Public Health , 4 th ed. (Burlington, MA: Jones & Bartlett, LLC, 2022), pp. 185-187.") Ultrasonographic technology (i.e., ultrasound) is typically used to confirm pregnancy and assess the overall health and growth of the fetus.[57](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn57 "Sharon Murray et al., \"Prenatal Diagnosis and Fetal Assessment During the Antepartum Period,\" in Foundations of Maternal-Newborn and Women's Health Nursing , 8 th ed. (St. Louis, MI: Elsevier, 2023), pp. 177-180.")

*Intrapartum care* includes the range of services provided from the onset of labor through the delivery of the newborn.[58](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn58 "For the purposes of this report, postnatal services provided to the infant following delivery are not considered part of the broader definition of maternity services .") These services include the monitoring of various maternal and fetal vital signs, the management of pain and other conditions that may arise during labor, and services specific to the method of delivery (e.g., vaginal vs. Cesarean section birth).[59](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn59 "The American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG), \" Labor & Delivery ,\" https://www.acog.org/  womens-  health/  pregnancy/  labor-  and-  delivery .")

*Postpartum care* broadly includes services provided immediately after delivery, as well as those provided anywhere from six weeks to one year after birth. Postpartum services may include mental health screenings (e.g., those that identify perinatal depression), physical health exams, social assessments, counseling and/or initiation of contraception, and the management of other chronic medical conditions that may have been initiated or exacerbated by pregnancy.[60](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn60 "HRSA, \"Women's Preventive Services Guidelines,\" December 2025, https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines . ACOG, \" Optimizing Postpartum Care : Committee Opinion No. 736,\" 2018, https://www.acog.org/clinical/clinical-guidance/committee-opinion/articles/2018/05/optimizing-postpartum-care (reaffirmed 2025).")

What Are Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

Prevention services in a reproductive health context seek to prevent, detect, or treat infections, diseases, and other disorders or conditions involving the reproductive system. Common reproductive infections include STIs[61](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn61 "CDC, \" About Sexually Transmitted Infections ,\" March 25, 2024 , https://www.cdc.gov/sti/about/ . \"Sexually transmitted diseases (STDs), also known as sexually transmitted infections or STIs, are very common. Millions of new infections occur every year in the United States.\"") such as chlamydia, gonorrhea, human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)/acquired immune deficiency syndrome (AIDS), and human papillomavirus (HPV).[62](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn62 "CDC, \"About HIV,\" January 14, 2025, https://www.cdc.gov/hiv/about/index.html . CDC, \" About Genital HPV Infection ,\" January 31, 2025, https://www.cdc.gov/sti/about/about-genital-hpv-infection.html . Human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) and human papillomavirus (HPV) infections can cause other diseases and certain cancers.") Other reproductive disorders include malignant cancers of the reproductive tract and breast, benign cysts and tumors, and infertility.[63](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn63 "CDC, \"Common Reproductive Health Concerns for Women,\" May 15, 2024 , https://www.cdc.gov/reproductive-health/women-health/common-concerns.html , and CDC, \"Prostate Cancer Basics ,\" February 11, 2025, https://www.cdc.gov/prostate-cancer/about/index.html  . For more information on infertility services, see the \" What Are Infertility Services? \" section of this report.")

In public health and in the provision of health care, prevention occurs along a continuum, depending on the outcomes to be prevented. For example, vaccination can prevent infectious disease, chemotherapy can prevent the progression of cancer, and hospice care can prevent pain and distress. Health programs and payers may categorize these services differently, including as treatment rather than prevention.

Health care services can be generally described as either prevention or treatment, as follows:

* **Preventive services** as health interventions can encompass a large range of activities with the goal of preventing the development or progression of infection, disease, or other adverse health outcome.[64](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn64 "See CRS Infographic IG10083, Public Health Prevention and the Determinants of Health , and CRS In Focus IF13100, The United States Preventive Services Task Force .") Prevention interventions can be applied at a community level, such as controlling lead in public drinking water, or directly to individuals, such as immunizations and screenings for disease. Preventive *screenings* aim to identify those without symptoms of a disease but who are at increased risk of developing a disease based on age, sex, health behaviors, or other risk factors. Preventive screenings also aim to identify those who are in the early stages of disease but are asymptomatic For example, cervical cytology, also known as a Pap test, is a screening service used to detect precancerous cervical cells that may develop into cervical cancer if not treated.[65](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn65 "CDC, \"Screening for Cervical Cancer,\" February 26, 2025, https://www.cdc.gov/cervical-cancer/screening/index.html .") Prevention services can also include *counseling*, which can include an assessment of a patient by a health care provider and subsequent patient education to avoid or reduce health behaviors that could result in an infection, disease, or other undesirable condition. For example, behavioral counseling for consistent condom use in sexually active adults can lower a person's risk for STIs.[66](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn66 "U.S. Preventive Services Task Force, \"Behavioral Counseling Interventions to Prevent Sexually Transmitted Infections,\" JAMA , vol. 324, no. 7 (August 18, 2020), pp. 674-681.") *Preventive medications* are another intervention tool, such as contraceptives to prevent unintended pregnancies and improve birth outcomes.[67](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn67 "HRSA, \" Women's Preventive Services Guidelines ,\" December 2025, https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines . Also s ee the section \" What Are Contraceptive and Contraceptive Counseling Services? \" in this report.")
* **Treatment services** are surgical and medical (including pharmaceutical) interventions to control or cure a disease, manage its symptoms, or both. Treatment services are provided to patients who have symptoms or diagnostic findings of an actual illness or condition. *Monitoring*, the use of diagnostic services to track the course or remission of a disease, is considered a form of treatment, thus it is not discussed separately in this report. Some reproductive health treatment services may address symptoms related to reproductive health conditions, such as managing symptoms for women experiencing menopause.[68](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn68 "CDC, \"Menopause, Women's Health, and Work,\" November 14, 2024, https://www.cdc.gov/womens-health/features/menopause-womens-health-and-work.html .")

|  |
| --- |
| Clinical Preventive Services Recommendations and Guidelines  Certain sets of clinical preventive service recommendations, including the "A" and "B" recommendations from the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) and the women's preventive services guidelines supported by the HHS Health Resources and Services Administration (HRSA), are referenced in federal law to define certain requirements for coverage of or payment for clinical preventive services. These recommendations also serve as guidance to clinicians in providing some of the reproductive health care services described in this report.  **U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) Recommendations**  The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force is an independent, volunteer panel of experts in prevention, evidence-based medicine, and primary care that makes recommendations for health care providers about clinical preventive services such as screenings, counseling services, and preventive medications. Depending on available evidence, recommendations are tailored to specific populations, such as age groups.  USPSTF recommendations apply only to people without recognized signs or symptoms of the disease or health condition, focus on screening to identify disease early and interventions to prevent the onset of disease, and address services offered in the primary care setting or services to which patients can be referred by primary care professionals.  In making its recommendations, USPSTF grades the preventive service based on the certainty of evidence and magnitude of the preventive service's net benefit. USPSTF gives "A" and "B" ratings to those services that it most highly recommends implementing for preventive care. These preventive services have a high or moderate net benefit for a given population (e.g., individuals of a certain demographic or risk group) based on available evidence.  **HRSA Women's Preventive Services Guidelines**  HRSA supports guidelines on preventive services that address the health needs of adolescent and adult women with strong evidence of their health benefits. To develop these guidelines, HRSA has funded cooperative agreements to the Women's Preventive Services Initiative (WPSI), which is a coalition of health professional organizations representing women's health care clinicians and patient advocates with expertise in women's health. Under cooperative agreements, WPSI has developed, reviewed, and updated the *Women's Preventive Services Guidelines* (Guidelines), which are based on a report initially published in 2011 by the National Academies of Science, Engineering, and Medicine (formerly known as the Institutes of Medicine) to provide evidence-based recommendations on preventive health services for women.  Beginning in 2016, HRSA funded a cooperative agreement with WPSI to review and update existing guidelines and develop new ones. Ultimately, HRSA has determined whether to support and approve the guidelines issued by WPSI in whole or in part. The most recent WPSI cooperative agreement ended in February 2026. In November 2026, HRSA announced a new funding opportunity for the Women's Initiative for Screening and Health (WISH) program to continue reviewing and updating the Guidelines. The Guidelines as supported by HRSA still have statutory requirements for federal health programs as described in this report.  The Guidelines aim to serve as a basis of recommendations to improve women's health across the lifespan, and to complement, build upon, and fill gaps in existing guidelines provided by the USPSTF. The Guidelines also serve as the basis for which preventive services should be covered by certain insurers without cost-sharing, notably contraceptive services and supplies.  **Sources:** CRS In Focus IF13100, [*The United States Preventive Services Task Force*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/IF13100). HRSA, "Women's Preventive Services Guidelines," December 2025, <https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines>. Institutes of Medicine, *Clinical Preventive Services for Women: Closing the Gaps*, Washington, DC, 2011. HHS, "HHS Opens $1 Million Funding Opportunity to Strengthen Women's Preventive Care," press release, November 6, 2026, <https://www.hhs.gov/press-room/hhs-opens-1-million-womens-preventive-care-funding-opportunity.html>. |

A given reproductive health service may be either a preventive service, a treatment service, or both. For example, mammography may be a preventive service when used to screen for breast cancer in asymptomatic patients with no history of the disease, or a non-preventive service when used to diagnose someone with relevant symptoms or to monitor a breast cancer patient's treatment progress or remission. Considering the definitions above, health care services may be considered preventive or treatment services based on their use. These services may be used as a diagnostic service for someone with symptoms or increased risk for a disease. Often, the use or purpose of a service determines how it is financed by the health programs described in this report. [**Table 2**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref65596914) lists examples of diseases or conditions and their respective prevention and treatment services and their uses.

Table 2. Examples of Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services

|  |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Disease or Condition | Prevention | | Treatment | |
|  | Prevention | Screening/Post-Exposure Prophylaxis (PEP) | Diagnosis and/or Monitoring | Medical/Surgical Treatment |
| Breast cancer[a](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref509391978) | None known, although some healthy behaviors may lower incidence | Mammography and other exams,[a](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref509391978) genetic counseling and testing | Mammography and other exams | Mastectomy/lumpectomy, chemotherapy, immunotherapy, radiation |
| Cervical cancer[b](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref509391979) | Human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine | Visual exam, cervical cytology (Pap smear), HPV testing | Visual exam, cervical cytology (Pap smear) | Surgery, chemotherapy, radiation |
| Human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) | Pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP),[c](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref509391980) counseling regarding safe sexual practices, bloodborne pathogens protections[d](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref509391981) | HIV testing, Post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP)[e](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref509391982) | Viral load testing, other bloodwork, retesting following exposure | Combination drug therapy, management of HIV-associated conditions |
| Gonorrhea[f](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref509391983) | Counseling regarding safe sexual practices | Testing following possible exposure or if at risk, PEP | Repeat testing, especially for antibiotic-resistant strains | Antibiotic therapy |

PrEP may be an option to help protect pregnant people and their babies from getting HIV while trying to get pregnant, during pregnancy, or while breastfeeding." There are currently three medications with FDA approval for use as PrEP: Truvada, Descovy, and Apretude.

What Are Gender-Affirming Services?

Gender-affirming services, also known as *gender-affirming care*, are medical, surgical, mental health, and nonmedical interventions designed to help align an individuals' physical traits with their gender identity.[69](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn69 "E. Coleman et al., \"Standards of Care for the Health of Transgender and Gender Diverse People, Version 8,\" International Journal of Transgender Health , vol. 23 (2022), https://www.wpath.org/publications/soc .") Although gender-affirming services do not always involve reproductive health care services, they are covered by this report because services can involve care affecting reproductive organ systems, and these health services are of legislative interest to Congress.

For the purposes of this report, the terms *gender-affirming services* or *gender-affirming care* refer to gender transition-related services unless specified in relevant statute, rules, regulations, and guidance.

|  |
| --- |
| Terminology Relevant to Gender-Affirming Care  This report uses the following terms and definitions relevant to the provision of gender-affirming care, unless otherwise defined by the federal health programs included in this report. The terms included here are non-exhaustive, and the usage and acceptability of these terms may change over time.  *Gender* can refer to gender identity, gender expression, or social gender roles, including social understandings and expectations culturally tied to people based on the sex they were assigned at birth.  *Gender affirmation* refers to the process of recognizing or affirming people in their gender identity. Gender affirmation is not only something experienced by transgender and gender diverse (TGD) individuals but also by individuals whose sex assigned at birth aligns with their gender identity.  *G**ender diverse* (GD) describes people with gender identities or gender expressions that differ from societal and cultural expectations of someone based on their sex assigned at birth.  *Gender dysphoria* is a feeling of significant discontent with someone's own biological sex and/or birth gender.  *Gender**identity* refers to an individual's deeply felt, internal, intrinsic sense of their own gender.  *Sex assigned at birth* refers to a person's sex usually being assigned or determined at birth based on the appearance of external genitalia.  *Transgender or trans* is an umbrella term used to describe people whose gender identities or gender expressions are not what is typically expected for the sex to which they were assigned at birth. |

Use of gender-affirming services may stem from a diagnosis of gender dysphoria. Not all individuals who experience feelings of distress related to their gender or who have a diagnosis stemming from that distress seek or receive gender-affirming services.[70](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn70 "Ibid.") Additionally, not all transgender and gender diverse (TGD) individuals experience gender dysphoria or feelings of distress. Health services that are gender-affirming are not synonymous with gender transition-related care. TGD individuals may still seek out or receive gender-affirming care. Gender-affirming care can be highly individualized and TGD people may vary in the gender-affirming and transition-related services they do or do not seek out with consultation from their health care provider(s).

Gender-affirming care services can include primary care, mental health services, hormone therapy, and surgical and postoperative care. Gender-affirming primary care is primary care with specific attention to the needs of TGD individuals and can involve appropriate preventive services such as cancer screenings, mental health screenings, and ongoing hormone therapy support depending on the provider's scope of care and knowledge. Clinical guidance for the care and treatment of TGD individuals in the primary care setting recommends that the general health of TGD individuals should be attended to within the primary care setting, without differentiation from services offered to people who are not TGD for physical and mental health issues.[71](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn71 "Ibid . , p. S143.") Depending on the scope of the health provider and their knowledge of providing gender-affirming care, transition-related care—such as hormone therapy—is also possible in primary care.[72](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn72 "Kevan Wylie et al., \"Serving transgender people: clinical care considerations and service delivery models in transgender health,\" The Lancet , vol. 388, no. 10042 (2016), pp. 401-411.") Although some care for TGD individuals can take place in primary care setting, gender-affirming care can require the coordination of multiple health care specialists. TGD individuals seeking care may be referred to multidisciplinary gender clinics where providers of varying specialties can coordinate an individual's gender-affirming care treatment and services.[73](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn73 "Rebecca M. Warwick and Daniel E. Shumer, \"Gender-affirming multidisciplinary care for transgender and non-binary children and adolescents,\" Children's Health Care , vol. 52, no. 1 (2021), pp. 91-155.")

Being transgender or gender diverse is not in itself a mental health disorder. As mentioned earlier in this section, TGD individuals may experience gender dysphoria or feelings of prolonged distress related to feelings of gender incongruence. Gender-affirming mental health care can involve the treatments and management of prolonged feelings of distress and other mental health issues.[74](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn74 "Therapy aimed at attempting to change a person's gender identity is not recognized as valid clinical treatment of gender dysphoria by health professional organizations, and the American Psychological Association recommends against the use of gender identity change efforts and that such efforts put TGD individuals at significant risk of harm. See American Psychological Association, \"APA Resolution on Gender Identity Change Efforts,\" February 2021, https://www.apa.org/about/policy/resolution-ge...") Gender-affirming surgical interventions and postoperative care can involve altering physical features to align with an individual's gender identity. Surgeries include, but are not limited to, those that alter the face, the chest/breasts, or genitals.

The availability of gender-affirming treatments may vary and depend on a health care provider's training and knowledge in providing gender-affirming treatments, state scope of practice laws, professional guidance, and laws specifying access to such care. Roughly half of states have enacted laws restricting the provision of certain gender-affirming care services, including to minors, for the treatment of gender dysphoria.[75](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn75 "Lindsey Dawson and Jennifer Kates, Policy Tracker: Youth Access to Gender Affirming Care and State Policy Restrictions , KFF, August 14, 2026, https://www.kff.org/lgbtq/gender-affirming-care-policy-tracker/ .") These restrictions generally limit or prohibit the provision of medical interventions (such as puberty-blockers and hormone therapy, and surgical gender-affirming care services to minors).

The Trump Administration has taken actions to limit federal support for gender-affirming care to minors. This has included two executive orders issued in January and February 2025 and subsequent agency guidelines addressing gender identity and gender-affirming care for minors. The January 2025 executive order established the Administration's policy to "recognize two sexes, male and female [which] are not changeable and are grounded in fundamental and incontrovertible reality."[76](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn76 "Executive Order 14168, \" Defending Women from Gender Ideology Extremism and Restoring Biological Truth to the Federal Government, \" 90 Federal Register 8615-8618, January 20, 2025.") The executive order goes on to define the term "sex" as an immutable biological classification as either male or female. A subsequent executive order issued in February 2025 directly addressed gender-affirming care for minors, stating the federal government will "not fund, sponsor, promote, assist, or support the so-called 'transition' of a child from one sex to another, and it will rigorously enforce all laws that prohibit or limit these destructive and life-altering procedures."[77](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn77 "Executive Order 14187, \" Protecting Children From Chemical and Surgical Mutilation, \" 90 Federal Register 8771-8773, February 3, 2025.") The executive order further directs the HHS Secretary to issue regulations to "take all appropriate actions to end the chemical and surgical mutilation of children, including regulatory and subregulatory actions." This report provides information on federal policies related to the provision and payment for gender-affirming care services, including changes that align with the Administration's policies to limit the provision of these services to minors. For an example, see the section "[Does Medicaid Cover Gender-Affirming Services?](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref239255396)"

Medicaid

Medicaid, authorized in Title XIX of the Social Security Act (SSA), is a federal-state program that jointly finances primary and acute medical services, as well as long-term services and supports (LTSS) to a diverse low-income population, including eligible children, pregnant women, adults, individuals with disabilities, and people aged 65 and older.[78](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn78 "For more information about the Medicaid program, see CRS Report R43357, Medicaid: An Overview .") Participation in Medicaid is voluntary for states; all states, the District of Columbia, and five U.S. territories choose to participate.

Medicaid is jointly financed by states and the federal government. At the federal level, Medicaid is administered by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) within HHS. States must follow federal rules to receive federal matching funds, but states have the flexibility to design their own versions of Medicaid within the federal statute's framework. This flexibility results in variability across state Medicaid programs in terms of eligibility and covered benefits, among other criteria. In FY2025, Medicaid provided health care services to an estimated 82 million individuals.[79](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn79 "This enrollment figure is measured according to average monthly enrollment and represents the number of beneficiaries with full and partial benefits who are enrolled on an average monthly basis. This enrollment measure differs from person-year equivalents, which represent the average program enrollment over the course of a year and from ever-enrolled counts, which measure the number of people covered by Medicaid for any period of time during the year. Congressional Budget Office (CBO) Bas...") Federal and state expenditures totaled approximately $949 billion in FY2024.[80](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn80 "CMS, Form CMS-64 data as of April 14, 2025 , at https://www.medicaid.gov/medicaid/financial-management/state-budget-expenditure-reporting-for-medicaid-and-chip/expenditure-reports-mbes/cbes .")

Medicaid provides a health care safety net for low-income populations, playing a more significant role for certain subpopulations.[81](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn81 "The health care safety net consists of those organizations and programs, in both the public and private sectors, with a legal obligation or a commitment to provide direct health care services to uninsured and underinsured populations.") For example, in 2024 approximately 21% of the U.S. population received Medicaid coverage.[82](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn82 "U.S. Census Bureau, American Community Survey Tables for Health Insurance Coverage, Table HI-05 _ACS , Health Insurance Coverage Status and Type of Coverage by State and Age for All Pe rsons : 20 24 , at https://www.census.gov/data/tables/time-series/demo/health-insurance/acs-hi.html .") In that same year, Medicaid provided health coverage for approximately 60% of all nonelderly individuals with incomes below 100% of the federal poverty level (FPL).[83](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn83 "KFF , Health Insurance Coverage of the Nonelderly (0-64) with Incomes below 100% Federal Poverty Level (FPL), as of 20 24 , State Health Facts, accessed June 123 , 202 6 , at https://www.kff.org/state-health-policy-data/state-indicator/health-insurance-coverage-population-0-64-up-to-100-fpl/?currentTimeframe=0&sortModel=%7B%22colId%22:%22Location%22,%22sort%22:%22asc%22%7D .") For some types of services (including reproductive health services), Medicaid is a significant payer. For instance, Medicaid paid for approximately 40% of all births in the United States in 2024.[84](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn84 "Joyce A. Martin et al., Births In the United States, 2024 , NCHS Data Brief, No. 535, July 2025, at https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/174613 .") According to the most recent data available, Medicaid provided 75% of all public expenditures on family planning services in FY2015.[85](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn85 "Michelle J . K . Osterman et al. , Births: Final Data for 2024, National Vital Statistics Reports , vol 75, no 2, Hyattsville, MD: National Center for Health Statistics, June 9, 2026, at https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/252440.")

|  |
| --- |
| The State Children's Health Insurance Program (CHIP)  CHIP is a federal-state program that provides health coverage to certain uninsured, low-income children and pregnant individuals in families that have annual income above Medicaid eligibility thresholds but do not have health insurance. Like Medicaid, CHIP is jointly financed by the federal government and the states and is administered by the states. In FY2025, CHIP covered health care services for an estimated 7 million individuals. Federal and state expenditures totaled an estimated $28 billion, with the federal government paying approximately $20 billion of that total.  Participation in CHIP is voluntary, and all states, the District of Columbia (DC), and five territories participate. As with Medicaid, the federal government sets basic requirements for CHIP, but states have the flexibility to design their own versions of CHIP within the federal government's basic framework. As a result, there is significant variation across CHIP programs.  **CHIP Program Design**  States may design their CHIP programs in one of three ways: a CHIP Medicaid expansion, a separate CHIP program, or a combination approach in which the state operates a CHIP Medicaid expansion and one or more separate CHIP programs concurrently. CHIP benefit coverage depends on program design. CHIP Medicaid expansions must follow the federal Medicaid rules for benefits. For separate CHIP programs, benefits are permitted to look more like private health insurance.  Under separate CHIP programs, child health assistance is defined at 42 C.F.R. §457.402 and includes services such as physician and surgical services, prenatal care, and pre-pregnancy family planning services and supplies. The law requires separate CHIP programs to cover certain services, including emergency services, well baby and well-child care (including age-appropriate immunizations), and dental services. If offered, mental health services must meet federal mental health parity requirements. As with Medicaid, federal funds may not be used for abortion services, except in the case of a pregnancy resulting from rape or incest, or when necessary to save the mother's life (SSA §2105(c)(7)).  According to a 2017 study that looked at the types of reproductive health services covered under separate CHIP plans, states generally provide routine gynecologic exams and obstetric care, STI/STD screening and treatment, age-appropriate sexuality education, family planning, pregnancy testing, and pregnancy care, among other services.  **CHIP Coverage of Pregnant Individuals**  Under separate CHIP programs, states may extend CHIP coverage to uninsured low-income pregnant individuals through various authorities: (1) the CHIP state plan option for pregnant individuals, (2) the Section 1115 waiver authority, and/or (3) the from conception to birth pathway (formerly known as the unborn child pathway). Under the state plan option, states are permitted to cover pregnant individuals through a state plan amendment when certain conditions are met. The period of coverage associated with the state plan option includes pregnancy through the postpartum period (through 60 days postpartum), and benefits include all services available to CHIP children in the state as well as prenatal, delivery, and postpartum care. States are permitted to provide different benefits to pregnant individuals than CHIP children.  Under CHIP-funded pregnancy-related Section 1115 demonstration waivers, with CMS approval, states define the eligibility criteria and benefit coverage (including duration of postpartum care), among other waiver features. States may target the benefit coverage to meet particular health care needs (e.g., treatment for pregnant women with substance use disorders).  States also are permitted to provide CHIP coverage to pregnant individuals (including individuals aged 19 and older) by extending coverage to children from conception to birth as permitted through federal regulation at 42 C.F.F.R. §457.10. Coverage available to such individuals may be limited to prenatal and delivery services but still is used in a number of states because it permits the extension of CHIP coverage to a pregnant individual regardless of that person's immigration status.  The American Rescue Plan Act of 2021 (ARPA; [P.L. 117-2](https://www.congress.gov/law/117/pub/2) ) permits states to offer 12 months of continuous postpartum coverage, regardless of changes in circumstances (with exceptions), under Medicaid and CHIP. States that elect this option under Medicaid must elect the parallel state plan option under CHIP. Coverage includes CHIP child health assistance available under the CHIP state plan for targeted low-income children or targeted low-income pregnant individuals through the 12-month postpartum period. However, states are permitted to provide more generous postpartum coverage.  **Sources:** CHIP average monthly enrollment for 50 states and DC from Congressional Budget Office (CBO), "CHIP Baseline Projections," February 2026, <https://www.cbo.gov/system/files/2026-02/51296-2026-02-chip.pdf>.
