However, in 2025, approximately 7.1 million beneficiaries under age 65 were enrolled in Medicare due to disability, including an unspecified number of people of reproductive age.[185](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn185 "Boards of Trustees, Federal Hospital Insurance and Federal Supplementary Medical Insurance Trust Funds, 2026 Annual Report of the Boards of Trustees of the Federal Hospital Insurance and Federal Supplementary Medical Insurance Trust Funds , June 9, 2026, at https://www.cms.gov/data-research/statistics-trends-and-reports/trustees-report-trust-funds .")

Does Medicare Cover Reproductive Health Services?

Medicare covers some types of reproductive health services. Cost sharing—a deductible and co-insurance—applies to some, such as many physician services, and is waived for others, such as most preventive services. Covered services, described further below, include prenatal and maternity care, and preventive services. Other services, such as contraception, abortion, infertility services, and gender-affirming services, may be covered in specified circumstances.

Many reproductive health services are recommended for Medicare beneficiaries who are older than reproductive age, including breast and gynecological exams for women, and STI screening and treatment for men and women. As a result, any type of reproductive health service may be sought or advised for at least some Medicare beneficiaries.

Does Medicare Cover Contraceptive Services?

There is no explicit statutory requirement for Medicare to cover contraceptive services or supplies for the purpose of pregnancy prevention. However, certain contraceptive services or supplies may be covered to treat a medical condition. For example, under Medicare Part B, IUDs may be covered to treat endometrial hyperplasia, a medical condition of the uterus.[186](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn186 "Additional coverage criteria apply. Noridian Healthcare Solutions, LLC, Billing and Coding: IUD (Hormone-Eluting) for Endometrial Hyperplasia - CPT 58999 , A55061, September 25, 2025, https://www.cms.gov/medicare-coverage-database/view/article.aspx?articleid=55061&ver=11&keyword=IUD&keywordType=starts&areaId=all&docType=NCA,CAL,NCD,MEDCAC,TA,MCD,6,3,5,1,F,P&contractOption=all&sortBy=relevance&bc=1 .") Male or female sterilization (e.g., vasectomy, tubal ligation) is covered only where it is a necessary part of the treatment of an illness or injury. For example, removal of reproductive organs may be required to treat cancers of those organs. Sterilization is not covered as an elective procedure or for the sole purpose of preventing pregnancy or any associated effects of a pregnancy.[187](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn187 "CMS, Medicare National Coverage Determination for Sterilization (230.3), https://www.cms.gov/medicare-coverage-database/view/ncd.aspx?ncdid=13 .")

Women Medicare beneficiaries may have access to contraceptive services in the form of hormonal contraception under their Medicare Part D prescription drug coverage. Hormonal contraception, such as IUDs, oral contraceptives, contraceptive patches, and implants, are considered covered Part D drugs.[188](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn188 "Covered Part D drugs are defined in SSA Section 1860D–2(e)(1) as (1) outpatient prescription drugs approved by the FDA and used for a medically accepted indication; (2) biological products that may be dispensed only upon a prescription and that are licensed under the Public Health Service (PHS) Act and produced at a licensed establishment; (3) insulin (including medical supplies associated with the injection of insulin); and (4) vaccines licensed under the P ublic Health Service Act. Contra...") There is no statutory requirement that Part D plans cover contraception, but Part D plan sponsors are generally required to cover at least two drugs in every class and category under their chosen drug classification system. Part D plan sponsors may choose to use any drug classification system, subject to CMS approval.[189](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn189 "CMS, Medicare Part D Manual , Ch. 6 – Part D Drugs and Formulary Requirements , Section 30.2-Provision of an Adequate Formulary , https://www.cms.gov/medicare/prescription-drug-coverage/prescriptiondrugcovcontra/downloads/part-d-benefits-manual-chapter-6.pdf .") The minimum number and type of contraceptives that a Part D plan is required to cover may vary based on their chosen classification system.[190](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn190 "Plans that choose to use the United States Pharmacopeial Convention Medicare Model Guidelines (USP MMG) are automatically approved by CMS. While there are drug classes such as \" Antidepressants \" and \" Anti-addiction/Substance Abuse Treatment Agents ,\" there is no USP MMG drug class for contraceptive agents. Hormonal contraception is instead in the drug class \" Hormonal Agents, Stimulant/Replacement/Modifying (Sex Hormones/Modifiers) \" and under the category of the same name. In contrast...")

Medicare Advantage plans are explicitly prohibited from offering contraceptives, such as spermicides and prophylactics, as part of an over-the-counter supplemental benefit.[191](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn191 "CMS, Medicare Managed Care Manual, Chapter 4, Benefits and Beneficiary Protections , April 22, 2016, p. 52, https://www.cms.gov/regulations-and-guidance/guidance/manuals/downloads/mc86c04.pdf .")

For individuals who are dually eligible for Medicare and Medicaid, Medicare is the primary payer. Medicaid pays for any additional services that it covers, and Medicare does not, after Medicare denies payment. For example, many contraceptive products and services for those dually eligible may be paid through the more generous Medicaid benefits.[192](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn192 "Henry J. Kaiser Family Foundation, \" Coverage of Sexual and Reproductive Health Services in Medicare ,\" April 30, 2024 , https://www.kff.org/medicare/issue-brief/coverage-of-sexual-and-reproductive-health-services-in-medicare/  .")

Does Medicare Cover Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

Abortions are not covered Medicare procedures except (1) if the pregnancy is the result of an act of rape or incest or (2) in the case where a woman suffers from a physical disorder, physical injury, or physical illness, including a life-endangering physical condition caused by or arising from the pregnancy itself, that would, as certified by a physician, place the woman in danger of death unless an abortion is performed.[193](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn193 "CMS, Medicare National Coverage Determination for Abortion (140.1), June 19, 2006, https://www.cms.gov/medicare-coverage-database/view/ncd.aspx?ncdid=127&ncdver=2&bc=0 .") Consistent with typical Medicare-covered physician services, a Medicare-covered abortion could include care activities such as taking a patient's medical and situational history, determining how the coverage criteria may apply, and discussing what specific procedures are under consideration, the potential complications, and follow-up.

Does Medicare Cover Infertility Services?

The Medicare Benefit Policy Manual states that "reasonable and necessary services associated with treatment for infertility are covered under Medicare. Infertility is a condition sufficiently at variance with the usual state of health to make it appropriate for a person who normally is expected to be fertile to seek medical consultation and treatment."[194](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn194 "CMS, Medicare Benefit Policy Manual , Ch. 15 – Covered Medical and Other Health Services, May 8, 2026 , p. 9 , https://www.cms.gov/Regulations-  and-  Guidance/Guidance/Manuals/Internet-  Only-  Manuals-  IOMs-  Items/CMS012673 .") The infertility services covered are not specified, and the term "reasonable and necessary" is not defined in this context. The lack of clarity around what constitutes "reasonable and necessary" services can result in different interpretations by Medicare administrators. Under Medicare Part D, therapies used to promote fertility are excluded from coverage, such as drugs used during IVF treatment.[195](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn195 "Section 1860D-2 ( e )(2)( A ) of the SSA . CMS annually publishes a list of excluded drugs in their Excluded Drug Reference File.") A Part D sponsor may elect to include one or more of these drugs in an enhanced Part D plan; however, no federal subsidy is available for the associated costs.

Does Medicare Cover Maternity Services?

The Medicare Benefit Policy Manual states that Medicare covers the "events of pregnancy" from diagnosis through prenatal care, delivery, and necessary postnatal care of the mother.[196](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn196 "Ibid., p. 8 .") Coverage applies whether the pregnancy ends in live birth, miscarriage, or therapeutic abortion (i.e., where the life of the mother would be endangered if the fetus were brought to term). Of note, covered services do not apply to care for a child; rather, they are limited to care of the mother, who is the Medicare beneficiary.

Does Medicare Cover Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

Medicare Part B covers a number of preventive services that involve reproductive health. These include, among others, annual wellness visits, breast cancer screening, screening pelvic exams, Pap smears, screening for HIV and other STIs, prostate cancer screening, and HIV pre-exposure PrEP.[197](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn197 "CMS, \" Medicare Preventive Services,\" interactive chart, June 2026 , https://www.cms.gov/Medicare/Prevention/PrevntionGenInfo/medicare-preventive-services/MPS-QuickReferenceChart-1.html .") Cost sharing is waived for most, but not all, of these preventive services.

In addition, Medicare Parts A or B typically cover diagnostic and treatment services furnished by a certified provider; cost sharing typically applies. Such reproductive health services include diagnosis and treatment of STIs and urinary tract infections, and management of precancerous and cancerous gynecological abnormalities.

Does Medicare Cover Gender-Affirming Services?

Medicare coverage of gender-affirming surgery is generally determined by Medicare Administrative Contractors (MACs) or MA plans, as is common for many Medicare-covered services. Prior to 2014, Medicare excluded coverage of affirmation-related medical care as "experimental."[198](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn198 "Eleesha Lockett, \"Gender Affirmation: Does Medicare Cover It?\" H ealthline , Updated July 22, 2024, https://www.healthline.com/health/medicare/does-  medicare-  cover-gender-  affirmation .") The HHS Departmental Appeals Board lifted that exclusion in 2014; as a result, MACs were able to determine coverage of gender-affirming surgery on a case-by-case basis.[199](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn199 "Department of Health and Human Services, Department Appeals Board, Appellate Division, NCD 140.3, Transexual Surgery , Docket No. A-13-87, Decision No. 2576, May 30, 2014, https://www.hhs.gov/  sites/  default/  files/  static/  dab/  decisions/  board-  decisions/  2014/  dab2576.pdf.") In 2016, CMS announced that it would not issue a national coverage determination (NCD) for gender-affirming surgery, instead allowing MACs and MA plans to continue to determine whether surgery is medically necessary on a case-by-case basis.[200](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn200 "CMS, Medicare Coverage Database, \"Decision Memo for Gender Dysphoria and Gender Reassignment Surgery (CAG-00446N),\" August 30, 2016, https://www.cms.gov/medicare-coverage-database/view/ncacal-decision-memo.aspx?proposed=N&ncaid=282 .")

Medicare coverage of nonsurgical gender-affirming services includes primary care and mental health services and counseling. Hormone therapy is also covered, although the specific drugs and beneficiary out-of-pocket costs vary by Part D plan or MA plan with Part D drugs.[201](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn201 "Michael T. Solotke et al., \"Medicare Prescription Drug Plan Coverage of Hormone Therapies Used by Transgender Individuals,\" LGBT Health , vol. 7, no. 3 (April 7, 2020), p. 139, https://www.liebertpub.com/doi/epdf/10.1089/lgbt.2019.0306 .") Certain services that may be considered gender-affirming are not covered under Medicare if they are primarily cosmetic.[202](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn202 "Social Security Act Section 1862(a)(10) excludes cosmetic surgery from coverage under Medicare.")

Federal Regulation of Private Health Insurance

Private health insurance is the predominant source of health insurance coverage in the United States. Approximately 192.6 million individuals, or 56.5% of the U.S. population at the time, had private health insurance as of December 2024.[203](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn203 "CRS analysis of National Supplemental Health Care Exhibit (SHCE) and California Department of Managed Health Care (DMHC) data, accessed July 2026. SHCE data accessed from Mark Farrah Associates, \"Health Coverage Portal\" (CRS subscription), https://www.markfarrah.com/. DMHC data accessed from California Health Care Foundation, \"California Health Insurers, Enrollment Almanac – December 2025 Edition,\" December 16, 2025, https://www.chcf.org/resource/california-health-insurers-enrollment-almanac/...")

Broadly, private health insurance includes *group plans* (largely made up of employer-sponsored insurance) and *nongroup plans* (i.e., plans that a consumer purchases directly from an insurer). Group plans may be *fully insured* or *self-insured*,[204](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn204 "When group plan sponsor s (e.g., employers or unions) purchase coverage from insurers and offer it to their employees or other groups, these plans are referred to as fully insured . Employers or other plan sponsors that self-insure set aside funds to pay for health benefits directly, and they bear the risk of covering medical expenses generated by the individuals covered under the self-insured plan.") and fully insured plans may be purchased in the *large-group* or *small-group markets*.[205](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn205 "The (fully insured) group market is divided into segments based on size: the small-group market and the large-group market. In general, for purposes of health insurance requirements, small groups are those with 50 or fewer individuals (e.g., employees). However, states can define small groups as having 100 or fewer individuals. The definition of large group is 51 or more individuals, or 101 or more individuals, depending on the definition of small group. Groups of any size may self-i...") The nongroup and small-group markets include plans both on and off the *health insurance exchanges.*[206](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn206 "The individual exchanges and small business health options program (SHOP) exchanges are virtual marketplaces in which consumers and small businesses, respectively, can shop for and purchase private health insurance coverage. Plans sold in the individual and SHOP exchanges must meet all the requirements applicable to the nongroup and small-group markets, respectively. Additional requirements apply only to exchange plans. See CRS Report R44065, Health Insurance Exchanges and Qualified Hea...")

Federal requirements on private health insurance plans generally apply to some or all of the plan types noted above (i.e., nongroup plans, fully insured small- and large-group plans, and self-insured group plans).[207](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn207 "In this section, references to \"plans\" include applicable group health plans and insurers. For more information about federal and state regulation of different types of plans, see the \"Background\" section of CRS Report R45146, Federal Requirements on Private Health Insurance Plans .") For the federal reproductive health coverage requirements discussed in this section, applicability to each coverage type is noted. In general, states also have authority to regulate all but self-insured group plans.[208](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn208 "States play a central role in the regulation of private health insurance, and they may enact their own requirements on nongroup and/or fully insured group plans.") While the focus of this report is federal requirements, some examples of relevant state requirements on private health insurance plans are also discussed in this section.

Some plans within a market segment may be exempt from requirements that otherwise apply to plans in that market segment. For example, *grandfathered plans* are nongroup or group plans in which at least one individual was enrolled as of enactment of the ACA, and which continue to meet certain criteria.[209](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn209 "The ACA was enacted on March 23, 2010. For more information about grandfathered plans, as well as certain other types of plans that do not have to comply with some or all federal health insurance requirements, see CRS Report R46003, Applicability of Federal Requirements to Selected Health Coverage Arrangements (last updated in 2019).") Plans that maintain their grandfathered status are exempt from some, but not all, federal requirements. For the reproductive health coverage requirements discussed in this section, applicability to grandfathered plans is noted.

Plans may voluntarily cover benefits, subject to applicable federal and state laws. This includes providing coverage that exceeds federal or state requirements, or providing coverage where there is no requirement to do so, as long as there is no prohibition on such coverage.

Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Reproductive Health Services?

Various federal laws address private health insurance coverage of different types of reproductive health services. For background on this term, see the "[What Are Reproductive Health Services?](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref61017354)" section of this report.

Two federal requirements—coverage of EHBs and coverage of certain preventive services without cost sharing—are applicable to multiple types of reproductive health services.[210](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn210 "The provisions described here have some direct relevance to coverage of reproductive health services, among other services. Other federal requirements on private health insurance may also be more generally related to coverage of reproductive health services. For example, the requirement to cover pre-existing health conditions could be relevant to an enrollee who has pre-existing reproductive health conditions. For more information about provisions not discussed in this report, see CRS Report...") Overviews of these provisions are provided below. These provisions are further discussed in the rest of this section as applicable to different types of reproductive health services, along with other federal requirements relevant to the different types of services.

Where there is a benefit coverage requirement, one or more details may be specified in statute or regulations. For example, coverage requirements may or may not specify any *cost-sharing* requirements or limitations. In general, private health insurance cost sharing includes deductibles, coinsurance, and copayments, up to annual out-of-pocket limits.[211](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn211 "In general, beginning with each plan year, an enrollee pays 100% of costs for covered health care benefits until the costs meet a certain threshold amount, called a deductible . Exceptions apply. After reaching the deductible, the enrollee pays coinsurance (a percentage amount) or co-payments (a flat amount) for covered benefits and the plan pays the rest. If an enrollee ' s spending meets an annual out-of-pocket limit , the plan generally will pay 100% of covered costs for the remainde...") Coverage requirements may depend on how or where the service or item is furnished (e.g., by an *in-network* versus *out-of-network* provider).[212](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn212 "Under private insurance, benefit coverage and enrollee cost sharing are often contingent upon whether the service or item is furnished by a provider that the insurer has contracted with (i.e., whether that provider is in network for a given plan). In instances where a contract between an insurer and provider does not exist, the provider is considered out of network . For more information, see CRS Report R47507, Private Health Insurance: A Primer , section on \" Provider Networks .\"")Coverage requirements may also specify whether plans are allowed to impose medical management requirements, such as prior authorization requirements.[213](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn213 "In general, medical management requirements include different types of standards or processes through which plans aim to ensure appropriate use of covered benefits and to control costs. For example, some plans require enrollees to obtain prior authorization from the plan, or approval of benefit coverage before receiving care, as a condition of covering specified benefits. See CRS Report R47507, Private Health Insurance: A Primer , section on \"Covered Benefits.\"") To the extent that a reproductive health benefit coverage requirement specifies one or more of these details, they are discussed below.

Overview: Coverage of the Essential Health Benefits (EHB)

The ACA requires certain plans to offer a core package of 10 categories of health care services, known as the *essential health benefits* (EHB).[214](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn214 "42 U.S.C. §300gg-6 and 42 U.S.C. §18022.") As implemented, states, rather than the federal government, generally specify the benefit coverage requirements within those categories. To date, EHB regulations allow each state to select an EHB "benchmark plan." The benchmark plan serves as a reference plan for applicable plans in that state, which must provide EHB coverage that is "substantially equal" to such coverage in the benchmark plan, as specified in regulations. Because states select their own EHB-benchmark plans, EHB coverage varies considerably from state to state.[215](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn215 "For more information on the process for defining the essential health benefits (EHB) in each state, as well as each state's benchmark plan, see CMS, CCIIO, \" Information on Essential Health Benefits (EHB) Benchmark Plans,\" https://www.cms.gov/cciio/resources/data-resources/ehb .")

EHB categories particularly relevant to reproductive health services include "maternity and newborn care" (further discussed in the maternity services question in this section) and "preventive and wellness services and chronic disease management" (which incorporates the preventive services provision discussed below). Other EHB categories may also include benefits relevant to reproductive health.[216](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn216 "The 10 categories of EHB are (1) ambulatory patient services; (2) emergency services; (3) hospitalization; (4) maternity and newborn care; (5) mental health and substance use disorder services, including behavioral health treatment; (6) prescription drugs; (7) rehabilitative and habilitative services and devices; (8) laboratory services; (9) preventive and wellness services and chronic disease management; and (10) pediatric services, including oral and vision care.")

Cost-sharing and medical management requirements are possible for most categories of EHB, subject to applicable federal and state requirements. Federal requirements limit overall cost sharing on the EHB.[217](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn217 "See CRS Report R45146, Federal Requirements on Private Health Insurance Plans , regarding several federal requirements relevant to consumer cost sharing on the EHB (e.g., maximum annual limitation on cost-sharing , minimum actuarial value requirements, and prohibition on lifetime and annual coverage limits).") Coverage and cost sharing for EHB services furnished by out-of-network providers may vary.

All nongrandfathered nongroup and small-group plans are required to cover the EHB.

Overview: Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Without Cost Sharing

The ACA, via Section 2713 of the PHSA, requires most plans to cover specified preventive services and items without cost sharing, "such as a copayment, coinsurance, or a deductible."[218](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn218 "42 U.S.C. §300gg-13; 45 C.F.R. §147.130. For more information, see CRS In Focus IF13010, The ACA Preventive Services Coverage Requirement .") Per statute and regulations, this includes at least the preventive services and items in the following four categories:

* Evidence-based items or services recommended with an "A" or "B" rating by the USPSTF.[219](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn219 "USPSTF , \"A & B Recommendations,\" https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/uspstf/recommendation-topics/uspstf-a-and-b-recommendations .")
* Immunizations with a recommendation in effect by the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP) for routine use for a given individual.[220](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn220 "Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP) , \"ACIP Vaccine Recommendations and Guidelines: Vaccine-Specific Recommendations,\" https://www.cdc.gov/acip-recs/hcp/vaccine-specific/ . Also see CDC, \"Healthcare Professionals: Immunization Schedules,\" https://www.cdc.gov/vaccines/hcp/imz-schedules/ . Per 45 C.F.R. §147.130 (a)(1)(ii) , an immunization recommendation is considered to be \"in effect\" if it is adopted by the CDC Director, and it is considered to be recommended \"...")
* Evidence-informed preventive care and screenings for infants, children, and adolescents as provided in guidelines supported by HRSA.[221](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn221 "HRSA , \"Bright Futures,\" https://mchb.hrsa.gov/programs-impact/bright-futures . See linked \"Periodicity Schedule.\"")
* Additional preventive care and screenings for women as provided in guidelines supported by HRSA. [222](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn222 "HRSA, \"Women's Preventive Services Guidelines,\" https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines . See the \"Clinical Preventive Service Recommendations and Guidelines\" text box earlier in this report for additional information about these guidelines.")

Examples of reproductive health preventive services and items in these four categories include (1) screening and counseling for STIs; (2) universal HIV screening; (3) breast cancer screening, genetic testing, and preventive medications such as Tamoxifen (to lower the risk of developing breast cancer among women with specified risk factors); (4) gynecological exams and Pap smears; (5) well-woman visits; (6) a variety of preventive prenatal care services; and (7) contraception.[223](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn223 "The preventive services that must be covered are listed in their entirety at Health C are.gov, \"Preventive health services,\" https://www.healthcare.gov/coverage/preventive-care-benefits/ .") These services are further discussed under the relevant questions in this section.

Plans are also required to cover, without cost sharing, items and services that are "integral to the furnishing of a recommended preventive service, regardless of whether the item or service is billed separately."[224](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn224 "Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury, \"FAQs about Affordable Care Act and Women's Health and Cancer Rights Act Implementation Part 68,\" October 21, 2024, https://www.cms.gov/files/document/faqs-implementation-part-68.pdf (hereinafter referred to as \"ACA FAQ 68, October 2024\"). See page 2, including footnote 8.") See the questions in this section on contraceptive coverage and reproductive health prevention and treatment services for examples of this.[225](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn225 "See \" Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Contraceptive Services? \" and \" Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services? \" in this report.") Different rules apply with regard to cost-sharing for office visit charges.[226](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn226 "45 C.F.R. §147.130(a)(2). In general, whether cost sharing for office visits is allowed or prohibited depends on whether the preventive service or item was the primary purpose of the visit, and whether the service or item was billed or tracked separately from the office visit.")

As specified in regulations and guidelines, plans can use "reasonable medical management techniques to determine the frequency, method, treatment, or setting for an item or service," to the extent not specified in the relevant recommendation or guideline.[227](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn227 "45 C.F.R. §147.130(a)(4).") The aforementioned two questions in this section also have examples of this.

By regulation, plans are generally not required to cover preventive services furnished out of network.[228](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn228 "45 C.F.R. §147.130(a)(3).") However, if the plan does not have an in-network provider who can furnish a recommended preventive service, the plan must provide coverage without cost-sharing when such service is furnished by an out-of-network provider.

If there are new or updated recommendations or guidelines in any of these categories (e.g., the USPSTF announces a new A or B rating), plans generally are required to provide relevant coverage as of plan years that begin one year after the change.[229](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn229 "45 C.F.R. §147.130(b).") If an existing recommendation or guideline is removed, plans must generally still provide coverage of the preventive service or item through the end of their plan year, unless, as specified, it is determined to pose a safety risk.[230](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn230 "Ibid.")

The requirement to cover specified preventive services without cost sharing is incorporated into the EHB category "preventive and wellness services and chronic disease management."[231](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn231 "45 C.F.R. §156.115(a)(4), referencing 45 C.F.R. §147.130.") These incorporated benefits are the only EHB that are federally required to be covered without cost sharing.

The preventive services coverage requirement does not apply *only* to plans subject to the EHB requirements; rather, it generally applies to nongrandfathered large-group, small-group, self-insured, and nongroup plans, unless a particular employer or insurer qualifies for an exemption set out in federal regulations.[232](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn232 "42 U.S.C. §300gg-13; 45 C.F.R. §147. 130 -133 . S ee \" Are There Exemptions for the Federal Contraceptive Coverage Requirement? \" below for further discussion of exemptions provided in regulations.")

Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Contraceptive Services?

One of the required categories of preventive services coverage without cost sharing is the services and items recommended per HRSA-supported guidelines on women's preventive health care services.[233](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn233 "42 U.S.C. §300gg-13(a)(4). See the \" Overview: Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Without Cost Sharing \" section of this report.") Since 2011, these Women's Preventive Services Guidelines (Guidelines) have included contraception.[234](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn234 "HRSA, \"Women's Preventive Services Guidelines Historical Files ,\" initially released August 1, 2011, https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines-historical-files .")

Preventive Services Requirement: Women's Preventive Services Guidelines on Contraception

The current Guidelines on contraception, updated in December 2021 and adopted by HRSA in January 2022, recommend that "adolescent and adult women have access to the full range of contraceptives and contraceptive care to prevent unintended pregnancies and improve birth outcomes," including screening, education, counseling, provision of contraceptives, and follow-up care, including management and removal.[235](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn235 "HRSA, \"Women's Preventive Services Guidelin es,\" page last reviewed December 2025, https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines . Also see HRSA, \"Update to the Women's Preventive Services Guidelines,\" 87 Federal Register 1763, January 12, 2022 , https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2022/01/12/2022-00465/update-to-the-womens-preventive-services-guidelines .") In addition, the current Guidelines on contraception recommend "that the full range of U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-approved, -granted, or -cleared contraceptives, effective family planning practices, and sterilization procedures be available as part of contraceptive care."[236](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn236 "Ibid.") This is specified to include 17 of the "methods" (i.e., categories) of contraception as listed in the FDA's Birth Control Guide as of December 2021—including, for example, two categories of IUDs, three categories of oral contraceptives, the contraceptive patch, contraceptive rings, condoms, two categories of emergency contraception, and sterilization surgery for women. The Guidelines also recommend access to "any additional contraceptives approved, granted, or cleared by the FDA," and further provide that "instruction in fertility awareness-based methods, including the lactation amenorrhea method, although less effective, should be provided for women desiring an alternative method."[237](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn237 "Ibid. Per the current Women's Preventive Services Guidelines on contraception, \"The full range of contraceptives currently includes those listed in the FDA's Birth Control Guide: (1) sterilization surgery for women, (2) implantable rods, (3) copper intrauterine devices, (4) intrauterine devices with progestin (all durations and doses), (5) injectable contraceptives, (6) oral contraceptives (combined pill), (7) oral contraceptives (progestin only), (8) oral contraceptives (extended or continuou...")

Although the FDA Birth Control Guide also includes male sterilization, this service is excluded from the Women's Preventive Services Guidelines on contraception because the HRSA Guidelines (and the related coverage requirement) are specific to women's preventive health. However, per agency guidance on the preventive services coverage requirements, the Guidelines on contraception for women include male condoms, in addition to female condoms.[238](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn238 "Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury, \"FAQs about Affordable Care Act Implementation Part 54,\" July 28, 2022, https://www.cms.gov/files/document/faqs-part-54.pdf (hereinafter referred to as \"ACA FAQ 54, July 2022\"). In this FAQ, see footnote 16 .")

Preventive Services Requirement: Contraceptive Coverage

Through rulemaking and guidance, the Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury have addressed the private health insurance contraceptive coverage requirements that are based on the Women's Preventive Services Guidelines. Applicable nonexempt[239](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn239 "See \" Are There Exemptions for the Federal Contraceptive Coverage Requirement? \" in this report.") plans must

(1) cover without cost sharing at least one form of contraception in each of the categories listed in the HRSA-supported Guidelines; and (2) cover without cost sharing any contraceptive services and FDA-approved, -cleared, or -granted products that an individual and their attending provider have determined to be medically appropriate for the individual, whether or not those services or products are specifically identified in the categories listed in the HRSA-supported Guidelines. The latter requirement extends to newer contraceptive products as they are approved, cleared, or granted by the FDA, whether or not such products are identified in the categories listed in the current HRSA-supported Guidelines.[240](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn240 "Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury, \"FAQs about Affordable Care Act Implementation Part 64,\" January 22, 2024, https://www.cms.gov/files/document/faqs-part-64.pdf (hereinafter referred to as \"ACA FAQ 64, January 2024\"). See page 4. This FAQ also summarizes prior rulemaking and guidance on contraceptive coverage.")

Subject to regulations and guidance, plans are allowed to impose "reasonable medical management" coverage limitations within each category of contraception, as long as they do not restrict access to a category altogether.However, plans' coverage limitations are only considered "reasonable" if they make coverage exceptions for medical necessity, as specified in the inset text above, and if they include "an easily accessible, transparent, and sufficiently expedient exceptions process that is not unduly burdensome."[241](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn241 "ACA FAQ 64, January 2024, page 4. Also see this FAQ for discussion of reasonable and \"potentially unreasonable\" medical management techniques with respect to contraceptive coverage. In addition, see questions 1-5 of this FAQ for a \"therapeutic equivalence\" approach to contraceptive coverage that plans may choose to adopt.")

As stated in the earlier overview of the preventive services coverage requirement, applicable plans must cover, without cost-sharing, services and items that are integral to the furnishing of a recommended preventive service. With respect to contraception, this includes, for example, pregnancy tests before the provision of contraception as well as anesthesia for tubal ligation.[242](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn242 "ACA FAQ 54, July 2022, question 1.")

Per additional guidance on contraceptive coverage, applicable plans are *required* to provide coverage without cost-sharing of emergency contraception that is available OTC, "when the product is prescribed for an individual by their attending provider," including for advance provision. Plans are also *encouraged* to cover without cost sharing OTC emergency contraception when purchased without a prescription.[243](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn243 "ACA FAQ 54, July 2022, question 5 . Also in this FAQ, per footnote 16, condoms (another type of OTC contraception) must be covered \"with a prescription.\"") Applicable plans are encouraged to cover a 12-month supply of contraceptives, such as oral contraceptives, if dispensed at once.[244](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn244 "ACA FAQ 54, July 2022, question 7.")

State Requirements and Additional Data

States play a central role in the regulation of private health insurance, and they may implement their own contraceptive coverage requirements on the plans they regulate.[245](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn245 "In general, states may regulate nongroup plans and fully insured group plans but not self-insured group plans. See the introduction to this section of this report.") According to analyses by two policy research and stakeholder groups, as of April 2026, at least 31 states and DC require private health insurance coverage of FDA-approved prescription contraceptives if the plan otherwise covers prescription drugs, and at least 19 of those 32 prohibit cost sharing on some or all of the required contraceptive coverage. Most of the 32 have one or more additional requirements, such as coverage of certain OTC contraceptives (although insurers may still require prescriptions), coverage of an extended supply of contraception when dispensed at once, or coverage of male sterilization. At least 20 of the 32 provide contraceptive coverage exemptions for certain employers and insurers.[246](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn246 "Guttmacher Institute, \"Insurance Coverage of Contraceptives,\" April 17, 2026, https://www.guttmacher.org/state-policy/explore/insurance-coverage-contraceptives . The Guttmacher Institute is a policy research organization focused on access to reproductive health services. See also National Conference of State Legislatures (NCSL), \"State Contraception Policies,\" updated July 17, 2026, https://www.ncsl.org/health/state-contraception-policies .")

Are There Exemptions for the Federal Contraceptive Coverage Requirement?

The ACA's implementing regulations initially exempted only houses of worship and similar religious orders from the contraceptive coverage requirement (i.e., with regard to the health plans they offer to their employees).[247](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn247 "Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury,   \" Group Health Plans and Health Insurance Issuers Relating to Coverage of Preventive Services Under the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act,\" 76 Federal Register 46621, August 3, 2011, https://www.federalregister.gov/d/2011-19684 .") Litigation and court rulings prompted a series of revised rules expanding upon these exemptions.[248](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn248 "See Little Sisters of the Poor Saints Peter & Paul Home v. Pennsylvania, 591 U.S. 657, 663–73 (2020) (discussing iterations of the federal contraceptive coverage rules and relevant litigation).") The latest iteration of these rules from November 2018 made exemptions available to most types of nonprofit and for-profit entities with sincerely held religious or moral beliefs against contraception.[249](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn249 "Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury, \" Religious Exemptions and Accommodations for Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Under the Affordable Care Act,\" 83 Federal Register 57536, November 15, 2018, https://www.federalregister.gov/d/2018-24512 . Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury, \"Moral Exemptions and Accommodations for Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Under the Affordable Care Act,\" 83 Federal Register 57592, November 15, 2018, https://www.federalregister.gov/...") As of the date of this report, the November 2018 rules are not in effect as a result of a federal district court decision that is on appeal.[250](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn250 "See Pennsylvania v. Trump , 795 F. Supp. 3d 607 (E.D. Pa. 2025), appeals filed , Nos. 25-2575, 25-2662 (3d Cir. 2026). Questions from congressional clients regarding legal issues addressed in this section may be directed to Victoria L. Killion, CRS Legislative Attorney.")

Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

Federal law does not generally *require* or *prohibit* private health insurance coverage of abortion services. However, per the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978 (PDA; [P.L. 95-555](https://www.congress.gov/law/95/pub/555)), applicable employers that provide health coverage to their employees must ensure coverage for abortion in cases of life endangerment or medical complications, as specified.[251](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn251 "See 42 U.S.C. §2000e(k) , including: \"This subsection shall not require an employer to pay for health insurance benefits for abortion, except where the life of the mother would be endangered if the fetus were carried to term, or except where medical complications have arisen from an abortion: Provided, That nothing herein shall preclude an employer from providing abortion benefits or otherwise affect bargaining agreements in regard to abortion.\" For more information about the Pregnancy Disc...")

There are federal provisionsaddressing abortion coverage by private health insurance plans, including qualified health plans (QHPs), which are private health insurance plans certified to meet relevant requirements to be sold in the health insurance exchanges.[252](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn252 "For more information on the exchanges and qualified health plans (QHPs), see CRS Report R44065, Health Insurance Exchanges and Qualified Health Plans: Overview and Policy Updates . QHPs may also be offered outside the exchanges, but this report's references to QHPs are generally regarding QHPs offered on the exchanges.") For example, the ACA specifies that none of its provisions "shall be construed" to require a QHP to cover abortion.[253](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn253 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b).")

In addition, while federal EHB provisions generally require nongroup and small-group plans (including QHPs) to provide coverage substantially equal to a state's selected EHB benchmark plan, there is an exception for abortion. In other words, even if a state selects an EHB benchmark plan that covers abortion services, applicable plans are not *federally* required to cover abortion in order to meet EHB standards.[254](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn254 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b) (1)(A) and 45 C.F.R. § 156.280(c) for QHPs; 45 C.F.R. §156.115(c ) for all plans subject to EHB requirements . Also see the \" Overview: Coverage of the Essential Health Benefits (EHB) \" section of this report.")

State Requirements and Additional Data

States play a central role in the regulation of private health insurance, and they may implement their own abortion coverage requirements on the plans they regulate.[255](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn255 "In general, states may regulate nongroup plans and fully insured group plans but not self-insured group plans. See the introduction to this section of this report.") The ACA also specifies that states are allowed to prohibit abortion coverage by QHPs offered in their exchange.[256](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn256 "42 U.S.C. §18023(a).") Furthermore, federal provisions regarding abortion coverage do not preempt any state laws "regarding the prohibition of (or requirement of) coverage, funding, or procedural requirements on abortions."[257](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn257 "42 U.S.C. §18023(c).") This means that beyond the issues discussed above, states are able to prohibit or require abortion coverage by any or all of the plans they regulate. Regarding the EHB example above, even though plans may not be federally required to cover abortion services, there may be applicable state requirements.

A July 2026 KFF analysis of state requirements on state-regulated private health insurance plans found the following:[258](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn258 "KFF, \" Abortion Coverage Limitations in Medicaid and Private Insurance Plans,\" updated July 24 , 2026, https://www.kff.org/womens-health-policy/abortion-coverage-limitations-in-medicaid-and-private-insurance-plans/ .")

* Ten states prohibit private health insurance plans, including QHPs, from covering abortion. Another 15 states prohibit coverage of abortion by QHPs but not other plans. Some states allow for certain exceptions, such as coverage if the abortion is necessary to save the woman's life. Some states allow insurers to sell a "rider" (a separate policy) for abortion coverage, although it is not clear how many insurers offer such riders.
* Thirteen states require private health insurance plans, including QHPs, to include abortion coverage. See below for further discussion of federal requirements related to financing of QHPs' coverage of abortion.
* The remaining 12 states and DC do not require or limit private health insurance abortion coverage by private health insurance plans, including QHPs. Per KFF review of QHP benefit materials in these states' exchanges, there are no QHPs covering abortion in six of these states. No information was provided on abortion coverage outside of the exchanges in the states that neither require nor prohibit it.

Data on private health insurance coverage of abortion may include plans subject to applicable requirements as well as plans providing coverage voluntarily. According to a KFF analysis of abortion coverage in the largest health plan offered by employers with 200 or more employees in 2023, 10% of such plans did not provide any abortion coverage, 18% provided coverage "under limited circumstances," 32% provided coverage "in most or all circumstances," and 40% of the respondents "do not know if their largest plan covers abortions, part of which could be attributed to lack of information about coverage for abortion in plan documents unless abortion is explicitly excluded."[259](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn259 "KFF, \"Coverage of Abortion in Large Employer-Sponsored Plans in 2023,\" February 29, 2024, https://www.kff.org/womens-health-policy/coverage-of-abortion-in-large-employer-sponsored-plans-in-2023/ . See Table 1 in this article for additional data by firm size, firm ownership (e.g., private or public) and region, and plan funding (e.g., self-insured or fully insured). The article also briefly discusses changes in coverage since the Dobbs decision, and employer-provided financial assistance for...")

Can Federal Funds Be Used to Pay for Abortion in Private Health Insurance Plans?

As discussed earlier in this report, there are annual restrictions on HHS appropriations known as the Hyde Amendment, generally providing that HHS funds may not finance abortion except in cases of rape, incest, or endangerment of a mother's life (sometimes referred to as Hyde "excepted" abortions).[260](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn260 "See the \" Can Federal Funds Be Used to Pay for Abortions or Abortion Counseling? \" introductory section of this report. For more information on PTCs, see CRS Report R44425, Health Insurance Premium Tax Credit and Cost-Sharing Reductions .") In other words, HHS funding is prohibited for abortion in other cases, referred to here as "non-excepted" abortions.

The ACA also includes restrictions related to the financing of abortion coverage, for QHPs that provide such coverage. These restrictions apply to the exchange financial subsidies that consumers may be eligible for, as well as the premiums that consumers pay themselves.

Certain consumers purchasing QHP coverage in the individual exchanges are eligible to receive premium tax credits (PTCs) from the federal government that effectively reduce the cost of specified plans. The PTCs are tax expenditures financed through a permanent appropriation; they are not part of HHS appropriations.[261](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn261 "31 U.S.C. §1324(b) (2) .") Such consumers may also be eligible for cost-sharing reductions (CSRs) that effectively reduce their out-of-pocket costs. The CSRs are not currently federally financed, but insurers must provide these subsidies to eligible consumers.[262](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn262 "For more information, see CRS Insight IN12562, Financing Cost-Sharing Reduction Reimbursements to Private Health Plans .")

The ACA's restrictions related to abortion coverage in QHPs reference the Hyde Amendment and its distinctions on abortion for which HHS funding is prohibited or allowed.[263](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn263 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b)(1)(B). Specifically, this ACA provision distinguishes between abortion for which the expenditure of HHS appropriations is prohibited or allowed , \"based on the law as in effect as of the date that is 6 months before the beginning of the plan year involved.\"") Under the ACA, individuals who receive a PTC and/or CSR are permitted to select a QHP that includes coverage for non-excepted abortions, to the extent that such coverage is allowed and offered in their state's exchange. However, the health insurer providing the QHP cannot use any funds attributable to the PTC or CSR to pay for non-excepted abortions.[264](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn264 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b)(2)(A) and 45 C.F.R. §156.280(e)(1).") Per regulations, insurers are required to exclude the value of such services when they calculate the premium rate on which any PTCs will be based.[265](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn265 "45 C.F.R. §156.470(a)(1).") This effectively means that if a QHP covers non-excepted abortions, the PTC will not reflect the value of such coverage.

Insurers offering QHPs that cover non-excepted abortions are also subject to ACA requirements and implementing regulations regarding the premiums that each enrollee pays for such a plan. The insurer is required to collect two separate payments from each enrollee (regardless of whether an enrollee receives a PTC, and regardless of an enrollee's "age, sex, or family status"): one payment that reflects an amount equal to the portion of the premium for coverage of health services other than non-excepted abortions, and another payment that reflects an amount equal to the actuarial value of the coverage for non-excepted abortions.[266](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn266 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b)(2)(B)(i) and 45 C.F.R. §156.280(e)(2)(i) and (ii). Through rulemaking, different Administrations have taken varied approaches to implementing this requirement. The most recent such rule, which also discusses prior rulemaking, is HHS, \"Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act; Updating Payment Parameters, Section 1332 Waiver Implementing Regulations, and Improving Health Insurance Markets for 2022 and Beyond,\" 86 Federal Register 53412, September 27, 2021, starting at pa...") The latter must be no less than $1 per enrollee, per month.[267](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn267 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b)(2)(D) and 45 C.F.R. §156.280(e)(4).") The insurer is required to deposit the separate payments into separate allocation accounts that consist solely of each type of payment and that are used exclusively to pay for the specified services.[268](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn268 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b)(2)(B)(ii) and (b)(2)(C), as well as 45 C.F.R. §156.280(e)(2)(iii) and (e)(3). For further guidance on the segregated accounts, including allowable uses of the segregated funds after the end of a plan year, see CMS, \"Frequently Asked Questions (FAQs) on Usage of Funds in Section 1303 Segregated Accounts by Qualified Health Plan (QHP) Issuers in the Individual Market,\" December 9, 2025, https://www.cms.gov/files/document/2025-faqs-1303-segregated-funds.pdf.")

State health insurance commissioners must ensure compliance with the segregation requirements in accordance with "applicable provisions of generally accepted accounting requirements," Office of Management and Budget (OMB) circulars on funds management, and Government Accountability Office (GAO) guidance on accounting.[269](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn269 "42 U.S.C. §18023(b)(2)(E)(i) and 45 C.F.R. §156.280(e)(5).") Also, within regulations generally addressing oversight of QHPs by the exchanges and HHS, the exchanges are required to review QHP insurers' valuation of their premiums for PTC purposes (which must exclude any coverage of non-excepted abortions), and exchanges must submit information as specified to HHS, for purpose of HHS oversight of the subsidies.[270](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn270 "45 C.F.R. §155.1030(b).")

Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Infertility Services?

No federal law specifically requires private health insurance coverage of infertility services. However, the requirement that certain plans cover 10 categories of EHB may be relevant, depending on state implementation.[271](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn271 "See the \" Overview: Coverage of the Essential Health Benefits (EHB) \" section of this report.") If a state selects an EHB benchmark plan that includes infertility services in one or more EHB categories, then applicable plans in that state must provide coverage substantially equal to the benchmark plan's coverage. EHB requirements apply to nongrandfathered plans in the nongroup and small-group markets.

State Requirements and Additional Data

States play a central role in the regulation of private health insurance, and they may implement their own infertility services coverage requirements on the plans they regulate.[272](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn272 "In general, states may regulate nongroup plans and fully insured group plans but not self-insured group plans. See the introduction to this section of this report.") Per an analysis by a fertility treatment stakeholder group, at least 25 states and DC have enacted requirements on private health insurance coverage of infertility services, as of late 2025.[273](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn273 "Resolve, \"Insurance Coverage by State,\" accessed June 2, 2026, https://resolve.org/learn/financial-resources-for-family-building/insurance-coverage/insurance-coverage-by-state/ . Also see Resolve, \"About Insurance Coverage,\" https://resolve.org/learn/financial-resources/insurance-coverage/.") These state laws vary widely in terms of their particular coverage requirements. For example, some of the laws are specific to coverage of fertility preservation services (e.g., before a covered medical treatment that could result in infertility), some generally require coverage of infertility treatments but do not mandate IVF coverage, and some require coverage of IVF and other ART services, to varying degrees. The state laws also vary terms of their applicability to (and exceptions for) different types of state-regulated plans, and their approaches to defining infertility and otherwise defining any patient requirements for coverage.

Data on private health insurance coverage of infertility services may include plans subject to applicable requirements as well as plans providing coverage voluntarily. The consulting firm Mercer fields an annual survey of employer-sponsored health plans in the United States.[274](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn274 "Information in this paragraph is from Mercer , \"National Survey of Employer-Sponsored Health Plans: 2025 Survey Report\" and accompanying Survey Tables, March 2026, available for purchase at https://shop.mercer.com/catalog/national-survey-of-employer-sponsored-health-plans.html (h ereinafter referred to as \"Mercer 2025 Survey Report, March 2026\" ). According to Mercer, its \"survey results have been weighted using employer size and geographic stratification to represent all employer healt...") According to its 2025 report, 71% of employers with 500 or more employees covered some type of fertility benefits. This includes, for example, 66% of employers with more than 500 employees that provide coverage of fertility evaluation services, 50% that provide coverage of IVF, and 22% that provide coverage of elective egg freezing. Coverage limits and cost-sharing requirements may vary across these plans, subject to applicable federal or state requirements. Per Mercer, "About two-thirds of employers [with 500 or more employees] that offer fertility benefits (66 percent) say that the benefits are intended to be inclusive—in other words, eligibility is not limited to women who meet the clinical definition of infertile." Coverage of fertility benefits generally varies by employer size: In 2025, 46% of employers with 50 or more employees, and 89% of employers with 20,000 or more employees, covered some type of fertility benefits.

Per CRS analysis of 2026 QHP benefit coverage, there are QHPs providing infertility coverage in 17 of the 30 states that use the federal exchange platform Healthcare.gov. This includes six states in which fewer than half of QHPs provide such coverage, two states in which more than half of QHPs provide such coverage, and nine states in which all QHPs provide such coverage.[275](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn275 "CRS analysis of the CMS 2026 Exchange Public Use File (PUF) Datasets, \"Benefits and Cost Sharing PUF\" and \"Plan Attributes PUF,\" updated April 28, 2026, https://www.cms.gov/marketplace/resources/data/public-use-files. These files provide data on covered benefits and other plan features for the qualified health plans (QHPs) and certain other plans offered on the health insurance exchanges in the 3 0 states that utilize the federal platform Healthcare.gov in plan year 2026 . The files do not...") There are state requirements on infertility coverage in at least five of these nine Healthcare.gov states in which all QHPs provide some infertility coverage.[276](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn276 "This is per CRS cross-reference of the resource discussed above in this section, on states with requirements on private health insurance coverage of infertility services. CRS did not search further for other state requirements that may be applicable.")

Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Maternity Services?

There are several federal requirements relevant to private health insurance coverage of maternity services.

EHB and Preventive Services Requirements: Maternity Services

One of the categories of required EHB coverage is "maternity and newborn care."[277](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn277 "See the \" Overview: Coverage of the Essential Health Benefits (EHB) \" section of this report.") This means that nongrandfathered plans in the nongroup and small-group markets must provide coverage of maternity and newborn care services substantially equal to such coverage provided by the state's EHB benchmark plan. The same is true of other EHB categories, some of which may also include services relevant to maternity care.

In addition, the preventive services provision described earlier in this section includes the requirement for applicable plans to cover, without cost sharing, certain prenatal and postpartum services, as recommended.[278](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn278 "See the \" Overview: Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Without Cost Sharing \" section of this report.") For example, the recommendation on "well-woman preventive visits" includes pre-pregnancy, prenatal, postpartum, and interpregnancy visits at which relevant recommended preventive services and items can be provided.[279](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn279 "HRSA, \"Women's Preventive Services Guidelines ,\" accessed June 2026, https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines .") There are also multiple recommendations on preventive services as specified for pregnant and postpartum individuals, including certain STI screenings, mental health and substance use screening and counseling, gestational diabetes screening and healthy weight counseling, folic acid supplements, and breastfeeding services and supplies.[280](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn280 "A number of USPSTF preventive services recommendations and HRSA Women's Preventive Services Guidelines are relevant to pregnant and postpartu m individuals . Certain HRSA/Bright Futures guidelines and ACIP recommendations are also relevant to pregnant and postpartum individuals. See footnotes footnote 219 through footnote 222 in this report for links to each resource.")

Coverage of Pregnancy-Related Conditions

The Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978 (PDA, [P.L. 95-555](https://www.congress.gov/law/95/pub/555)) requires applicable employers offering health insurance to cover "expenses for pregnancy-related conditions on the same basis as expenses for other medical conditions" for employees enrolled in the group plan.[281](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn281 "See 42 U.S.C. §2000e; 29 C.F.R. §1604.10, and, including for the language quoted above, 29 C.F.R. §1604, Appendix to Part 1604 —Questions and Answers on the Pregnancy Discrimination Act, P.L. 95-555 , 92 Stat. 2076 (1978) (hereinafter referred to as \"29 C.F.R. §1604 Appendix\"). Also see EEOC, \"Enforcement Guidance on Pregnancy Discrimination and Related Issues\" (particularly the \"Health Insurance\" section), June 25, 2015, https://www.eeoc.gov/laws/guidance/enforcement-guidance-pregnancy-disc...") If the group plan offers coverage to employees' spouses and other dependents, the requirement to cover pregnancy-related services also applies to employees' spouses, but not necessarily to other dependents, enrolled in the plan.[282](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn282 "See 29 C.F.R. §1604 Appendix, questions 21-23 regarding coverage of pregnancy-related conditions for spouses and other dependents. Also note that other federal requirements are relevant to employers' offer of coverage for dependents. For example, most plans that offer dependent coverage are required to make that coverage available for both married and unmarried adult children under the age of 26 (42 U.S.C. §300gg-14 and 45 C.F.R. §147.120). In addition, the employer shared-responsibility prov...")

There do not appear to be specific requirements related to cost sharing, out-of-network coverage, or medical management, other than the requirement that features of the plan related to coverage of pregnancy-related conditions must not be substantially different than they are for other medical conditions. For example, if a plan has an overall deductible, it cannot have a higher deductible for pregnancy-related services. The PDA applies to employers with 15 or more employees, whether the coverage is fully insured or self-insured.[283](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn283 "EEOC Q&A.")

Coverage of Minimum Hospital Stay After Childbirth

The Newborns' and Mothers' Health Protection Act of 1996 ([P.L. 104-204](https://www.congress.gov/law/104/pub/204), as amended) prohibits most plans (if they otherwise cover maternity-related hospital stays) from restricting the length of a hospital stay for childbirth for either the mother or newborn child to less than 48 hours following vaginal deliveries and to less than 96 hours following caesarian deliveries.[284](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn284 "42 U.S.C. §300gg-25; 45 C.F.R. §146.130.") In addition, prior authorization requirements for these stays are prohibited. There is an exception to the length-of-coverage requirement when providers make earlier discharge decisions in consultation with mothers. Plans are prohibited from offering incentives or penalties to providers or mothers to encourage shorter stays.

Cost sharing is allowed, as long as the cost sharing for the portions of hospital stays addressed by this law (those following deliveries) is not greater than cost sharing for preceding portions of such stays. The law does not specify whether its requirements apply out-of-network.

The law generally applies to nongroup, small-group, large-group, and self-insured plans (that cover maternity-related hospital stays), regardless of grandfathered status. The law's hospital stay requirements do not apply when a state has its own law (meeting specified requirements) about such hospital stays.

State Requirements

States play a central role in the regulation of private health insurance, and they may implement their own maternity services coverage requirements on the plans they regulate.[285](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn285 "In general, states may regulate nongroup plans and fully insured group plans but not self-insured group plans. See the introduction to this section of this report.") The National Conference of State Legislatures (NCSL) has several state law databases that can be searched for particular topics of interest, including health insurance requirements on coverage of and cost-sharing for maternity care benefits, coverage of maternity care furnished by certain types of providers, and other topics.[286](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn286 "See, for example, National Conference of State Legislatures (NCSL), \"Health Costs, Coverage, and Delivery State Legislation,\" 2022-present, https://www.ncsl.org/health/health-costs-coverage-and-delivery-state-legislation . Also see NCSL, \"Health Innovations State Law Archives Database, 2015-2021,\" https://www.ncsl.org/health/health-innovations-state-law-archive-database-2015-2021 , and NCSL, \"Maternal and Child Health Legislative Database,\" 2017-present, https://www.ncsl.org/health/mater...") For example, searches in their "Health Costs, Coverage, and Delivery State Legislation" database for keywords including "maternity," "birth," "postpartum," and "doula," filtered for "market," return various results.

Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

There are certain federal requirements relevant to private health insurance coverage of reproductive health screening, prevention, and treatment services.

Preventive Services and EHB Requirements: Prevention and Treatment Services

The preventive services provision includes the requirement for applicable plans to cover, without cost sharing, certain reproductive health screening and preventive services and items, as recommended for women, men, and/or adolescents.[287](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn287 "See the \" Overview: Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Without Cost Sharing \" section of this report.") This includes, for example,

* screening and counseling for STIs/STDs, universal HIV screening, and PrEP medication for persons at high risk of HIV acquisition (oral and injectable formulations);
* breast cancer screening, genetic testing, and preventive medications such as Tamoxifen (to lower the risk of developing breast cancer among women with specified risk factors);
* gynecological exams, Pap smears, and cervical cancer screenings;
* patient navigation for breast and cervical cancer screening services; and
* colorectal cancer screenings, screening for urinary incontinence, and the HPV vaccine.[288](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn288 "A number of USPSTF preventive services recommendations and HRSA Women's Preventive Services Guidelines are relevant to reproductive health screening and prevention services . Certain HRSA/Bright Futures guidelines and ACIP recommendations are also relevant to such services . See footnotes 219 through 222 in this report for links to each resource. Regarding coverage of specified oral and injectable forms of PrEP, also see ACA FAQ 68, October 2024.")

It also includes at least one well-woman preventive visit per year, or a series of visits as needed, for women to obtain all recommended preventive services.

As stated in the earlier overview of the preventive services coverage requirement, applicable plans must cover, without cost-sharing, services and items that are integral to the furnishing of a recommended preventive service. Agency guidance has clarified that this includes coverage of ancillary services such as blood testing recommended to monitor one's health status while on PrEP, and coverage of "polyp removal during or anesthesia provided in connection with a preventive screening colonoscopy," among other examples.[289](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn289 "Departments of Labor, HHS, and the Treasury, \"FAQs A bout Affordable Care Act Implementation Part 47,\" July 19, 2021, https://www.cms.gov/CCIIO/Resources/Fact-Sheets-and-FAQs/Downloads/FAQs-Part-47.pdf . Also see ACA FAQ 54 , July 2022 . See question 1 in both documents.") Recent updates to HRSA Guidelines on breast cancer and cervical cancer screening have also specified that additional imaging, testing, or pathological evaluation may be needed to complete a screening process.[290](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn290 "See \"Screening for Cervical Cancer\" (updated December 2025) and \"Breast Cancer Screening for Women at Average Risk\" (updated December 2024) at HRSA, \"Women's Preventive Services Guidelines,\" accessed June 2026, https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines .")

Plans are required to provide coverage of the recommended *preventive* services and items, *as specified* in relevant recommendations and guidelines. For example, the USPSTF and HRSA both recommend cervical cancer screening for average risk or asymptomatic women aged 21-65, using different methodologies and at different intervals depending on age.[291](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn291 "Ibid . , regarding HRSA guideline on cervical cancer screening. Also see USPSTF, \"Cervical Cancer: Screening,\" August 21, 2018, https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/uspstf/recommendation /cervical-cancer-screening.") Plans must provide coverage accordingly, but otherwise may use "reasonable medical management techniques to determine the frequency, method, treatment, or setting for an item or service."[292](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn292 "45 C.F.R. §147.130(a)(4).")

If someone needs a *diagnostic* test (e.g., for breast cancer) based on the presence of relevant symptoms—or otherwise seeks a test outside of the relevant recommendations—the preventive services coverage requirements generally do not apply. The plan may still provide coverage, with or without cost-sharing, subject to any other applicable requirements. Similarly, if a recommended preventive screening results in a diagnosis of a condition (such as an STI or reproductive cancer), no federal laws specifically mandate coverage of relevant *treatment* services. However, treatments for various conditions may be covered under different EHB categories in the benchmark plans selected by states, which would require applicable plans to cover such treatments.[293](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn293 "See the \" Overview: Coverage of the Essential Health Benefits (EHB) \" section of this report.")

Coverage of Reconstruction after Mastectomy

The Women's Health and Cancer Rights Act of 1998 (WHCRA; [P.L. 105-277](https://www.congress.gov/law/105/pub/277), as amended) requires that if plans provide coverage for mastectomies, they must also cover breast reconstruction services "in a manner determined in consultation with the attending physician and the patient."[294](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn294 "29 U.S.C. §1185b , 42 U.S.C. §300gg-27 , and 42 U.S.C. §300gg-52 .") The coverage must include all stages of reconstruction on the breast on which the mastectomy was performed, surgery and reconstruction on the other breast for a symmetrical appearance, and prostheses and other specified supplies. Agency guidance has also clarified the applicability of WHCRA coverage requirements to nipple and areola reconstruction and chest wall reconstruction with aesthetic flat closure.[295](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn295 "See D epartments of Labor , HHS, and the Treasury, \"FAQs About Affordable Care Act Implementation Part 31, Mental Health Parity Implementation, And Women's Health And Cancer Rights Act Implementation,\" April 20, 2016, https://www.cms.gov/CCIIO/Resources/Fact-Sheets-and-FAQs/Downloads/FAQs-31_Final-4-20-16.pdf , question 12. Also see ACA FAQ 68, October 2024, question 7.") Guidance also has provided that this coverage requirement is applicable to female and male enrollees, and the mastectomy does not need to have been connected to a cancer diagnosis.[296](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn296 "S ee Employee Benefits Security Administration, \"Compliance Assistance Guide: Health Benefits Coverage Under Federal La w, \" September 2014, https://www.dol.gov/general/topic/health-plans/womens .")

Cost sharing is allowed if consistent with cost sharing for other covered medical/surgical benefits. The law does not specify whether its requirements apply out-of-network.

The requirement applies to nongroup, small-group, large-group, and self-insured plans, regardless of grandfathered status.

State Requirements

States play a central role in the regulation of private health insurance, and they may implement their own reproductive health prevention (and treatment) services coverage requirements on the plans they regulate.[297](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn297 "In general, states may regulate nongroup plans and fully ins ured group plans but not self-insured group plans. See the introduction to this section of this report.")
