For example, at one of the NCSL state legislation databases cited earlier, a search for keywords "breast" or "mammog" returns numerous recently enacted state laws, and a search for "sexually transmitted" returns several such state laws.[298](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn298 "NCSL, \"Health Costs, Coverage, and Delivery State Legislation,\" 2022-present, at https://www.ncsl.org/health/health-costs-coverage-and-delivery-state-legislation . Searches conducted June 4, 2026, with a filter on \"market.\"")

Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Gender-Affirming Services?

No federal law specifically requires or prohibits private health insurance coverage of gender-affirming services. However, regulations and guidance have addressed other federal benefit coverage requirements, with respect to such services.

Regulatory Prohibition on Coverage of Specified Sex-Trait Modification Procedures as an Essential Health Benefit

As discussed earlier, federal regulations on essential health benefit requirements generally allow states to specify their EHB package by selecting a benchmark plan, and applicable plans in the state must provide coverage that is "substantially equal." [299](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn299 "See the \" Overview: Coverage of the Essential Health Benefits (EHB) \" section of this report. EHB regulations include requirements on states in their selection of benchmark plans (e.g., 45 C.F.R. § 156.110-111) and requirements on applicable plans in their coverage of the EHB (e.g., 45 C.F.R. § 156.115).") Per statute, the EHB must be equal to the scope of benefits provided under a "typical employer plan," but beyond the 10 required categories of EHB, statute does not require or prohibit the coverage of any particular benefits as EHB.[300](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn300 "42 U.S.C. §18022(b)(2).") Regulations since 2013 have specified that certain benefits may not be covered *as EHB* (e.g., long-term/custodial nursing home care benefits; nonmedically necessary orthodontia).[301](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn301 "45 C.F.R. §156.115(d) as added by rulemaking in 2013. The 2013 rule also prohibited the coverage of routine non-pediatric eye exams and routine non-pediatric dental services as EHB. A 2024 rule allowed coverage of routine non-pediatric dental as EHB, but a 2026 rule prohibited such coverage again. See 45 C.F.R. §156.115 for rulemaking citations.")

For the benefits that may not be covered as EHB, plans may still provide such coverage as *non-EHB*, either voluntarily or subject to state requirements other than as related to the EHB. However, if a benefit is not considered EHB, it is not subject to certain other federal requirements that apply only to the EHB (such as a maximum annual limit on cost sharing). In addition, for consumers eligible for subsidized coverage on the health insurance exchanges, the calculation of the subsidies may include only the value of the EHB.[302](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn302 "45 C.F.R. § 156.470.")

In June 2025, HHS issued a "Marketplace Integrity" final rule that, among other things, prohibited plans from covering "specified sex-trait modification procedures" as EHB.[303](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn303 "Department of HHS, \"Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act; Marketplace Integrity and Affordability,\" 90 Federal Register 27074, June 25, 2025, https://www.federalregister.gov/d/2025-11606 . Hereinafter referred to as the \"2025 Marketplace Integrity final rule.\"") This was defined as "any pharmaceutical or surgical intervention that is provided for the purpose of attempting to align an individual's physical appearance or body with an asserted identity that differs from the individual's sex," as specified.[304](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn304 "This definition was added at 45 C.F.R. §156.400 and referenced at 45 C.F.R. §156.115(d).") The definition excluded treatments for certain conditions (e.g., "a medically verifiable disorder of sexual development"), such that these treatments could still be covered as EHB. In the preamble to the final rule, HHS indicated that mental health and substance use disorder services as related to gender dysphoria treatment could still be covered as EHB.[305](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn305 "2025 Marketplace Integrity final rule, page 27159, https://www.federalregister.gov/d/2025-11606/p-943 . Within this section of the preamble, HHS also discusse d that its use of the term \"specified sex-trait modification procedure\" was intended to have a narrower scope than the term \"gender affirming care,\" per stakeholder comments that the latter \"generally encompasses a broader set of medical services, such as mental health services.\"")

This provision of the final rule was applicable as of plan years beginning January 2026, but as part of a legal challenge, in August 2026, a U.S. district court vacated the provision.[306](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn306 "California v. Kennedy , No. 25-12019, 2026 U.S. Dist. LEXIS 182153, at *24 (D. Mass. Aug. 14, 2026).")

In the preamble to the final rule, HHS reported that five states have EHB benchmark plans that specifically include "coverage of some sex-trait modification," six states have benchmark plans that neither include nor exclude such coverage, and 40 states have EHB benchmark plans that exclude such coverage.[307](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn307 "2025 Marketplace Integrity final rule, page 27154, rule footnote 196. These numbers in the final rule are similar to those in an analysis submitted to CMS by an LGBT health policy research organization in January 2023: Whitman Walker Institute, Comment Letter to the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services, \"RE: Request for Information on the Essential Health Benefits (87 Federal Register 74097, Dec. 2, 2022),\" January 31, 2023, https://www.regulations.gov/comment/CMS-2022-0186-0663 .") The final rule did not require states to change their benchmark plans; it modified the EHB requirements on plans.[308](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn308 "Although the 2025 Marketplace Integrity final rule did not require states to change their EHB benchmark plans with respect to the specified sex-trait modification procedures, it did discuss implications for states that have relevant benefits in their benchmark plan, and/or otherwise have state mandates on such coverage. See the final rule for discussions of existing requirements on state defrayal of certain costs of required benefits, at 45 C.F.R. § 155.170.")

Guidance on Coverage of Sex-Specified Recommended Preventive Services

Federal guidance on the preventive services coverage requirement[309](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn309 "See the \" Overview: Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Without Cost Sharing \" section of this report.") has addressed coverage of sex-specific recommended preventive services, as related to an individual's sex assigned at birth, gender identity, or recorded gender. Applicable plans must provide coverage without cost-sharing of recommended preventive services that are determined "medically appropriate for a particular individual" by the individual's attending provider. This may include, for example, "a mammogram or pap smear for a transgender man who has residual breast tissue or an intact cervix – and the individual otherwise satisfies the criteria in the relevant recommendation or guideline as well as all other applicable coverage requirements."[310](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn310 "ACA FAQ XXVI, May 2015, question 5. This was referenced again in ACA FAQ 64, January 2024, footnote 14.")

State Requirements and Additional Data

States play a central role in the regulation of private health insurance, and they may implement their own gender-affirming services coverage requirements on the plans they regulate.[311](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn311 "In general, states may regulate nongroup plans and fully insured group plans but not self-insured group plans. See the introduction to this section of this report .") According to a stakeholder group tracking such laws, as of 2026, 24 states and the District of Columbia prohibit health insurance plans from explicitly excluding coverage of gender-affirming care, and two states permit such exclusions.[312](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn312 "Movement Advancement Project (MAP), \"Equality Maps: Healthcare Laws and Policies: Private Insurance,\" accessed July 21, 2026, https://mapresearch.org/equality-map/healthcare-laws-and-policies/#private-insurance . MAP is a policy research organization focused on \"equality and opportunity for all.\"")

Data on private health insurance coverage of gender-affirming services may include plans subject to applicable requirements as well as plans providing coverage voluntarily. Per Mercer's 2025 survey report on U.S. employer-sponsored health benefits, 23% of all employers with 50 or more employees provide coverage for "gender affirmation surgery," and such coverage is more likely as employer size increases (18% of employers with 50-499 employees, 54% of employers with 500 or more employees, and 78% of employers with 20,000 or more employees). Mercer found that among employers covering the surgery, most also cover some associated services. For example, among employers with 500 or more employees that do cover the surgical services, 77% also cover nonsurgical gender affirmation treatment/hormone therapy while fewer cover reconstructive procedures (49%) or puberty suppression (25%).[313](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn313 "Mercer 2025 Survey Report, March 2026. See footnote 274 for additional details.")

Per CRS analysis of 2026 QHP benefit coverage, there are QHPs providing coverage of gender-affirming care services in 22 of the 30 states that use the federal exchange platform Healthcare.gov. This includes 18 states in which fewer than half of QHPs provide such coverage, three states in which more than half of QHPs provide such coverage, and one state in which all QHPs provide such coverage.[314](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn314 "CRS analysis of the CMS 2026 Exchange Public Use File (PUF) Datasets, \"Benefits and Cost Sharing PUF\" and \"Plan Attributes PUF,\" updated April 28, 2026, https://www.cms.gov/marketplace/resources/data/public-use-files. These files provide data on covered benefits and other plan features for the qualified health plans (QHPs) and certain other plans offered on the health insurance exchanges in the 3 0 states that utilize the federal platform Healthcare.gov in plan year 2026 . The files do not i...")

Federal Employees Health Benefits Program (FEHB)

The Federal Employees Health Benefits (FEHB) Program, which includes the Postal Service Health Benefits Program (PSHB), provides health insurance to most federal employees, retirees, and their dependents, as well as certain Tribal employees and their families.[315](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn315 "Members of Congress and certain congressional staff no longer receive health benefits through FEHB as a benefit of their employment but may be eligible to enroll in FEHB in retirement. In addition, l ike most other federal agencies, the USPS offers health care benefits to its employees, retirees, and their dependents through FEHB. Since 2025, USPS employees, retirees, and their dependents have received health benefits through Postal Service Health Benefits (PSHB) Program. In this report, r...") As of the end of 2025, FEHB covered more than 8 million people.[316](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn316 "U.S. Office of Personnel Management, Federal Benefits Open Season Highlights 202 6 Plan Year , 202 5 , p. 1, https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/healthcare/reference-materials/reference/federal-benefits-open-season-highlights-for-plan-year-2026.pdf .")

The FEHB Program is administered by the Office of Personnel Management (OPM) in accordance with statute and its implementing regulations.[317](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn317 "See Title 5, Chapter 89 of the U.S. Code , and Title 5, Chapter 1, Subchapter B , Part 890 and Title 48, Chapter 16 of the Code of Federal Regulations .") OPM contracts with private health insurance carriers to make plans available to eligible individuals. The FEHB statute establishes the basic rules for benefits, enrollment, and participation, among other general requirements, while still allowing OPM broad authority to implement regulations, contract with carriers, establish benefits, and administer the program.

Premium costs are shared between the federal government and the employee or retiree. The government contributions toward FEHB premiums are funded through discretionary spending and mandatory spending.[318](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn318 "More specifically, a gency payments for government contributions to their nonpostal employees ' premiums are classified as discretionary spending. Payments for the government share of nonpostal retiree premiums are classified as mandatory spending. The government contributions for postal employees and postal retirees come from USPS revenues and the Postal Service Retiree Health Benefits Fund, respectively.") The government's share of premiums for all retirees and for nonpostal employees is set in statute, while for United States Postal Service (USPS) employees, the USPS contribution to premiums is determined through collective bargaining agreements.

Do FEHB Plans Cover Reproductive Health Services?

FEHB plans cover reproductive health services consistent with statutory and OPM requirements. Statute specifies coverage of certain broad categories of benefits, such as hospital, surgical, and obstetrical benefits.[319](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn319 "5 U.S.C. § 8904.") In addition, plans are required or encouraged to cover additional benefits that are specified by OPM as it contracts with plans to participate in the FEHB program. For example, OPM requires FEHB plans to cover certain infertility services, and encourages them to offer other infertility benefits, as discussed below.[320](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn320 "OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits Program Call Letter , \" Letter Number 2023 - 04 , March 1 , 20 23, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2023/2023-04.pdf , and OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits Program Call Letter , \" Letter Number 2022 - 03 , February 17 , 20 22, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2022/2022-03.pdf (h ereinafter OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits Program Call Letter (2024),\" and...")

FEHB plans also are required to cover certain preventive services without cost-sharing, and to cover the EHB, generally consistent with how such requirements apply to private health insurance plans.[321](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn321 "The preventive services requirement for FEHB plans is similar to the requirement applicable to most private health insurance plans. For more information see OPM, \" FEHB Program Carrier Letter,\" Letter Number 2019-01, March 14, 2019, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2019/2019-01.pdf , and \" Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Reproductive Health Services? \" In regards to the application of the private health insurance EHB requi...")

Outside of applicable requirements, benefits and cost-sharing vary among FEHB plans.

Do FEHB Plans Cover Contraceptive Services?

Appropriations language generally requires FEHB plans to include "a provision for contraceptive coverage."[322](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn322 "For example, see Section 726 in Title VII, Division E, of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 ( P.L. 119-75 ).") In addition, FEHB plans are required to cover services and items recommended in HRSA-supported guidelines on women's preventive health care services.[323](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn323 "See \" Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Contraceptive Services? \" and OPM, \" Contraceptive Coverage in the FEHB Program, \" Letter Number 2022-05, March 15, 2022, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2022/2022-05.pdf ; OPM, \" Contraception,\" Letter Number 2022-17, August 19, 2022, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2022/2022-17.pdf .") Under this requirement, FEHB plans must cover, without cost sharing, at least one form of contraception in each of the categories listed in the HRSA-supported Women's Preventive Services Guidelines.[324](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn324 "HRSA, Women's Preventive Services Guidelines , page last reviewed December 202 5 , https://www.hrsa.gov/womens-guidelines , and OPM, \"Consolidated Pharmacy Benefits Guidance for the FEHB Program,\" Letter Number 2025-07, April 25, 2025, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2025/2025-07.pdf .") FEHB plans also are required to cover, without cost sharing, any contraceptive services and FDA-approved, -cleared, or -granted products that are determined to be medically appropriate, regardless of whether those services or products are specifically identified in the categories listed in the HRSA-supported guidelines. FEHB plans also must make available an exceptions process so that enrollees can get coverage of medically necessary services or items without cost sharing.[325](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn325 "OPM, \"Contraceptive Drugs and Drug-Led Devices; Member and Provider Education,\" Letter Number 2024 - 03 , January 30 , 20 24 , at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2024/2024-03.pdf (h ereinafter, OPM, \"Contraceptive Drugs and Drug-Led Devices; Member and Provider Educatio n \" ).") Outside of applicable requirements, benefits and cost sharing vary among FEHB plans.

FEHB plans are allowed to impose "reasonable medical management" coverage limitations, though OPM strongly encourages FEHB plans to follow the "therapeutic equivalence" approach to coverage, outlined in guidance.[326](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn326 "OPM, \"Contraceptive Drugs and Drug-Led Devices; Member and Provider Education.\"") Under this approach, a FEHB plan's medical management technique would be considered reasonable if the plan covers "all FDA-approved contraceptive drugs and drug-led devices without cost-sharing, other than those where there is at least one therapeutic equivalent drug or drug-led device that is covered without cost sharing."[327](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn327 "OPM would consider a contraceptive to be therapeutically equivalent to another contraceptive \"if the drug products or drug-led devices are identified as therapeutic equivalents [in the FDA's Orange Book].\" OPM, \"Contraceptive Drugs and Drug-Led Devices; Member and Provider Education.\"") FEHB plans also would need to provide an exceptions process for therapeutic equivalents under this approach.

OPM encourages FEHB plans to recognize the "Dispense as Written" function as the prescriber's expression of medical necessity, which would result in the drug or drug-led device being covered without cost-sharing.[328](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn328 "OPM, \"Contraceptive Drugs and Drug-Led Devices; Member and Provider Education.\"") OPM also requires FEHB plans to cover over-the-counter emergency contraceptive products without cost-sharing when they are prescribed for an individual, and encourages FEHB plans to cover such products without cost-sharing when they are purchased without a prescription.[329](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn329 "OPM, \"Contraception,\" Letter Number 2022 - 17 , August 19 , 20 22 , at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2022/2022-17.pdf .")

Do FEHB Plans Cover Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

As a result of Hyde-Amendment-like language often included in the Financial Services and General Government (FSGG) Appropriations Act, FEHB plans are prevented from covering abortion services except in cases of rape, incest, or endangerment of a mother's life.[330](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn330 "For example, see Section 613 and 614 in Title VI, Division E, of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 ( P.L. 119-75 ).")

Per the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978 (PDA; [P.L. 95-555](https://www.congress.gov/law/95/pub/555)), FEHB plans must provide coverage for abortion in instances "where the life of the mother would be endangered if the fetus were carried to term, or ... where medical complications have arisen from an abortion."[331](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn331 "See 42 U.S.C. §2000e(k) . In addition, statute specifies \" That nothing herein shall preclude an employer from providing abortion benefits or otherwise affect bargaining agreements in regard to abortion.\" For more information about the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978, see \" Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Maternity Services? \" The federal government is subject to the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978 in its capacity as an employer providing health cov...")

Do FEHB Plans Cover Infertility Services?

OPM has established requirements relating to coverage of infertility services under the FEHB program. Outside of applicable requirements, benefits and cost-sharing for infertility services vary among FEHB plans.

OPM requires FEHB plans to provide coverage for the following: artificial insemination (intrauterine insemination, intracervical insemination, and intravaginal insemination), drugs associated with artificial insemination procedures (when deemed medically necessary), the cost of IVF-related drugs for three cycles annually, and standard fertility preservation procedures for persons facing the possibility of iatrogenic infertility.[332](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn332 "OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits Program Call Letter (2024),\" OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits Program Call Letter (2023),\" and OPM, \"Technical Guidance and Instructions for 2025 Benefit Proposals,\" Letter Number 2024 - 06 , March 7 , 20 24 , at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2024/2024-06.pdf . OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits and Postal Service Health Benefits Programs Call Letter,\" Letter Number 2025-01, January 15, 2025, at https://w...") In addition, FEHB health maintenance organizations (HMOs) generally must satisfy any state IVF coverage requirements.[333](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn333 "OPM may allow federal preemption of state level IVF requirements , and such preemption would be negotiated annually. OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits and Postal Service Health Benefits Programs Call Letter (2026).\"")

OPM strongly encourages FEHB plans to continue and expand coverage of fertility benefits, including IVF, and to provide discounted or negotiated rates for noncovered ART procedures.[334](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn334 "OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits and Postal Service Health Benefits Programs Call Letter (2026),\" and OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits Program Call Letter (2024).\"")

In 2026, over 25 FEHB plans are offering coverage of IVF services, which is the greatest number of plans to date.[335](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn335 "Kevin Moss, \"FEHB Costs are Climbing in 2026. Here's What that Means for Feds,\" Government Executive , November 4, 2025, at https://www.govexec.com/pay-benefits/2025/11/fehb-costs-are-climbing-2026-heres-what-means-feds/409286/ .")

Do FEHB Plans Cover Maternity Services?

FEHB plans are statutorily required to cover obstetrical benefits.[336](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn336 "5 U.S.C. § 8904(a).") In addition, FEHB plans are required to cover maternity and newborn care, consistent with the EHB requirement, and are required to cover certain preventive prenatal and postpartum services (as recommended) without cost-sharing, consistent with OPM guidance and the preventive services requirement.[337](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn337 "For more information about EHB and preventive services coverage requirements related to maternity and newborn care and preventive prenatal and postpartum services, see the \" Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Maternity Services? \" section of this report.") There are multiple recommended preventive services for pregnant and postpartum individuals, including certain STI screenings, mental health and substance use screening and counseling, gestational diabetes screening and healthy weight counseling, folic acid supplements, and breastfeeding services and supplies.

Per the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978 (PDA; [P.L. 95-555](https://www.congress.gov/law/95/pub/555)), FEHB plans must cover "expenses for pregnancy-related conditions on the same basis as expenses for other medical conditions" for federal employees and their spouses.[338](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn338 "For more information on the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978, see \" Does Federal Law Require Private Health Insurance Coverage of Maternity Services? \" The federal government is subject to the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978 in its capacity as an employer providing health coverage to its employees . 42 U.S.C. §2000e-16. Also, s ee 42 U.S.C. §2000e; 29 C.F.R. §1604 Appendix. Also see \"EEOC Enforcement Guidance\" and \"EEOC Q&A . \"") In other words, FEHB coverage of pregnancy-related conditions must not be substantially different than they are for other medical conditions.

Outside of applicable requirements, benefits and cost sharing vary among FEHB plans, including coverage of services provided by midwives.

OPM encourages FEHB plans to cover additional maternity services. As examples, OPM encourages FEHB plans to address mental health among pregnant women and in the postpartum year.[339](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn339 "OPM, \"Technical Guidance and Instructions for 2023 Benefit Proposals , \" Letter Number 2022 - 04 , March 16 , 20 22, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2022/2022-04.pdf (h ereinafter, OPM, \"Technical Guidance and Instructions for 2023 Benefit Proposals\" ).") OPM also encourages FEHB plans to consider expanding coverage and services in support of prenatal and postpartum care, including "childbirth education classes, group prenatal care, home visiting programs, care management for high-risk pregnancies, and self-measured blood pressure monitoring (SMBP) for individuals with hypertension."[340](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn340 "OPM, \"Technical Guidance and Instructions for 2023 Benefit Proposals.\"") For plan year 2027, OPM is encouraging FEHB plans to incentivize the use of preconception screening bundles and outreach programs for individuals interested in becoming pregnant within the next year, and plans are to ensure access to treatments for conditions adversely affecting fertility.[341](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn341 "Such screening bundles and outreach programs are to follow American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG) pre-pregnancy recommendations and USPSTF recommendations rated \"A\" or \"B.\" OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits and Postal Service Health Benefits Programs Call Letter,\" Letter Number 2026-07, March 31, 2026, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2026/2026-07.pdf (h ereinafter, OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits and Postal Service Health Benefits P...")

Do FEHB Plans Cover Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

FEHB plans are required to cover specified preventive services and, as such, must cover certain reproductive health screening and preventive services (as recommended) without cost-sharing. This includes, for example, screening and counseling for STIs/STDs; universal HIV screening; PrEP medication and related monitoring for persons at high risk of HIV acquisition; well-woman visits; breast cancer screening, genetic testing, and preventive medications such as Tamoxifen (to lower the risk of developing breast cancer among women with specified risk factors); gynecological exams, Pap smears, and cervical cancer screenings; colorectal cancer screenings; and the HPV vaccine.[342](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn342 "See footnote 321 .")

Treatments for various reproductive health diagnoses may be covered consistent with FEHB statute, the EHB requirement, and other OPM requirements.

Outside of applicable requirements, benefits and cost sharing vary among FEHB plans.

Do FEHB Plans Cover Gender-Affirming Services?

As a result of new OPM requirements for the 2026 plan year, FEHB plans generally are no longer allowed to cover gender-affirming services but must provide a case-by-case exception process for coverage of such services for individuals who are undergoing surgical and/or hormonal treatment for diagnosed gender dysphoria.[343](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn343 "The prohibition on covering hormone treatments applies only with respect to gender-affirming services. Hormone treatments may still be covered in other instances. OPM, \"Chemical and Surgical Sex-Trait Modification Services for Plan Year 2026 Proposals,\" Letter Number 2025-01b, August 15, 2025, at https://www.opm.gov/healthcare-insurance/carriers/fehb/2025/2025-01b.pdf .") OPM will require FEHB plans to remove the exception process starting in the 2027 plan year.[344](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn344 "OPM, \"Federal Employees Health Benefits and Postal Service Health Benefits Programs Call Letter (2027).\"")

OPM requires FEHB plans to cover counseling services for individuals with possible or diagnosed gender dysphoria.

Federal Agencies and Departments

Several federal agencies provide health services directly to specific service populations. These agencies, the populations they serve, and the reproductive services they provide or pay for are discussed below. Agencies are organized alphabetically by agency name.

Bureau of Prisons (BOP)

The Bureau of Prisons (BOP) within the Department of Justice (DOJ) operates the federal prison system, which includes 118 facilities across the country.[345](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn345 "U.S. Department of Justice (DOJ), Bureau of Prisons (BOP), About Our Facilities , https://www.bop.gov/about/facilities/federal_prisons.jsp .") BOP was established in 1930 to house federal prisoners, professionalize the prison service, and ensure consistent and centralized administration of the federal prison system.[346](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn346 "U.S. Department of Justice (DOJ), BOP, About the Bureau of Prisons , June 2015, p. 1.") BOP must confine any offender convicted and sentenced in a federal court to a term of imprisonment. As of the end of FY2025, there were approximately 155,300 prisoners under BOP's jurisdiction.[347](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn347 "DOJ, BOP, \"Statistics,\" https://www.bop.gov/about/statistics/population_statistics.jsp#old_pops (data on the federal prison population was accessed on May 5, 2026).") BOP provides medically necessary health care treatment to all prisoners housed in BOP-operated facilities, including medically necessary reproductive health services.[348](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn348 "DOJ, BOP, FY 2027 Performance Budget, Congressional Submission, Salaries and Expenses , p. 22 (hereinafter, \"BOP FY2027 S&E budget justification\").") Most of this treatment is provided through health care clinics operated in each BOP facility. Most clinics have examination rooms, treatment rooms, dental clinics, radiology and laboratory areas, a pharmacy, and administrative offices.[349](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn349 "U.S. Government Accountability Office (GAO), Bureau of Prisons: Better Planning and Evaluation Needed to Understand and Control Rising Inmate Health Care Costs , GAO-17-379, June 2017, p. 8 (hereinafter, \"GAO BOP rising inmate health care costs report\").") When BOP cannot provide services at a BOP facility, it transports prisoners to a community health care facility or provider (e.g., a hospital). Generally, each BOP facility maintains its own contract with health care facilities or providers and sets the rate to be paid for providing medical treatment to inmates.[350](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn350 "GAO BOP rising inmate health care costs report, p. 11.") For prisoners with acute or chronic long-term care needs that cannot be managed through in-prison clinics, BOP transfers these patients to one of its Federal Medical Centers.[351](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn351 "Examples of services provided at Federal Medical Centers include dialysis for inmates with chronic renal failure; oncology treatment (i.e., chemotherapy and radiation therapy); inpatient and forensic mental health; surgery (i.e., limited orthopedic and general surgery procedures); prosthetics and orthotics; long-term ventilator-dependent management; dementia care; and end-of-life care . BOP FY2027 S&E budget justification, p. 23.")

All prisoners serving a period of incarceration are given an intake screening within 24 hours of arriving at the facility. The screening is performed to identify

* urgent medical, oral, or mental health care needs;
* indications of acute drug or alcohol intoxication or signs of withdrawal;
* presence of transmissible skin, respiratory, or gastrointestinal infections;
* pregnancy in female prisoners;
* sensory, cognitive, or physical disabilities requiring further evaluation and potential accommodations; and
* medication needs.[352](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn352 "DOJ, BOP, Patient Care , Program Statement 6031.05, p. 35 (hereinafter \" Patient Care \").")

In addition to the intake screening, BOP conducts a comprehensive physical assessment on all new prisoners to assess, diagnose, and treat medical needs.[353](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn353 "Patient Care , p. 37.") The assessment includes a medical records review. During the assessment, BOP can order diagnostic tests and immunizations, including

* HIV testing;
* hepatitis screening;
* STD testing, if clinically indicated;
* pregnancy testing, if clinically indicated and not conducted during the intake screening;
* a measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine for women of child-bearing age who report never having the vaccine as an adult.[354](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn354 "Patient Care , p. 37.")

BOP policy requires facilities to make age-appropriate medical screening available to all prisoners.

Does BOP Provide Reproductive Health Services?

BOP provides medically necessary health care treatment to all prisoners housed in BOP-operated facilities, including medically necessary reproductive health services.[355](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn355 "BOP FY2027 S&E budget justification, p. 22.")

In addition to the intake medical examination mentioned above, BOP policy requires facilities to provide age-appropriate medical screening, which may include reproductive health screening, to all prisoners.

Does BOP Provide Contraceptive Services?

BOP policy requires medical staff to provide female prisoners with information related to birth control, if requested.[356](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn356 "DOJ, BOP, Female Offender Manual, Program Statement 5200.09 CN-1, p. 13 (hereinafter, \" Female Offender Manual. \")") Female prisoners have access to birth control while incarcerated, but it is usually limited to hormone replacement therapy.[357](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn357 "Ibid.") Birth control can be prescribed for other reasons, but only if a clinician believes it is medically appropriate and the prescription is approved by BOP's medical director.[358](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn358 "The medical director is a part of the executive staff of BOP's Health Services Division, which is responsible for overseeing the programs, operations, and delivery of health care at all BOP facilities.")

BOP does not provide sterilization to male or female prisoners except for bona fide medical indications (e.g., as the result of surgical treatment for cancer of the reproductive organs).[359](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn359 "Patient Care , p. 69.")

Does BOP Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

BOP does not directly provide abortions; however, it will permit pregnant prisoners to terminate their pregnancies, with certain conditions.[360](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn360 "Traditionally, as a part of the annual Commerce, Justice, Science, and Related Agencies Appropriations Act, Congress places limitations on how BOP can use its funding to provide abortion services to prisoners. For example, the Commerce, Justice, Science, and Related Agencies Appropriations Act, 2026 (Division A of P.L. 119-74 ), states that \"none of the funds appropriated by [Title II of Division A] shall be available to pay for an abortion, except where the life of the mother would...") Wardens are required to offer pregnant prisoners medical, religious, and social counseling to help them decide whether to carry a pregnancy to term. If a prisoner chooses to terminate the pregnancy, the prisoner is required to sign a statement to that effect.[361](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn361 "Female Offender Manual , p. 18.") Upon receipt of the signed statement, the facility's clinical director arranges for an abortion.[362](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn362 "Clinical directors are responsible for clinical care provided at each BOP facility. The clinical director provides clinical oversight of health care services and is responsible for all health care delivered.") BOP assumes the cost of the procedure only when the mother's life is endangered by carrying the pregnancy to term or in the case of rape or incest.[363](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn363 "Female Offender Manual , p. 18.") In all other cases, the prisoner must arrange payment for the procedure.[364](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn364 "Ibid.") All expenses related to the procedure, including transportation and escort costs, must be covered by the prisoner, the prisoner's family, or from other "appropriate non-federal government funded sources approved by the Warden."[365](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn365 "Ibid.")

Does BOP Provide Infertility Services?

BOP's policies regarding prisoner health care and regarding health care for female prisoners, specifically, do not address infertility services.

Does BOP Provide Maternity Services?

BOP has several programs that provide parenting assistance.[366](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn366 "For a description of BOP's national parenting from prisons program, see DOJ, BOP, First Step Act Approved Programs Guide , March 2026, p. 22.") With regard to maternity services, the most relevant program is the Mothers and Infants Nurturing Together (MINT) program.

MINT is a community-based residential program where pregnant prisoners are allowed to give birth and spend time bonding with their newborn outside of a secure facility. To be eligible for the MINT program, prisoners must be pregnant when they begin their period of incarceration, must have an expected delivery date prior to their scheduled release date, must have less than five years of incarceration remaining, must be eligible for halfway house placement, and must assume financial responsibility for child care.[367](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn367 "DOJ, Office of the Inspector General, Review of the Federal Bureau of Prisons' Management of Its Female Inmate Population , Evaluation and Inspections Division 18-05, September 2018, p. 9.") Prisoners in the MINT program are transferred to a Residential Reentry Center (RRC) (BOP's term for a halfway house) during the last two months of pregnancy, and they are allowed to stay at the RRC for at least three months, though BOP policy recommends a minimum of six months.[368](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn368 "Female Offender Manual , p. 20.") Once they complete the program, prisoners are returned to their designated facility to serve the remainder of their sentences.

Does BOP Provide Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

All federal prisoners receive a medical screening upon intake at a BOP facility, which includes ordering appropriate laboratory and diagnostic tests, if medically indicated. Such tests include age-appropriate preventive health examinations. BOP provides medically necessary treatment, including treatment for reproductive health, to all federal prisoners.[369](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn369 "The GAO notes, \"Multiple U.S. courts over the years have determined that inmates have a constitutional right to adequate medical and mental health care.\" GAO BOP rising inmate health care costs report, p. 2.") BOP is responsible for providing medically necessary care in a manner consistent with the standards of care for nonprisoners.[370](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn370 "GAO BOP rising inmate health care costs report, p. 8.")

In addition, BOP policy requires facilities to make age-appropriate medical screening available to all prisoners.

In general, BOP tests for STIs when there is a clinical indication that a prisoner has an STI.[371](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn371 "DOJ, BOP, Infectious Disease Management , Program Statement 6190.04, p. 11 (hereinafter, \" Infectious Disease Management \").") BOP has special procedures related to testing for HIV. If a prisoner who is sentenced to six months or more has risk factors for HIV, or if there is a clinical indication that the prisoner has HIV, then HIV testing is mandatory.[372](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn372 "Infectious Disease Management , p. 5.") HIV testing is also mandatory when there is a well-founded belief that a prisoner has transmitted HIV to BOP employees or to other non-BOP employees working in the facility.[373](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn373 "Ibid.") In addition, BOP conducts HIV testing, as necessary, to collect information on the prevalence of HIV in the prison population (i.e., surveillance testing). BOP provides HIV testing to prisoners upon request; such tests are limited to one per 12-month period, unless BOP determines that additional testing is warranted.[374](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn374 "Infectious Disease Management , p. 6.") BOP provides pre- and post-test counseling to all prisoners who are tested for HIV, regardless of the test results.[375](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn375 "Ibid.")

Does BOP Provide Gender-Affirming Services?

In response to the Trump Administration's EO 14168, "Defending Women from Gender Ideology Extremism and Restoring Biological Truth to the Federal Government," BOP rescinded its policy to provide gender-affirming care treatment to transgender prisoners in February 2025.[376](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn376 "DOJ, BOP, Transgender Offender Manual , Program Statement 5200.08.") BOP then issued several new policies related to the treatment of prisoners with gender dysphoria (GD),[377](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn377 "See text box in \" What Are Gender-Affirming Services? \" section of this report for terms and definitions related to gender-affirming care .") including a new policy regarding treatments for prisoners with GD in February 2026.[378](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn378 "DOJ, BOP, Management of Inmates with Gender Dysphoria , Program Statement 5260.01.") A group of inmates filed a lawsuit in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia to challenge the BOP policies banning gender-affirming care. At the time of publication of this report, BOP's policies have been temporarily paused from going into effect, including the February 2026 policy, as a result of that litigation, which remains ongoing.[379](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn379 "Kingdom v. Trump, No. 25-691, 2026 WL 2690647 (D.D.C. Aug. 26, 2026); appeal filed , Kingdom v. Trump , No. 26-5310 (D.C. Cir. Aug. 31, 2026).")

BOP's February 2026 policy states that EO 14168 prohibits BOP from using appropriated funds to provide any medical procedure, treatment, or drug for the purpose of conforming a prisoner's appearance to that of the opposite sex to the extent consistent with applicable law.[380](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn380 "Ibid., pp. 5-6.") The policy states that BOP will comply with the EO's mandate unless prohibited by a court injunction or order.[381](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn381 "Ibid., p. 6.") While BOP will not provide sex trait modification surgery to prisoners who have been diagnosed with GD, BOP will provide necessary medical care to prisoners who had sex trait modification surgery prior to incarceration to address any complications or resulting conditions.[382](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn382 "Ibid., p. 7.")

BOP's policy states that prisoners who have not started hormone therapy for GD will not be prescribed hormones to address GD. For prisoners who were on hormone therapy before incarceration, prison medical staff are required to develop plans to taper most prisoners off hormone therapy. The policy notes that "for inmates who (1) are post sex trait modification surgery or (2) have been receiving hormones to address GD for an extended period of time and develop severe physiological and psychological withdrawal effects from tapering, it may not be appropriate in all cases for the initial tapering plan to include cessation of hormones. But tapering plans should be reevaluated regularly with respect to cessation of hormones, including during the inmate's chronic care clinic appointments."[383](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn383 "Ibid., p. 8.")

The February 2026 policy requires prison clinicians to develop individualized treatment plans for prisoners who have been diagnosed with GD. The treatment plans developed for prisoners with a GD diagnosis are required to focus on treating medical and psychological comorbidities before treatment for GD proceeds.[384](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn384 "Ibid., p. 6.") The policy directs BOP clinicians to treat comorbidities through psychotherapy, psychotropic medication, or other appropriate medically accepted interventions, with a priority on psychotherapy.[385](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn385 "Ibid.") Treatment specifically for GD can proceed after comorbidities are resolved and ruled out as potential causes of the GD diagnosis.[386](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn386 "Ibid.") Treatment for GD is required to focus on "managing the psychological distress/dysphoria, assisting with adjustment to incarceration, community re-entry, and strengthening resilience."[387](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn387 "Ibid.")

Department of Defense (DOD)

DOD (which is "using a secondary Department of War designation," under Executive Order 14347, dated September 5, 2025) administers a statutory health benefit (under Title 10, Chapter 55, of the *U.S. Code*) through the Military Health System (MHS).[388](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn388 "See CRS In Focus IF10530, Defense Primer: Military Health System , by Bryce H. P. Mendez .") The MHS offers health care benefits and services through its TRICARE program to approximately 9.5 million beneficiaries, comprising members and retirees of the uniformed services and their family members.[389](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn389 "Defense Health Agency, About the Military Health System , https://www.health.mil/About-MHS . The term uniformed services includes the Armed Forces (Army, Navy, Marine Corps, Air Force, Space Force, and Coast Guard), the commissioned corps of the Public Health Service, and the commissioned corps of the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. CRS In Focus IF10530, Defense Primer: Military Health System .") TRICARE offers a range of health care services, including reproductive health services, in military hospitals and clinics (also known as military treatment facilities, or MTFs) and from participating civilian health care providers. With the exception of active duty servicemembers, beneficiaries are subject to certain [cost-sharing requirements](https://tricare.mil/Costs/Compare) based on beneficiary category, health plan or benefit program, and the sponsor's initial enlistment or appointment date to military service.[390](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn390 "For more on TRICARE's cost-sharing features, see CRS Report R45399, Military Medical Care: Frequently Asked Questions (\"Question 7 . What are the Different TRICARE Plans?\"). A sponsor refers to a servicemember or military retiree. For more on sponsors and family members, see https://www.tricare.mil/Plans/Eligibility .")

The United States Coast Guard (USCG) is a uniformed service that operates primarily under the authority of the Department of Homeland Security. The Coast Guard delivers a limited range of outpatient medical and dental care in its own facilities. Patients with medical needs exceeding a USCG clinic's capabilities may be referred or medically evacuated to a DOD MTF or civilian medical facility participating in TRICARE.[391](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn391 "U.S. Government Accountability Office, Coast Guard Health Care: Additional Actions Could Help Ensure Beneficiaries' Access , GAO-23-105574, April 2023, pp. 7-10. For additional information about the TRICARE program, see th e \" Department of Defense (DOD) \" section of this report.")

Does DOD Provide Reproductive Health Services?

By law, DOD is required to offer certain *primary and preventive health services* to all active duty servicemembers and retirees.[392](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn392 "The Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act (ACA; P.L. 111-148 ) requires most insurance programs and plans to cover women's preventive health services. Those requirements do not apply to the TRICARE program; however, 10 U.S.C. §1074d require s TRICARE to cover similar preventive health services. For more information on the ACA's requirements, see the \" Overview: Coverage of Certain Preventive Services Without Cost Sharing \" section of this report.") Eligible family members of servicemembers and retirees may also access these services. Primary and preventive health services are generally offered at no cost to beneficiaries; however, some services may be subject to certain cost-sharing requirements.

Does DOD Provide Contraceptive Services?

DOD offers contraceptive services, including the following:

* "Medical contraceptives," including "injections, placement, and removal of intrauterine devices, implantable rods, and diaphragm measurements and fittings" as part of "Well Woman Examinations."[393](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn393 "See Chapter 7, Section 2.1, paragraph 1.1.4, of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated June 4, 2026 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/FileName/C7S2_1 .")
* Prescription contraceptives, including diaphragms, and emergency contraceptives.[394](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn394 "See Chapter 7, Section 2. 3 , paragraph 1. 2. 1 of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M, updated March 17, 2026 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C7S2_3 .")
* Surgical sterilization.[395](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn395 "See Chapter 7, Section 2.3, paragraph 1.1 of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M, updated March 17, 2026 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C7S2_3 .")

DOD does not cover

* condoms,
* spermicides, or
* reversal of surgical sterilization, unless medically necessary.[396](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn396 "See Chapter 7, Section 2.3 of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated March 17, 2026 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C7S2_3 .")

Deployed servicemembers may also receive prescribed contraceptives (up to 180-day supply) prior to their departure and while in-theater (90-day supply increments) when subscribed to the Deployed Prescription Program (DPP).[397](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn397 "The Deployed Prescription Program (DPP) delivers prescription medications to deployed servicemembers via the military mail system (i.e., Army Post Office, Fleet Post Office). DOD civil service employees and DOD contractors without other health insurance are also eligible for DPP. For more information on the DPP, see https://tricare.mil/dpp .") In-theater military health care providers may issue new or renewal prescriptions that would be filled through the DPP.

Does DOD Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

Title 10, Section 1093, of the *U.S. Code* states, "Funds available to the Department of Defense may not be used to perform abortions except where the life of the mother would be endangered if the fetus were carried to term or in a case in which the pregnancy is the result of an act of rape or incest."[398](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn398 "10 U.S.C. §1093. Part §199.4(e)(2) of Title 32 , Code of Federal Regulations , further specifies that \"abortions performed for suspected or confirmed fetal abnormality (e.g., ancephalic) or for mental health reasons (e.g., threatened suicide) do not fall within the exceptions\" permitted in statute.") Abortion counseling, referral, preparation, and follow-up care are covered for covered abortions, but not for noncovered abortions.[399](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn399 "See Chapter 4, Section 18.3 of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated July 14, 2021 , at https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/FileName/C4S18_3 .")

Does DOD Provide Infertility Services?[400](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn400 "See CRS In Focus IF11504, Infertility in the Military , coordinated by Michael J. Vassalotti .")

DOD offers certain counseling and treatment services for infertility, when medically necessary and combined with natural conception, including

* correction of physical causes of infertility;[401](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn401 "See Chapter 4, Section 15.1 paragraph 4.7 , and Section 17.1 paragraph 3.1, of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated March 28, 2025 at https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C4TOC .")
* erectile dysfunction if it has a physical cause;[402](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn402 "See Chapter 4, Section 15.1 paragraph s 4 .1 – 4.6.5. of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M, updated March 28, 2025 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C4TOC .") and
* diagnostic services (e.g., semen analysis, hormone evaluation, chromosomal studies, immunologic studies, special and sperm function tests, and bacteriologic investigation).[403](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn403 "See Chapter 4, Section 15.1 paragraph 4.7 , and Section 17.1 paragraph 3.1 , of the of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated March 28, 2025 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C4TOC . For more on DOD infertility services, see CRS In Focus IF11504, Infertility in the Military .")

In general, DOD does not offer or cover other types of infertility or ART services under the TRICARE program.[404](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn404 "For more on assisted reproductive technologies (ART), see https://tricare.mil/CoveredServices/IsItCovered/AssistedReproductiveServices . Noncoital refers to sexual or reproductive activities that do not involve heterosexual intercourse.") Excluded services include artificial insemination, including costs related to donors or sperm banks, reversal of tubal ligation or vasectomy (unless medically necessary), erectile dysfunction resulting from psychological causes, and *noncoital* reproductive procedures (e.g., IVF, gamete or zygote intrafallopian transfer, tubal embryo transfer).[405](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn405 "See Chapter 4, Sections 15.1 and 17.1 , and Chapter 7, Section 2.3 , of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated March 17, 2026 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C4TOC .")

In certain instances, DOD may pay for limited ART services for seriously or severely ill or injured active duty servicemembers and their spouses, unmarried partners, or third-party gestational carriers with a qualifying diagnosis.[406](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn406 "10 U.S.C. §1074(c) authorizes DOD to provide extended care benefits to servicemembers who \"incur a serious injury or illness on active duty.\"") Limited ART services include sperm or egg retrieval; IVF; and artificial insemination.[407](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn407 "Defense Health Agency, Assisted Reproductive Technology Services , https://tricare.mil/CoveredServices/IsItCovered/AssistedReproductiveServices .") Eight DOD hospitals offer ART services to eligible servicemembers and their spouses:

* Madigan Army Medical Center (Joint Base Lewis-McChord, WA);
* Naval Medical Center Portsmouth (Portsmouth, VA);
* Naval Medical Center San Diego (San Diego, CA);
* Brooke Army Medical Center (Fort Sam Houston, TX);
* Tripler Army Medical Center (Honolulu, HI);
* Walter Reed National Military Medical Center (Bethesda, MD);
* Wright Patterson Medical Center/88th Medical Group (Greene County, OH); and
* Womack Army Medical Center (Fayetteville, NC).[408](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn408 "Defense Health Agency, Assisted Reproductive Technology Services.")

Per the Defense Health Agency, these services "are available on a first-come, first-served basis at a greatly reduced cost."[409](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn409 "Defense Health Agency, Assisted Reproductive Technology Services.")

Does DOD Provide Maternity Services?

DOD offers and pays for medically necessary maternity care, including "care and treatment related to conception, delivery, abortion,[410](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn410 "DOD will pay for abortions only in limited circumstances. For more information , see \" Does DOD Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling? \" in this report.") including prenatal and postnatal care (generally through the sixth post-delivery week), and also including treatment of the complications of pregnancy."[411](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn411 "32 C.F.R. § § 199.2 and 199.4 (e)(16) .") Maternity care for pregnancies resulting from noncoital reproductive procedures or surrogacy is also offered.[412](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn412 "For more on TRICARE coverage of maternity care, see Chapter 4, Section 18.1 of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M, April 2021 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C4TOC .") Labor doula services may also be covered under the TRICARE Childbirth and Breastfeeding Support Demonstration (CBSD) program for TRICARE Prime, TRICARE Prime Remote and TRICARE Select enrollees. The CBSD program operates until December 31, 2026. It expanded overseas on January 1, 2025.[413](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn413 "For more on this program, see Defense Health Agency, TRICARE Childbirth and Breastfeeding Support Demonstration , https://tricare.mil/Plans/SpecialPrograms/CBSD .")

Does DOD Provide Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

DOD offers a range of clinical preventive services, including certain reproductive health screening and preventive services.[414](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn414 "For more on DOD's provision of clinical preventive services, see Chapter 7, Sections 2.1 and 2.2 , of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated July 31, 2025 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C7TOC .") These services include, but are not limited to, screening and counseling for breast, cervical, colon, testicular, and prostate cancers; STIs or STDs; HIV screening; and physical or psychological conditions resulting from an act of violence.[415](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn415 "Chapter 7, Sections 2.1 and 2.2 , of the TRICARE Policy Manual 6010.63-M , updated July 31, 2025 , https://manuals.dha.mil/View-Publication/TPT5/Revision/58/FileName/C7TOC .") DOD also offers medically necessary treatment or therapy options to eligible beneficiaries with a reproductive health issue identified during a clinical screening.[416](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn416 "For more on DOD administered/sponsored medically necessary treatment or therapy options, see 32 C.F.R. §199.4.")

Does DOD Provide Gender-Affirming Services?

DOD may offer or pay for beneficiaries aged 19 or older to receive mental health care and counseling related to gender dysphoria. Apart from diagnostic consultations and mental health counseling, other gender dysphoria care (such as cross-sex hormone therapy) is to be referred to the private sector. Gender-affirming surgeries (also called sex reassignment surgeries) are excluded from TRICARE private sector coverage. Military treatment facility staff may not make referrals for noncovered care. Military Departments may request case-by-case exceptions from the Assistant Secretary of Defense for Health Affairs for nonsurgical treatment if required to protect the health of a service member.[417](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn417 "Department of Defense, Additional Guidance on Treatment for Gender Dysphoria , May 9, 2025, https://health.mil/Reference-Center/Policies/2025/05/09/Additional-Guidance-on-Treatment-of-Gender-Dysphoria .")

U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) Noncitizen Detention

The Department of Homeland Security's (DHS's) Immigration and Customs Enforcement's (ICE) stated mission "is to protect America from the cross-border crime and illegal immigration that threaten national security and public safety."[418](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn418 "Department of Homeland Security (DHS), Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) , at http://www.dhs.gov/  topic/  immigration-  and-  customs-  enforcement .") ICE's Enforcement and Removal Operations (ERO) is responsible for immigration enforcement in the interior of the United States, including managing and overseeing the immigrant detention system.[419](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn419 "The law provides ICE with broad authority to detain noncitizens while awaiting a determination of whether they should be removed from the United States, and mandates that certain categories of noncitizens are subject to mandatory detention (e.g., when the noncitizen is removable on account of certain criminal or terrorist activity). See 8 U.S.C. §§1225, 1226, 1226a, 1231, and 1357. For more information, see CRS In Focus IF11343, The Law of Immigration Detention: A Brief Introduction .")

ICE detention standards were originally developed in 2000 and have been updated several times, resulting in various sets of standards that vary in terms of scope and rigor. Although there are different sets of standards, all facilities housing noncitizen detainees must generally comply with one of the sets of ICE detention standards, including health care standards.[420](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn420 "For more information, see CRS In Focus IF12623, Medical Care Standards in Immigrant Detention Facilities .") Contracts or agreements between ICE and detention facilities specify which set of standards facilities are required to follow.[421](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn421 "ICE owns and operates a relatively small number of its own facilities, and it has arrangements through contracts with private companies that operate the majority of immigration detention facilities. In addition, immigrant detention facilities owned by state or local governments or private entities operate through intergovernmental agreements. (GAO, ICE Should Enhance Its Use of Facility Oversight Data and Management of Detainee Complaints , 20-596, August 2020, pp. 6-7.)")

Two sets of detention standards are applied at facilities that house the majority of the adult detained population: the 2011 Performance-Based National Detention Standards (PBNDS)[422](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn422 "The 2011 Performance-Based National Detention Standards (PBNDS) was revised in 2016 to meet detention standards consistent with federal legal and regulatory requirements, as well as prior ICE policies and policy statements. The 2011 PBNDS is an updated version of the 2008 PBNDS; some facilities have contracts agreeing to adhere to the 2008 version. (GAO, ICE Should Enhance Its Use of Facility Oversight Data and Management of Detainee Complaints , 20-596, August 2020.)") and the 2026 National Detention Standards (NDS).[423](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn423 "There have been multiple versions of the NDS over the years: 2026, 2025, 2019, and 2000 . In the 2025 revision, ICE explained that \"references to gender have been replaced with sex throughout the standards\" to align with the January 2025 Executive Order 14168, \"Defending Women from Gender Ideology Extremism and Restoring Biological Truth to The Federal Government\" (ICE, \"National Detention Standards,\" revised 2026 , \" at https://www.ice.gov/doclib/detention-standards/2026/nds2026.pdf ( her...") The 2011 PBNDS and 2026 NDS provide identical guidance on certain standards, including many health care standards. In the frequently asked questions section that follows, the two sets of standards provide the same guidance unless otherwise noted. The following sections present these standards as enumerated in ICE guidance. There are multiple DHS Office of Inspector General (OIG) and GAO reports that identify inadequate compliance with these standards.[424](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn424 "For example, see GAO, Immigration Detention: DHS Should Define Goals and Measures to Assess Facility Inspection Programs , GAO- 25-107580 , May 2025; GAO, Immigration Detention: ICE Can Improve Oversight and Management , GAO-23-106350, January 2023; GAO, Immigrant Detention: ICE Needs to Strengthen Oversight of Informed Consent for Medical Care , GAO -23-105196, October 2022; GAO, Immigrant Detention: ICE Should Enhance Its Use of Facility Oversight Data and Management of Detainee Com...")

Does ICE Provide Reproductive Health Services?

ICE provides certain reproductive health services to noncitizens in detention. Detained noncitizens are entitled to medical care per Title 42, Section 249, of the *U.S. Code* and Title 42, Section 34.7(a), of the *Code of Federal Regulations*. Medical care standards are outlined in all of ICE's detention standards; those related to reproductive health services are discussed in the sections below.

Does ICE Provide Contraceptive Services?

According to ICE guidance, detainees are entitled to impartial family planning and contraceptive consultations with medical personnel. Detainees may be prescribed contraception.[425](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn425 "ICE, Performance-Based National Detention Standards 2011 (hereinafter, \"2011 PBNDS\") ; \"4.4 Medical Care (Women)\"; 2026 NDS, \"4.3 Medical Care.\"")

Does ICE Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

According to ICE detention standards, ICE provides abortion services in certain circumstances. ICE assumes the cost of terminating the pregnancy "if the life of the mother would be endangered by carrying a fetus to term, or in the case of rape or incest."[426](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn426 "2011 PBDNS, \"4.4 Medical Care (Women)\" and 2026 NDS, \"4.3 Medical Care.\"") In all other circumstances, the detainee bears the cost of terminating the pregnancy. In all instances, ICE arranges transportation to the medical appointment at no cost to the detainee and, if requested, to religious or social counseling.

Does ICE Provide Infertility Services?

ICE detention standards are silent on the provision of infertility services. In October 2020, CRS confirmed with ICE that it "does not generally provide infertility services."[427](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn427 "CRS communication with ICE on October 21, 2020.")

Does ICE Provide Maternity Services?

In July 2021, ICE announced a new directive that specifies that immigrants who are pregnant, nursing, or postpartum will not be detained while they wait for immigration court proceedings, unless they are subject to mandatory detention or there are exceptional circumstances.[428](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn428 "ICE, \"Directive: Identification and Monitoring of Pregnant, Postpartum, or Nursing Individuals,\" https://www.ice.gov/directive-identification-and-monitoring-pregnant-postpartum-or-nursing-individuals , updated July 9, 2021 .") As of February 2026, according to news reports, "the administration has given the courts conflicting, unclear answers about whether it is following" this policy or whether it has been rescinded.[429](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn429 "Kyle Cheney, \" Judges decry treatment of nursing and pregnant detainees in ICE custody ,\" Politico, February 18, 2026.")

For those who are detained, ICE detention standards outline maternity services that are to be provided to detainees. ICE considers pregnant detainees one of its vulnerable populations. According to ICE guidance, "pregnant detainees shall have access to prenatal and specialized care, and comprehensive counseling on topics including, but not limited to, nutrition, exercise, complications of pregnancy, prenatal vitamins, labor and delivery, postpartum care, lactation, family planning, abortion services and parenting skills."[430](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn430 "2011 PBDNS, \"4.4 Medical Care (Women)\" and 20 26 NDS \"4.3 Medical Care.\"") In addition, ICE standards state that ICE accommodates a pregnant individual's special needs, such as an additional pillow or a special diet, as identified by a medical professional. Finally, if a health care practitioner identifies pregnant detainees as being high risk, ICE standards state that those detainees "shall be referred to a physician specializing in high-risk pregnancies."[431](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn431 "Ibid.")

Does ICE Provide Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

According to ICE detention standards, all detainees are to be provided "comprehensive, routine and preventive health care, as medically indicated."[432](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn432 "2011 PBDNS, \"4.3 Medical Care\" and 20 26 NDS \"4.3 Medical Care.\"") The 2011 PBNDS guidance states that "detainees shall have access to a continuum of health care services, including screening, prevention, health education, diagnosis and treatment."[433](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn433 "2011 PBDNS, \"4.3 Medical Care.\"") Similarly, the 2025 NDS guidance states that "all detainees shall have access to appropriate medical, dental, and mental health care, including emergency services."[434](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn434 "20 26 NDS \"4.3 Medical Care.\"")

For detained women, ICE standards state that ICE offers routine preventive screening services, such as pelvic and breast examinations, Pap smears, testing for STIs, and mammograms.

In addition, according to ICE standards, the initial health assessment for women entering immigration detention collects information regarding

* pregnancy testing for detainees aged 18-56 and documented results;
* if the detainee is currently nursing (breastfeeding);
* use of contraception;
* reproductive history (number of pregnancies, number of live births, number of spontaneous/elective abortions, pregnancy complications, etc.);
* menstrual cycle;
* history of breast and gynecological problems;
* family history of breast and gynecological problems; and
* any history of physical or sexual victimization and when the incident occurred.[435](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn435 "2011 PBDNS, \"4.4 Medical Care (Women)\" and 2019 NDS \"4.3 Medical Care.\"")

Although ICE detention standards are silent on men's reproductive health screening and preventive services specifically, according to correspondence with CRS, "ICE offers routine age- and gender-appropriate preventive health services and examinations for all male and female detainees annually. Testing for STIs is available upon detainee request and as clinically indicated."[436](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn436 "CRS communication with ICE on October 21, 2020.")

Does ICE Provide Gender-Affirming Services?

ICE might provide gender-affirming services, depending on which set of standards the facility follows.[437](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn437 "See the section \" U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) Noncitizen Detention \" for a discussion of the different sets of detention standards.") The 2011 PBNDS, the 2019 NDS, and the 2026 NDS differ in terms of their guidance about transgender detainees' health care.

Per the 2011 PBNDS guidance, transgender detainees have access to the hormone therapy they were receiving prior to being detained. Furthermore, "all transgender detainees shall have access to mental health care, and other transgender-related health care and medication based on medical need."[438](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn438 "2011 PBDNS, \"4.3 Medical Care\"") The guidance also states that their "treatment shall follow accepted guidelines regarding medically necessary transition-related care," though it does not reference specific guidelines.

The 2019 NDS guidance had stated that the detention facility and ICE/ERO should coordinate care "based on [the] medical needs" of self-identified transgender detainees.[439](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn439 "2019 NDS, \"4.3 Medical Care.\"") The 2026 NDS guidance does not include any references to transgender detainees.

Indian Health Service (IHS)

IHS provides health care directly or provides funds for Indian tribes or tribal organizations to operate health care facilities.[440](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn440 "The Indian Health Service (IHS) also provides grants to Urban Indian Organizations (UIOs) that operate smaller health facilities in urban areas. These facilities vary in terms of the services available; some provide comprehensive services, while others provide information and referral services. The following discussion does not include UIOs because as grantees they have more flexibility in the services they provide. Outside of the grants they receive, UIOs are generally not eligible to receive...") It provides services free of charge to approximately 2.8 million eligible American Indians and Alaska Natives in 37 states.[441](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn441 "HHS, IHS, Fiscal Year 2027 Indian Health Service Justification of Estimates for Appropriations Committees , https://www.ihs.gov/sites/ofa/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/IHS%20Draft%20CJ%20FY%202027.pdf, pp. CJ-1; IHS FY2027 Budget Justification.") IHS does not have a standard medical benefit that includes or excludes certain services.[442](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn442 "CRS Report R43330, The Indian Health Service (IHS): An Overview .")
