The agency generally focuses on primary and preventive services and does so through a network of more than 600 facilities, which include hospitals (44), health centers, (391), and small health stations (102). Other facility types include school health centers (19), youth regional treatment centers (13), and Alaska village clinics (147).[443](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn443 "IHS FY2027 Budget Justification, pp. CJ-25-26 and CJ-166.")

Does IHS Provide Reproductive Health Services?

IHS does not have a standard medical benefit that includes or excludes certain services, but some facilities provide reproductive health services and maternity care services. Among other services, IHS provides specific women's health services, such as mammograms and other preventive services.

Specific reproductive health services may or may not be available at IHS because it has limited funding and some facilities serve small populations. As such, not all facilities offer reproductive health services, and the services available vary. In addition, IHS's ability to pay for services outside of its system is limited. IHS receives annual appropriations for its purchased referred care (PRC) program,[444](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn444 "HHS, IHS, \"Purchased Referred Care,\" https://www.ihs.gov/prc/  . In FY2026, the program received an appropriation of $996,755,000. IHS's FY2026 appropriation was provided in FY2025 appropriations law ( P.L. 119-4 ) as an advance appropriation.") which enables the agency to pay for outside services. PRC funds are limited and may not be available later in any given fiscal year. IHS-operated facilities are required to report information on care deferred or denied. In FY2025, these facilities reported that they denied or deferred close to 65,000 services for eligible American Indians and Alaska Natives in FY2025 because of these funding limitations.[445](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn445 "IHS FY2027 Budget Justification, pp. CJ-78.") Moreover, only a subset of the IHS population is eligible for PRC, as eligibility is restricted to IHS-eligible individuals who live in certain geographic areas. PRC funds are authorized only for services in instances when the PRC-eligible individual does not have an alternate resource (e.g., Medicaid).

PRC pays for services, to the extent that funds are available, based on medical priorities. In 2025, IHS revised these priorities to reflect a care continuum, placing its priorities into four categories (this replaced an earlier five-priority level system). One of the new specified priority level categories is "Category C: Reproductive and Maternal/Child Health Services." Within each of these categories, there are services that are "deemed core, intermediate, and elective representing priority 1, priority 2, and priority 3 respectively."[446](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn446 "For more information, see HHS, IHS, \"Purchased Referred Care, Requirements: Priorities of Care,\" https://www.ihs.gov/prc/medical-priorities/.") The fourth priority level is excluded services; these are excluded because they are experimental or cosmetic. PRC funds cannot be used to pay for these services.[447](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn447 "HHS, IHS, \"Indian Health Service Medical Priority Levels,\" February 6, 2025, https://www.ihs.gov/sites/prc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/PRC_Medical_Priorities.pdf , pp. 2-3.")

[**Table 3**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref241234740) details the IHS priority levels for Reproductive & Maternal/Child Health Services. The new guidelines categorize infertility services as elective/justifiable. IHS describes this priority level as "clinically justifiable services intended to enhance health and well-being."[448](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn448 "HHS, IHS, \"Indian Health Service Medical Priority Levels,\" February 6, 2025, https://www.ihs.gov/sites/prc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/PRC_Medical_Priorities.pdf .") Prior guidance has considered these services to be experimental and had excluded them.[449](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn449 "Information on IHS's prior priority levels is available at https://www.ihs.gov/sites/prc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/IHSMedicalPrioritiesRolesAndResponsibilities.docx .")

Table 3. IHS Priority Levels: Reproductive & Maternal/Child Health Services (Category C)

(2025)

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| Priority Level | Service |
| Core (Priority 1): Essential | Prenatal Care; Labor and Delivery; Postpartum and Newborn Care; Sexual Assault Evaluation; Obstetric/Gynecologic Medical/Surgical Consultation, Acute Colposcopy |
| Intermediate (Priority 2): Necessary | Breastfeeding Equipment/Counseling; Obstetric/Gynecologic Medical/Surgical Consultation, Chronic; Reproductive/Family Planning Services; Long-Acting Reversible Contraception; Bilateral Tubal Ligation; Vasectomy |
| Elective (Priority 3): Justifiable | Infertility Services |

**Source:** CRS adaptation of IHS, "Indian Health Service Priority Levels, February 6, 2025, <https://www.ihs.gov/sites/prc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/PRC_Medical_Priorities.pdf>, p. 6.

**Notes:** This table includes services that are discussed in this report and not the universe of reproductive and maternal/child health services. Screening, such as routine mammograms, are included under the "Preventive and Rehabilitative Services" category and are considered to be essential services. This category also includes child health services, which are not included in this table because they are beyond the scope of this report.

Reproductive health services are generally included within Priorities 1 and 2 of Category A. Priority Level 1 includes services that are essential, including maternity services such as delivery and prenatal care. Family planning services are included as Priority 2—necessary. Some reproductive health services—such as screening mammography and services to prevent and treat STIs—are included in either the "Category A: Preventive and Rehabilitative Services" or as "Category B: Medical, Dental, Vision and Surgical Services" PRC categories. Category A includes "services designated to maintain health, prevent disease or the complications of disease, as well as those services intended to return or maintain a higher level of physical functioning." Category B includes "services provided by medical, dental, vision, or surgical specialists, as well as diagnostic tests, equipment, and supplies, whose purpose is the diagnosis and treatment of disease."[450](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn450 "HHS, IHS, \"Indian Health Service Medical Priority Levels,\" February 6, 2025, https://www.ihs.gov/sites/prc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/PRC_Medical_Priorities.pdf , p. 1.") In general, reproductive health care, such as those to screen and treat an STI, would be considered to be Priority 2—necessary services, those that are necessary for the diagnosis and treatment of chronic and non-emergent conditions.

IHS's 2025 document does not mention gender-affirming care. IHS does have a website that discusses "Two-Spirit/LGBTQI+ Health." The website includes a notice at the top stating that HHS was required to restore the website (as of February 14, 2025) because of a court order. It further states that the information on this page promoting gender ideology is extremely inaccurate and Trump Administration rejects gender ideology."[451](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn451 "HHS, IHS, \"Two-Spirit/.LGBTQI+ Health,\" https://www.ihs.gov/lgbt/ .") Prior PRC guidance had excluded GAC for that reason. The current guidance is silent on how GAC is treated for PRC purposes.

PRC programs are managed locally, and these local programs determine what priority level will be paid and may add or remove services within specific priority levels. In FY2025, 88% of IHS operated PRC programs were able to pay for services beyond Priority Level 2.[452](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn452 "IHS FY2027 Budget Justification, pp. CJ-78.")

Does IHS Provide Contraceptives?

As mentioned above, IHS does not have a standard medical benefit package, so services provided vary by facility. Most facilities offer pharmaceutical services that include contraception. IHS uses a National Core Formulary, which individual facilities can supplement with additional drugs depending on facility needs. The formulary includes oral contraceptives, IUDs, and implants.[453](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn453 "HHS, IHS, \"National Core Formulary,\" https://www.ihs.gov/nptc/formulary search/  .") IHS also expanded its formulary to include the Opill in 2023.[454](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn454 "HHS, IHS, \"National Pharmacy and Therapeutics Committee: Formulary Brief: OTC Norgestrel 0.075mg (Opill®)\" August 2023, https://www.ihs.gov/sites/nptc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/guidance/NPTC-Formulary-Brief-Opill-FINAL.pdf . See also text box in the \"What Are Contraceptive Services?\" section in this report.") As with other IHS services, pharmaceuticals are provided to eligible American Indians and Alaska Natives free of charge.

IHS provides EC (Plan B One-Step [Levonorgestrel]) through its pharmacies, emergency departments, and health clinics. The June 2013 FDA approval of Plan B One-Step as an over-the-counter drug presented a challenge for IHS, because the agency generally does not dispense drugs without a provider order.[455](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn455 "See, for example, Mary Annette Pember, \"Emergency Contraception Finally Available Through All IHS Facilities,\" Indian Country , October 19, 2015, https://indiancountrytodaymedianetwork.com/2015/10/19/emergency-contraception-finally-available-through-all-ihs-facilities-162134 .") This issue was resolved in October 2015, when IHS amended its internal policies to make EC available without a provider visit or a requirement that patients register with the facility.[456](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn456 "HHS, IHS, \"Indian Health Manual: Part 1- General, Chapter 15-Emergency Contraception,\" https://www.ihs.gov/ihm/pc/part-1/p1c15/ .") When services are not available at an IHS facility, PRC will pay for reproductive planning services, including LARCs, as a necessary (Priority 2) service (see [**Table 3**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref241235167) above).

IHS does provide sterilization services if requested, but it must follow HHS procedures when doing so.[457](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn457 "42 C.F.R. 50.205(b).") This service permits only tubal ligation or vasectomy and prohibits the use of a hysterectomy for purposes of sterilization. It also prohibits providing these procedures to anyone under the age of 21 or anyone incapable of giving consent.[458](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn458 "HHS, IHS, \" Indian Health Manual: Part 3 – Professional Services , Chapter 13-Maternal and Child Health\" https://www.ihs.gov/IHM/pc/part-3/p3c13/#3-13.12F5 .")

Does IHS Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

IHS is generally prohibited from using any of its appropriated funds to perform or pay for abortion services.[459](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn459 "25 U.S.C. §1676.") IHS funds may be used in cases where the mother's life is endangered, or if the pregnancy is the result of an act of rape or incest. IHS has developed and implemented protocols for its physicians to determine and certify cases when an abortion may be paid for; the pregnancy criteria described must be met to merit this circumstance.[460](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn460 "HHS, IHS, \" Indian Health Manual: Part 3 – Professional Services , Chapter 13-Maternal and Child Health\" https://www.ihs.gov/IHM/pc/part-3/p3c13/ and HHS, IHS, Indian Health Circular No. 22-15, \"Use of Indian Health Service Funds for Abortions,\" https://www.ihs.gov/  ihm/  circulars/  2022/  use-of-  indian-  health-  service-  funds-  for-  abortions/ .") In addition, IHS will provide health services necessary to terminate an ectopic pregnancy[461](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn461 "HHS, IHS, \" Indian Health Manual: Part 3 – Professional Services , Chapter 13-Maternal and Child Health\" https://www.ihs.gov/IHM/pc/part-3/p3c13/ .")—a pregnancy that occurs outside the womb (uterus)—which can be life-threatening.[462](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn462 "National Institutes of Health, National Library of Medicine, MedlinePlus Medical Encyclopedia, Ectopic pregnancy: MedlinePlus Medical Encyclopedia .")

IHS policies do not discuss abortion counseling, thus it is unclear whether the agency provides such services. It is also unclear which of the tiered IHS PRC medical priority groups abortion counseling would fit into if it were to be offered.

Does IHS Provide Infertility Services?

IHS provides some limited infertility services when obstetrician/gynecologist (OB/GYN) specialists are available at an IHS facility. In addition, each IHS area or specific facility may develop its own specific protocols. According to IHS's program manual (the agency's document governing its care),

the basic elements should be provided to women and men when requested and indicated, including history and exam, basal temperature charting, semen analysis and post coital testing, and serum progesterone assay. Endometrial biopsy, hysterosaipingography [sic] and diagnostic laparoscopy should be made available in those facilities with OB/GYN specialists on-site. Specific clinical protocols can be developed by consultation with gynecological consultants within each Area/Program.[463](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn463 "HHS, IHS, \" Indian Health Manual: Part 3 – Professional Services , Chapter 13-Maternal and Child Health\" https://www.ihs.gov/ihm/pc/part-3/p3c13/#3-13.12F4 . A hysterosalpingography is a contrast X-ray of the uterus and fallopian tubes.")

IHS is limited in terms of payments for infertility services under PRC—it is included within Category C as Priority Level 3—justifiable (see [**Table 3**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref241850751)).

Does IHS Provide Maternity Services?

IHS does not have a standard medical benefit that includes or excludes certain services, but some facilities provide reproductive health services and maternity care services. The IHS system includes 44 hospitals that offer inpatient care;[464](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn464 "IHS FY2027 Budget Justification pp. CJ-25 and CJ27.") however, specific data on the number of hospitals performing deliveries are not available. IHS has funded maternal health initiatives and proposes to focus on improving maternal health in the FY2027 budget request. Specifically, it is focused on obstetric preparedness in emergency departments. The agency stated that clinicians have completed Advanced Life Support in Obstetrics training and that these courses are offered regularly at IHS and tribal sites.[465](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn465 "IHS FY2027 Budget Justification p. CJ-51.")

IHS facilities that have access to obstetric services provide more comprehensive maternity services. In instances where these services are not available at the facility, PRC pays for deliveries and prenatal care as a Category C, Priority Level 1 (essential) services (see [**Table 3**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref241679790)).

Does IHS Provide Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

As noted above, the services available at IHS facilities vary, but some facilities may provide reproductive preventive services and treatment for conditions identified within the facility. Preventive screenings, such as routine mammograms, are included under the "Category A: Preventive and Rehabilitative Services" category and are considered to be essential services. [466](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn466 "For more information, see HHS, IHS, \"Purchased Referred Care, Requirements: Priorities of Care,\" https://www.ihs.gov/prc/eligibility/requirements-priorities-of-care/ .") IHS also funds or operates programs to screen individuals at risk of HIV/AIDS and to provide treatment services as necessary.[467](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn467 "HHS, IHS, \"HIV/AIDS,\" https://www.ihs.gov/hivaids/  .") These activities are coordinated through IHS's National HIV/AIDS Program, which coordinates the HIV/AIDS specific medical care delivered throughout the IHS system and undertakes public health activities related to prevention and testing.[468](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn468 "Ibid.") IHS provides access to both PrEP and PEP. As part of an attempt to reduce rates of HIV in the Native population, IHS simplified PrEP and PEP policies and created guides for providers to facilitate offering PrEP to eligible individuals.[469](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn469 "IHS, \"PrEP and PEP,\" https://www.ihs.gov/hivaids/clinicalinfo/prepandpep/ .")

Does IHS Provide Gender-Affirming Services?

In 2022, IHS approved adding gender-affirming medications to its core formulary.[470](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn470 "IHS, \"National Pharmacy and Therapeutics Committee: Formulary Brief: Gender-Affirming Medications,\" February 2022, https://www.ihs.gov/sites/nptc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/guidance/NPTC-Formulary-Brief-Gender-Affirming-Medications.pdf .") In the notice doing so, it stated that the criteria

for the use of hormone therapy for gender-affirmation in transgender people include: persistent, well-documented gender dysphoria, capacity (legal and otherwise) to provide informed consent, and medical and mental health concerns otherwise present must be reasonably well-controlled prior to starting hormone treatment. Provider responsibilities in initiating hormone therapy include evaluation and diagnosis and providing informed consent with discussion of the benefits and risks of gender-affirming medications—with particular attention to fertility risk and fertility preservation options.[471](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn471 "IHS, \"National Pharmacy and Therapeutics Committee: Formulary Brief: Gender-Affirming Medications,\" February 2022, https://www.ihs.gov/sites/nptc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/guidance/NPTC-Formulary-Brief-Gender-Affirming-Medications.pdf . A 2026 search of the National Core Formulary did not include testosterone, which was included in the 2022 formulary brief. It did include estradiol , with notes that this was for menopause treatment , with links to a 2025 document on...")

With this exception, IHS policies do not mention "gender-affirming" services. As noted above, IHS's website for "Two-Spirit/LGBTQI+ Health" includes a notice at the top stating that HHS was required to restore the website (as of February 14, 2025) because of a court order. It further states that "the information on this page promoting gender ideology is extremely inaccurate and Trump Administration rejects gender ideology."[472](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn472 "HHS, IHS, \"Two-Spirit/.LGBTQI+ Health,\" https://www.ihs.gov/lgbt/ .") IHS current PRC policies are silent on GAC. Prior IHS PRC policies had included GAC at an excluded service because it was considered to be experimental or cosmetic.[473](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn473 "Information on IHS's prior priority levels is available at https://web.archive.org/web/20241122213406/https://www.ihs.gov/sites/prc/themes/responsive2017/display_objects/documents/IHSMedicalPrioritiesRolesAndResponsibilities.docx .") It is not clear whether IHS offers or will pay for other types of gender-affirming services (see the section "[What Are Gender-Affirming Services?](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref241236338)" in this report), either through PRC or within its system.

Department of Veterans Affairs (VA)

The Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) provides health care services through the Veterans Health Administration (VHA) for approximately 9.3 million enrolled veterans[474](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn474 "Department of Veterans Affairs, FY202 7 Congressional Submission, Medical Programs , vol. 2 of 5, April 202 6, p. VHA- 37 .") at over 1,700 VA sites of care.[475](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn475 "FY202 7 Congressional Submission, Budget in Brief , April 2026 , p. BiB- 22 .") VHA is primarily a direct provider of care; it owns the facilities and employs the clinicians. However, under certain circumstances, VA will pay for a veteran to receive care in the community.[476](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn476 "Under certain circumstances, the VA is authorized to pay for primary and specialty care under the Veterans Community Care Program (38 U.S.C. §1703 and 38 C.F.R. §17.4000), for emergent care (38 U.S.C. §1725 and §1728), for urgent care (38 U.S.C. §1725A), and health care abroad (38 U.S.C. §1724), among others.")

Not all veterans qualify for enrollment in the VA health care system. Enrollment is based primarily on veteran status (i.e., previous military service), service-connected disability, and income.[477](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn477 "For more information on veterans health care eligibility and enrollment, see CRS Report R42747, Health Care for Veterans: Answers to Frequently Asked Questions .") All enrolled veterans are eligible for a standard medical package, which includes a full range of health care, gender-specific medical services, prescription drugs, long-term care, and social support services.[478](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn478 "38 C.F.R. §17.38.")

Does VA Provide Reproductive Health Services?

The VA standard medical benefits package includes reproductive health services, such as routine physical exams, cervical and prostate cancer screening, evaluation and treatment of vaginal infections, pelvic pain and abnormal uterine bleeding, treatment of erectile dysfunction, reproductive mental health, and STI screening, among other services, to eligible veterans who are enrolled in the VA's health care system.[479](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn479 "VA, Veterans Health Administration (VHA), Women's Health Services,  State of Reproductive Health In Women Veterans-VA Reproductive Health Diagnoses and Organization of Care , January 20 23 , p p . 3 6-38 .")

Does VA Provide Contraceptive Services?

VA provides both contraception counseling and contraceptives as part of the standard medical benefits package. VA uses a national formulary for medications.[480](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn480 "VA, VHA, VHA Formulary Management Process , VHA Directive 1108.08, July 2022 .") The formulary includes oral contraceptives, IUDs, and implants, among others.[481](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn481 "VA, \"Pharmacy Benefits Management Services, VA Formulary Advisor , https://www.va.gov/formularyadvisor/ . For a list of VA National Formulary Hormonal Contraceptives, see https://www.va.gov/formularyadvisor/DOC_PDF/CRE_Contraceptive_Agents_Hormonal_on_VA_National_Formulary_Rev_Aug_2026.pdf .") VA health care maintains a tiered structure for copayments for medication, which is dependent on each veteran's enrollment status. Some veterans are subject to copayments for medication, whereas some receive medication free of charge.[482](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn482 "For more information on copayments for medication, see CRS Report R42747, Health Care for Veterans: Answers to Frequently Asked Questions .")

VA provides EC (e.g., Plan B One Step [Levonorgestrel]). VA policy requires that EC be made available to patients on the same day as their appointment, even in cases where the provider requested to opt out from providing EC due to right-of-conscience claims.[483](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn483 "VA, VHA, Healthcare Services for Women Veterans , VHA Handbook 1330.01( 7 ), February 2017.")

VA provides sterilization services (e.g., salpingectomy, tubal occlusion procedures, and vasectomy) as part of the medical benefits package. All surgeons performing sterilization procedures must ensure that the patient is aware of the risks and benefits of the procedure, including the potential for regret, the chances of failure, the permanence of the sterilization procedure, and the availability of reversible, highly effective contraceptives (e.g., IUD and subcutaneous contraceptive implants).[484](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn484 "VA, VHA, Fertility Management , VHA Directive 1332, May 2024 .")

Does VA Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

Under current regulations, VA is prohibited from providing abortions or abortion counseling.[485](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn485 "38 C.F.R. §17.38(c)(1).") Medically necessary procedures for the management of a miscarriage are covered under the medical benefits package.[486](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn486 "A miscarriage, or pregnancy loss, is also called a spontaneous abortion , which is \"the unexpected loss of a fetus before the 20 th week of pregnancy, or gestation.\" ( Source: https://www.nlm.nih.gov/medlineplus/miscarriage.html . ) For clinical purposes, \"spontaneous abortion often is subdivided into threatened abortion, inevitable abortion, incomplete abortion, missed abortion, septic abortion, recurrent spontaneous abortion, and complete abortion.\" (Source: Craig P. Griebel et al....") In addition, VA provides care under the medical benefits package for ectopic pregnancies (i.e., pregnancy occurring outside the uterine cavity).[487](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn487 "VA, VHA, Maternity Health Care and Coordination, VHA Directive 1330.03, November 3, 2020, p. 2. VA directives do not describe the type of care provided for ectopic pregnan cy.")

Does VA Provide Infertility Services?

VA does provide certain infertility services to veterans. Covered infertility services for both female and male veterans are listed in [**Table 4**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref241236801). These covered services are provided to all enrolled veterans without exception.

Table 4. Infertility Services Offered by the VA

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| Diagnosis and Treatment for Female Veterans | Diagnosis and Treatment for Male Veterans |
| Diagnostic Tests:   * Laboratory blood testing: follicle stimulating hormone (FSH); thyroid stimulating hormone (TSH) * Genetic counseling and testing * Pelvic and/or transvaginal ultrasound * Hysterosalpingogram * Saline-infused sonohysterogram | Diagnostic Tests:   * Laboratory blood testing: serum testosterone, FSH, luteinizing hormone (LH), estradiol * Semen analysis * Genetic counseling and testing * Transrectal and/or scrotal ultrasonography * Postejaculatory urinalysis |
| Treatments:   * Surgical correction of structural pathology * Reversal of tubal ligation * Intrauterine insemination (IUI) * Medication for ovulation induction (e.g., clomiphene) * Injectable gonadotropin medications * Hormonal therapies (e.g., controlled ovarian hyperstimulation) * Additional hormonal therapies as approved by VA Pharmacy Benefits Management * Oocyte cryopreservation for medically indicated conditions up to 5 years. | Treatments:   * Evaluation and treatment of erectile dysfunction * Surgical correction of structural pathology * Vasectomy reversal * Hormonal therapies (e.g., clomiphene citrate, human chorionic gonadotropin, phosphodiesterase type 5 medications, testosterone) * Sperm retrieval techniques * Sperm cryopreservation for medically indicated conditions * Ejaculation techniques (e.g., electroejaculation, vibratory stimulation) |

**Source:** Prepared by CRS based on U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs (VA), Veterans Health Administration (VHA), *In Vitro Fertilization Counseling and Services Available to Certain Eligible Veterans and Their Spouses*, VHA Directive 1334(1), April 4, 2024.

**Notes:** This table, including terminology, is adapted directly from VHA Directive 1334(1). The use of gender-specific terminology to refer to available infertility services corresponds to how the services are represented in the directive.

VA is not authorized to provide or cover the cost of IVF or other ART. A narrow exception to this policy allows VA to provide IVF services to veterans and their spouses if a service-connected disability results in the inability of the veteran to procreate without the treatment.[488](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn488 "38 C.F.R. §17.380.") This exception is authorized on an annual basis through appropriations acts.[489](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn489 "This policy has been authorized in appropriations acts since FY2017. Most recently, Section 234 of Division D of the Continuing Appropriations, Agriculture, Legislative Branch, Military Construction and Veterans Affairs, and Extensions Act, 2026 ( P.L. 119-37 ), continued allowing the use of FY2026 appropriations and FY2027 advance appropriations for this purpose.") Such services and benefits may be provided in a manner similar to those described in a memorandum issued by the Assistant Secretary of Defense for Health Affairs,[490](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn490 "DOD, Office of the Assistant Secretary of Defense for Health Affairs, \"Policy for Assisted Reproductive Services for the Benefit of Seriously or Severely Ill/Injured (Category II or III) Active Duty Service Members,\" dated April 3, 2012.") along with guidance issued by DOD. VA is exempt from DOD requirements applicable to the duration of embryo cryopreservation and storage.[491](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn491 "VA, \"Final Rule-Fertility Counseling and Treatment for Certain Veterans and Spouses,\" 84 Federal Register 8254-8257, March 7, 2019.") Namely, VA may provide cryopreservation and storage for an unlimited amount of time.[492](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn492 "38 C.F.R. §17.380(b).")

Under this exception, VA allows the use of donor eggs, sperms and embryos. In addition, eligible veterans are not required to be married to access the benefit under this authority. However, a veteran's partner must be legally married to the veteran to receive evaluation or treatment.[493](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn493 "VA, \"Instructions for Determining Eligibility for In Vitro Fertilization (IVF) Benefit,\" 89 Federal Register 23518, March 28, 2024.") VA is not authorized to cover gestational surrogacy treatment under any circumstances.[494](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn494 "The authorization for VA's IVF benefit limits these services specifically to a covered veteran and the spouse of a covered veteran. Due to this limitation, despite the DOD memo allowing gestational surrogacy, VA cannot provide IVF services to an individual who is not a covered veteran or a spouse of a covered veteran.") This policy can affect any eligible veteran regardless of gender or sexual orientation. For example, a male veteran in a same-sex relationship could use this benefit to create a fertilized embryo with donor eggs, but VA will not pay for transfer of the embryo into a gestational carrier.[495](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn495 "VA, VHA, Vitro Fertilization Counseling and Services Available to Certain Eligible Veterans and Their Spouses, VHA Directive 1334(1), April 4, 2024. VA Office of Women's Health, FAQs: Expansion of In Vitro Fertilization at VA , March 11, 2024, https://www.womenshealth.va.gov/WOMENSHEALTH/docs/final_FAQs_for_Expansion_of_VA_Fertility_Benefits_3_11_24.pdf .")

Does VA Provide Maternity Services?

VA currently provides and pays for maternity and newborn health care services to eligible veterans and their family members.[496](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn496 "VA, VHA, Maternity Health Care and Coordination , VHA Handbook 1330.03, November 2020.") Veterans can access maternity care as soon as their pregnancies are confirmed. However, VA medical facilities do not provide onsite obstetrics services.[497](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn497 "VA, VHA, Women's Health Services,  State of Reproductive Health In Women Veterans-VA Reproductive Health Diagnoses and Organization of Care , January 2023 , p. 3 1 .") VA does not provide these services in its facilities since there is an insufficient population at each VA medical facility to ensure that clinicians can maintain their accreditation. As a result, VA furnishes maternity care through arrangements with community providers to ensure that pregnant veterans and veterans who wish to become pregnant have options near their homes. VA may perform emergency childbirth deliveries.

VA is authorized to provide certain health care services to a newborn child of a veteran receiving maternity care furnished by the VA. Health care for the newborn is authorized for a maximum of seven days after the birth of the child if the veteran delivered the child in a VA facility or in another facility pursuant to a VA contract for maternity services.[498](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn498 "38 U.S.C. §1786. The William M. (Mac) Thornberry National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2021 ( P.L. 116-283 ) created an exception to the seven-day maximum coverage allowance to authorize VA, pursuant to regulations prescribed by the Secretary of VA, to cover newborns for longer periods if medically necessary. However, VA has not promulgated regulations to implement this provision.")

Does VA Provide Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

VA provides reproductive health preventive services as part of the standard medical benefits package. Preventive screenings, such as mammography, are offered as part of routine health care. VA also operates a national HIV program with policies for screening, prevention, and treatment.[499](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn499 "VA, VHA, National Human Immunodeficiency Virus Program , VHA Directive 1304, August 2019.") It is VA policy that all veterans receiving care through the VA are tested for HIV at least once as part of their routine care. More frequent testing is available for veterans who are at higher risk of contracting HIV. VA follows CDC guidance regarding the use of PrEP, and it is a covered benefit for veterans enrolled in the VA health care system. All FDA-approved medications for PrEP must be readily available at all VA medical facilities, and such medications must be offered routinely as part of a comprehensive risk-reduction program for veterans who are considered to be at an increased risk for HIV infection.

In addition, VA provides medically necessary reproductive health treatment services as part of the standard medical benefits package. With limited exceptions (e.g., abortions and certain IVF discussed in previous sections), VA will provide care to individuals if the appropriate health care professionals determine that the care is needed to promote, preserve, or restore the health of the individual and is in accord with generally accepted standards of medical practice.[500](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn500 "38 C.F.R. §17.38(b).")

Does VA Provide Gender-Affirming Services?

Under current regulations, VA is prohibited from providing gender-confirming/affirming surgeries.[501](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn501 "38 C.F.R. §17.38(c)(4). On May 9, 2016, the VA received a petition for rulemaking to remove the exclusion for gender alterations. The VA sought comments regarding such removal in 2018. No action has been taken since. VA, \"Exclusion of Gender Alterations From the Medical Benefits Package,\" 83 Federal Register 31711, July 9, 2018.") As of March 2025, VA no longer provides medical treatment for gender dysphoria. This includes services such as hormone therapy, voice and communication trainings, and gender-affirming prosthetics, among others.[502](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn502 "VA, \"VA to phase out treatment for gender dysphoria,\" press release, March 17, 2025, https://news.va.gov/press-room/va-to-phase-out-treatment-for-gender-dysphoria/ .")

Prior to March 2025, VA provided some services as part of the standard medical benefits package, such as hormonal therapy, mental health care, and preoperative evaluation. In addition, VA provided medically necessary postoperative and long-term care following gender-confirming surgeries if an appropriate health care professional determined that the care is needed to promote, preserve, or restore the health of the individual and is in accord with generally accepted standards of medical practice.[503](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn503 "VA, VHA, Providing Health Care for Transgender and Intersex Veterans , VHA Directive 1341(2), February 2018 (rescinded).") Veterans who were already receiving such care from either VA or DOD prior to March 2025 will continue to receive it.[504](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn504 "VA, \"VA to phase out treatment for gender dysphoria,\" press release, March 17, 2025, https://news.va.gov/press-room/va-to-phase-out-treatment-for-gender-dysphoria/ .")

Grant Programs Focused on Reproductive Health

The following sections discuss federal programs that focus on one or more specific reproductive health topics. The first two programs—family planning and adolescent pregnancy prevention programs—discuss each of the reproductive health service categories included in this report. The final three questions for these programs focus on programs that provide specific reproductive health services; these questions discuss information about program missions and the specific services provided.

The Title X Family Planning Program

The Title X Family Planning Program (Title X) was enacted in 1970 as Title X of the PHSA.[505](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn505 "Title X was enacted by P.L. 91-572 , Family Planning Services and Population Research Act of 1970. It is codified as amended at 42 U.S.C. §§300 through 300a-6.") Title X provides grants to public and nonprofit agencies for family planning services, research, and training. The Office of Population Affairs (OPA) within HHS's Office of the Assistant Secretary for Health administers Title X, which is the only domestic federal program devoted solely to family planning and related preventive health services.[506](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn506 "Phil Killewald et al., Family Planning Annual Report: 2023 Summary , HHS, Office of Population Affairs (OPA), September 2024, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-08/2023-FPAR-national-summary.pdf . For more on the Title X program, see CRS In Focus IF10051, Title X Family Planning Program")

Title X of the PHSA authorizes the Secretary of HHS to award grants for projects to provide family planning services to any person desiring such services, with priority given to individuals with low incomes.[507](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn507 "42 U . S . C . 300a-4(c)(1) . Federal regulations 42 C.F.R. §59.2 further define low-income as a family whose income does not exceed 100% of the most recently issued Poverty Guidelines. In CY2026, a family of two with a combined income of $21,640 is considered 100% of the federal poverty level in all states except Alaska and Hawaii. See HHS, \"Annual Update of the HHS Poverty,\" 91 Federal Register 10, January 15, 2026.") Furthermore, services must be provided at no cost for individuals under 100% of the federal poverty level and to provide sliding scale fees for individuals between 100% and 250% of the federal poverty level.[508](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn508 "Individuals with a household income over 250% of the federal poverty level can still receive care at Title X facilities, but they must pay for the full cost of service. All individuals may utilize their health insurance coverage to potentially cover costs associated with care provision. 42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(7)-(8).") For unemancipated minors who request confidential services, eligibility for discounts is based on the minor's own income.[509](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn509 "42 C.F.R. §59.2 (defining low-income family ).") If a third-party is authorized or legally obligated to pay for services, such as an insurer or government agency, Title X programs must make all reasonable efforts to obtain third-party payment without application of discounts.[510](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn510 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(10).") The authorization of appropriations for the Title X program expired in FY1985, but Congress has continued to provide annual appropriations to carry out the program. For FY2026 Congress appropriated $286,479,000.[511](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn511 "See Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 [ P.L. 119-75 ], Division B, Title II.")

Title X is implemented through competitively awarded grants generally for a period of five years, subject to available annual appropriations for the program. Title X grantees can provide family planning services to clients directly or they can subaward Title X monies to other entities to provide services. In 2023, the most recent year for which client data are available, Title X projects served 2.80 million clients through 3,853 service sites operated by 95 grantees and their 1,614 subrecipients (also known as subgrantees or subawardees).[512](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn512 "Phil Killewald et al., Family Planning Annual Report: 2023 Summary , HHS, Office of Population Affairs (OPA), September 2024, p. 42, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-08/2023-FPAR-national-summary.pdf . Current and past directories of Title X grantees, subawardees, and service sites are at HHS, OPA, Current Title X Service Grant Recipient s, https://opa.hhs.gov/grant-programs/title-x-service-grants/current-title-x-service-grant-recipients . For the 2022 project year , OP...")

Do Title X Projects Provide Reproductive Health Services?

Title X projects are required to provide a "broad range of acceptable and effective family planning methods and services (including natural family planning methods, infertility services, and services for adolescents)."[513](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn513 "42 U.S.C. §300(a).") Title X regulations define *family planning services* to include certain reproductive health services, such as

a broad range of medically approved services, which includes Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-approved contraceptive products and natural family planning methods, for clients who want to prevent pregnancy and space births, pregnancy testing and counseling, assistance to achieve pregnancy, basic infertility services, sexually transmitted infection (STI) services, and other preconception health services.[514](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn514 "42 C.F.R. §59. 2 (defining family planning services ).")

Current regulations further specify that Title X projects must provide services in a manner that "ensures equitable and quality service delivery consistent with nationally recognized standards of care."[515](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn515 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(3).") The program's policies for providing such care to clients points to guidance outlined in *Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S.* *Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024)* (QFP).[516](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn516 "HHS, OPA, OPA Program Policy Notice 2024-02—Implementing Quality Family Planning Services Recommendations within Title X Projects , OPA Program Policy Notice: 2024 – 02, November 19, 2024, https://opa.hhs.gov/node/4173 . Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6, Supplement (December 2024), pp. S41-S86.") QFP provides guidance for relevant Title X reproductive health services, such as breast and cervical cancer screening and prevention; STI and HIV prevention education, counseling, testing, treatment, and referral; preconception health services; basic infertility services; and counseling on establishing a reproductive life plan.[517](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn517 "Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6, Supplement (December 2024), pp. S41-S86. HHS, OPA, \"Title X Program Expectations,\" December 2024, https://opa.hhs.gov/grant-programs/title-x-service-grants/about-title-x-service-grants/title-x-program-expectations .")

Do Title X Projects Provide Contraceptive Services?

As noted above, program regulations define *family planning services* to include FDA-approved contraceptive products and natural family planning methods.[518](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn518 "42 C.F.R. §59.2; see Table 1 in \" What Are Contraceptive and Contraceptive Counseling Services? \" .") Program regulations require that each Title X project must provide "a broad range of acceptable and effective medically approved family planning methods (including natural family planning methods…"[519](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn519 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(1).")

Program regulations also require projects to provide contraceptive services related to family planning (including consultation by a clinician, examination, prescription and continuing supervision, laboratory examination, contraceptive supplies), in-person or via telehealth, and any referral needed to other health care when indicated, and provide for the effective usage of contraceptive devices and practices.[520](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn520 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(b )(1).") The regulations permit the HHS Secretary to omit this requirement, with an established good cause.[521](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn521 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(b).")

The QFP, last updated in 2024, advises providers that contraceptive services should include (1) establishing and maintaining client rapport; (2) asking about a client's contraceptive preferences; (3) determining with the client which contraceptive method (if any) aligns with the client's preferences; (4) conducting exams related to contraceptive use, if indicated; and (5) providing or referring for the chosen method, including giving instructions about correct and consistent use.[522](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn522 "Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6, Supplement (December 2024), pp. S41-S86.") Regulations require that Title X service sites unable to provide clients with access to a broad range of acceptable and effective medically approved contraceptives must be able to provide a prescription to the client for their method of choice, or referrals to another provider, as requested.[523](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn523 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(1).") See [**Table 1**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref173940442) in "[What Are Contraceptive and Contraceptive Counseling Services?](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#_Ref174105078)" for a list of FDA-approved contraceptive methods.[524](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn524 "Title X clinical guidelines are laid out in HHS, OPA, OPA Program Policy Notice 2024-02—Implementing Quality Family Planning Services Recommendations within Title X Projects , OPA Program Policy Notice: 2024 – 02, November 19, 2024, https://opa.hhs.gov/node/4173 . See also HHS, OPA, Title X Program Handbook , December 2024, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/title-x-program-handbook-dec-2024.pdf .")

OPA's Title X *Family Planning Annual Report* presents the following 2023 data on female Title X clients' primary contraceptive methods:[525](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn525 "Phil Killewald et al., Family Planning Annual Report: 2023 Summary , HHS, Office of Population Affairs (OPA), September 2024, p. 67, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-08/2023-FPAR-national-summary.pdf . Percentages may not sum to 100% due to rounding. C ontraceptive methods and their effectiveness are listed in Table 1 .")

* 21% relied on the "most effective" methods (including vasectomy, female sterilization, implants, and IUDs);
* 33% relied on "moderately effective" methods (including injectable contraception, vaginal ring, contraceptive patch, and pills);
* 20% relied on "less effective" methods (including male condoms, female condoms, contraceptive sponge, diaphragm or cervical cap, withdrawal, fertility awareness-based methods [FAM] and lactational amenorrhea methods [LAM], spermicides used alone, and non-spermicidal gel used alone);
* 5% relied on abstinence;
* 15% used no contraceptive methods because, for example, they were pregnant or seeking to become pregnant; and
* for 7%, the primary contraceptive method was unknown.

The *Family Planning Annual Report* presents the following 2023 data on male Title X clients' primary contraceptive methods:[526](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn526 "Phil Killewald et al., Family Planning Annual Report: 2023 Summary , HHS, Office of Population Affairs (OPA), September 2024, p. 72, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-08/2023-FPAR-national-summary.pdf . Percentages may not sum to 100% due to rounding. C ontraceptive methods and their effectiveness are listed in Table 1 .")

* 59% relied on any method of contraception, including relying on their partner to use any method of contraception;
* 11% relied on abstinence;
* 14% reported not using any method; and
* for 12% of male clients, primary contraception was unknown.

Do Title X Projects Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

By law, Title X funds may not be used for abortions.[527](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn527 "42 U.S.C. §300a-6.") In addition, language in annual Departments of Labor, Health and Human Services, and Education, and Related Agencies appropriations bills have also stated that Title X funds "shall not be expended for abortions" and "that all pregnancy counseling shall be nondirective."[528](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn528 "Consolidated Appropriations Act, 202 6 [ P.L. 119-75 ], Division B , Title II.") Under program guidance, the prohibition on abortion does not apply to all the activities of a Title X grantee; it applies only to activities that are within the Title X project.[529](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn529 "HHS, OPA, Title X Program Handbook , December 2024, p. 27, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/title-x-program-handbook-dec-2024.pdf .") If the grantee provides abortion services, its abortion activities have to be "separate and distinct" from the Title X project activities.[530](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn530 "HHS, OPA, \"Provision of Abortion-Related Services in Family Planning Services Projects,\" 65 Federal Register 41281-41282, July 3, 2000, https://federalregister.gov/a/00-16759 . Program guidance states that a grantee's abortion-related activities and its Title X project activities can share the same facility, staff, waiting room, and records system, \"so long as it is possible to distinguish between the Title X supported activities and non-Title X abortion-related activities,\" for example,...") The guidance notes that "a Title X project may not provide services that directly facilitate the use of abortion as a method of family planning, such as providing transportation for an abortion, explaining and obtaining signed abortion consent forms from clients interested in abortions, negotiating a reduction in fees for an abortion, and scheduling or arranging for the performance of an abortion, promoting or advocating abortion within Title X program activities, or failing to preserve sufficient separation between Title X program activities and abortion-related activities."[531](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn531 "65 Federal Register 41281.")

Program regulations require Title X projects to offer pregnant clients information and counseling on each of the following options: prenatal care and delivery; infant care, foster care, or adoption; and pregnancy termination.[532](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn532 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(5)(i).") If the client requests such information and counseling, the project has to give "neutral, factual information and nondirective counseling on each of the options, and referral upon request, except with respect to any option(s) about which the pregnant client indicates they do not wish to receive such information and counseling."[533](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn533 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(5)(ii). In May 2025 , HHS wrote to the Tennessee Title X grantee, \"Tennessee is one of only two states to have lost funding for failure to comply with the Title X 2021 regulations requiring counseling and referral for abortion, 42 CFR § 59.5(a)(5). The Department is declining to enforce this provision against the state, and you may rely on this letter to that effect.\"")

Do Title X Projects Provide Infertility Services?

Title X authorizing statute and program regulations require projects to provide infertility services that involve services for both partners of an infertile couple.[534](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn534 "42 U.S.C. §300(a). 42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(1). Program regulations also define family planning services as including \"basic infertility services\" (42 C.F.R. §59.2).") Basic infertility services include understanding the client's reproductive life plan, their difficulty achieving pregnancy by taking a medical history, and providing sexual health assessments and physical exams consistent with QFP recommendations.[535](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn535 "HHS, OPA, Title X Program Handbook , December 2024, p. 17, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/title-x-program-handbook-dec-2024.pdf .") Clinical guidelines recommend screening all persons attempting to achieve pregnancy for infertility, providing counseling and resources to clients, and referring clients promptly for specialty care for clients who want to pursue medically assisted reproduction.[536](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn536 "Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6, Supplement (December 2024), pp. S41-S86. See section \" What Are Infertility Services? \" for a description of infertility and ART services.") Medically assisted reproductive services, such as IVF or other ART, are generally provided by other providers. A 2024 study examining directory data of Title X and ART clinics found no ART clinics received Title X funding.[537](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn537 "Junying Zhao et al., \"Early national trends in non-abortion reproductive care access after Roe,\" Frontiers in Public Health , vol. 12, no. 1309068 (March 2024), p. 2.")

Do Title X Projects Provide Maternity Services?

Program regulations require Title X projects to provide a broad range of family planning services, including pregnancy testing and counseling to clients who are pregnant.[538](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn538 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(1). Program regulations also define family planning services as including \"pregnancy testing and counseling\" (42 C.F.R. §59.2).") In 2023, 6% of female clients reported using no method of contraception because they were pregnant or seeking to become pregnant.[539](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn539 "Phil Killewald et al., Family Planning Annual Report: 2023 Summary , HHS, Office of Population Affairs (OPA), September 2024, p. 67, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-08/2023-FPAR-national-summary.pdf .") With respect to pregnancy counseling, program regulations require Title X projects to offer pregnant clients information and counseling on each of the following options: prenatal care and delivery; infant care, foster care, or adoption; and pregnancy termination.[540](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn540 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(5)(i).") If the client requests such information and counseling, the project has to give "neutral, factual information and nondirective counseling on each of the options, and referral upon request, except with respect to any option(s) about which the pregnant client indicates they do not wish to receive such information and counseling."[541](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn541 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(5)(ii).")

With respect to referrals, program regulations generally require Title X projects to provide for "coordination and use of referrals and linkages with primary healthcare providers, other providers of healthcare services, local health and welfare departments, hospitals, voluntary agencies, and health services projects supported by other federal programs, who are in close physical proximity to the Title X site, when feasible, in order to promote access to services and provide a seamless continuum of care."[542](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn542 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(b)(8).")

Do Title X Projects Provide Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

Federal regulations require Title X projects to provide clients with "sexually transmitted infection (STI) services, and other preconception health services."[543](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn543 "42 C.F.R. §59. 2 defining family planning services .") Title X clinical guidelines recommend that providers offer STI services in accordance with the CDC's STI treatment and HIV testing guidelines, and cervical and breast cancer screening in accordance with professional recommendations, such as USPSTF recommendations.[544](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn544 "Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6, Supplement (December 2024), pp. S41-S86 . See text box \"Standards of Care\" in the HHS, OPA, Title X Program Handbook , December 2024, p. 11, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/title-x-program-handbook-dec-2024.pdf .42 C.F.R. § 59.5(a)(1); and 42 C.F.R. § 59.2...") Title X clinical guidelines emphasize that clinics offering sexual and reproductive health services may be the only source of health care for people of reproductive age, so both the provision of preventive health services and referral to other health care providers are an important component to providing care at Title X service sites.[545](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn545 "Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6, Supplement (December 2024), pp. S41-S86.")

The *Family Planning Annual Report* presents the following 2023 data on reproductive prevention and treatment services provided to clients:

* 433,844 female clients screened for cervical cancer, 18% of all female clients;
* 1,106,746 female clients tested for chlamydia, 47% of all female clients;
* 236,657 male clients tested for chlamydia, 56% of all male clients;
* 1,325,638 gonorrhea tests for female clients;[546](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn546 "The percent age of clients who receive gonorrhea, syphilis, and HIV tests are not included in the Family Planning Annual Report .")
* 241,477 gonorrhea tests for male clients;
* 544,429 syphilis tests for female clients;
* 190, 450 syphilis tests for male clients;
* 729,637 HIV tests for female clients;
* 254,738 HIV tests for male clients.[547](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn547 "Phil Killewald et al., Family Planning Annual Report: 2023 Summary , HHS, Office of Population Affairs (OPA), September 2024, pp. 74, 76-78, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-08/2023-FPAR-national-summary.pdf .")

In general, Title X services focus on family planning and related *preventive* health services, but treatment services are more limited. Title X clinical guidelines recommend that providers offer STI services in accordance with the CDC's STI treatment guidelines.[548](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn548 "Sarah E. Romer et al., \"Providing Quality Family Planning Services in the United States: Recommendations of the U.S. Office of Population Affairs (Revised 2024),\" American Journal of Preventive Medicine , vol. 67, no. 6, Supplement (December 2024), pp. S41-S86; and Kimberly A. Workowski, Laura H. Bachmann, and Philip A. Chan, \"Sexually Transmitted Infections Treatment,\" Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report, Recommendations and Reports , vol. 70, no. 4 (July 23, 2021).")

With regard to HIV/AIDS and cancers of reproductive organs, Title X clinical guidelines recommend various services related to prevention and screening, such as supplemental testing and prompt referral. Title X projects focus on prevention and may not have the capacity to treat such conditions.[549](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn549 "HHS, OPA, Title X Program Handbook , December 2024, p. 17, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/title-x-program-handbook-dec-2024.pdf .") Title X regulations require projects more generally to "provide for coordination and use of referrals and linkages with primary health care providers, other providers of health care services, local health and welfare departments, hospitals, voluntary agencies, and health services projects supported by other federal programs, that are in close physical proximity to the Title X site, when feasible, in order to promote access to services and provide a seamless continuum of care."[550](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn550 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(b)(8).")

In 2022, HHS described the availability of treatment services under Title X:

Regarding HIV/AIDS treatment services, Title X projects provide screening and prevention, through the distribution of PrEP, for instance, however Title X funds are not used for treatment. Title X program funding is limited to services necessary to help individuals prevent or achieve pregnancy, and to help individuals determine the number and spacing of children. Thus, Title X funds are not used for treatment.

Similarly, screening for cancers of reproductive organs (e.g., breast cancer, cervical cancer) is eligible for Title X funding, but treatment is not eligible.[551](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn551 "Emails from the HHS Office of the Assistant Secretary for Legislation, February 3, 2020, December 1, 2020, and July 1, 2022.")

Do Title X Projects Provide Gender-Affirming Services?

Title X program funding is limited to services necessary to prevent or achieve pregnancy, and help individuals determine whether to have children and, if so, how to space pregnancies. Therefore, gender-affirming medications and procedures outside of family planning services are outside the scope of Title X projects.[552](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn552 "Emails from the HHS Office of the Assistant Secretary for Legislation, February 3, 2020, December 1, 2020, and July 1, 2022.")

Although gender-affirming medications and procedures are not services covered by Title X funds, current federal regulations require Title X projects to "provide services in a manner that does not discriminate against any client based on religion, race, color, national origin, disability, age, sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, sex characteristics, number of pregnancies, or marital status."[553](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn553 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(4).") Title X program regulations also encourage taking a client-centered approach by, among other things, delivering services in a culturally appropriate and inclusive manner so as to meet the needs of all clients, including those who are lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, or queer (LGBTQ+). [554](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn554 "42 C.F.R. §59.5(a)(3) and 42 C.F.R. § 59.2 defin e client-centered , culturally and linguistically appropriate services , and inclusive . OASH policy priorities currently include ending diversity, equity, and inclusion policies and practices across its programs. See OASH, \" Priorities of Office of the Assistant Secretary for Health ,\" https://health.gov/priorities .")

In addition, Title X is subject to nondiscrimination law and projects must be administered "in compliance with federal civil rights laws that prohibit discrimination on the basis of race, color, national origin, disability, age and, in some circumstances, religion, conscience, and sex (including gender identity, sexual orientation, and pregnancy)."[555](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn555 "HHS, OPA, Title X Program Handbook , December 2024, p. 30, https://opa.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/title-x-program-handbook-dec-2024.pdf .")

What Are Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs?

Given the consequences associated with adolescent births for both the adolescent parents and their children, federal law authorizes programs designed to delay sexual activity and prevent pregnancy and the spread of STIs among children and adolescents.[556](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn556 "For further information about adolescent birth rates and consequences of adolescent pregnancy , see CRS Report R45184, Teen Births in the United States: Overview and Recent Trends .") Four HHS programs focus exclusively on providing adolescent pregnancy prevention education: the (1) Teen Pregnancy Prevention (TPP) program, (2) the Personal Responsibility Education Program (PREP), (3) the Title V Sexual Risk Avoidance Education program, and (4) the General Departmental Management (GDM) Sexual Risk Avoidance Education program.[557](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn557 "Despite their similar names and purposes, the latter two programs have different authorizing laws and funding mechanisms. For FY202 6 , the Teen Pregnancy Prevention (TPP) program was funded at $101 million; the Personal Responsibility Education Program (PREP) and Title V Sexual Risk Avoidance Education programs were each funded at $75 million (prior to sequestration); and the General Departmental Management (GDM) Sexual Risk Avoidance Education program was funded at $35 million. For inform...") These programs generally serve children and youth, with a focus on those with risk factors for adolescent pregnancy. HHS awards program funding both competitively and by formula to grantees that may include states, community-based organizations, and selected other entities. The programs provide education and social supports in schools, afterschool programs, community centers, and other settings. The activities carried out under these programs vary, but they generally seek to support youth in making healthy choices about preventing pregnancy and STIs and reducing sexual risk behaviors.

Do Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs Provide Reproductive Health Services?

Adolescent pregnancy prevention programs are intended to prevent pregnancy, STIs, and associated sexual risk behaviors for children and youths through preventive education. The programs vary in their approaches and specific activities.[558](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn558 "CRS Report R45183, Adolescent Pregnancy: Federal Prevention Programs .")

Grantees that use a *sexual risk avoidance* approach emphasize refraining from nonmarital sexual activity. Such programs may also address *sexual risk cessation*, or discontinuing consensual sexual activity after having engaged in it.[559](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn559 "Mathematica, Factors Influencing Youth Sexual Activity: Conceptual Models for Sexual Risk Avoidance and Cessation for HHS, OASH and ACF, Office of Planning, Research, and Evaluation (OPRE), January 2021.") Both approaches may provide information about preventing STDs and HIV, the benefits of practicing sexual abstinence, the risks that can be associated with sexual activity outside of marriage, and strategies and tactics to practice abstaining from sex and building relationships without having sex.[560](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn560 "See for example, HHS Teen Pregnancy Prevention Review , \"Adult Identity Mentoring (Project AIM) Program Overview\" and \"Heritage Keepers Abstinence Education Program Overview , \" available through https://youth.gov/evidence-innovation/tpper/compare-programs . These are examples of abstinence education approaches and are included for illustrative purposes only.") Grantees that implement broader sexual health education programs may focus on increasing participants' knowledge about STDs and HIV, reducing risk behaviors, and building skills in problem solving and negotiation related to relationships and sexual activity. Some programs may encourage abstinence, negotiating skills around abstaining from sex, improving contraceptive use, and using condoms correctly, among other topics.[561](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn561 "See for example, HHS Teen Pregnancy Prevention Review , \"¡Cuídate! Program Overview\" and \"Be Proud! Be Responsible Program Overview , \" available through https://youth.gov/evidence-innovation/tpper/compare-programs . These are examples of broader sexual health education approaches and are included for illustrative purposes only.") The Title V Sexual Risk Avoidance Education and the GDM Sexual Risk Avoidance Education programs focus exclusively on abstaining from premarital sex. The PREP program requires most grantees to place "substantial emphasis on both abstinence and contraception for the prevention of pregnancy among youth and sexually transmitted infections."[562](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn562 "Section 513(b)(2)(A)(i) of the SSA.") Under the TPP program, either or both approaches may be used.

Grantees that receive funding under these four programs use education models that have been developed by research organizations and other entities, with curriculum that is generally carried out by trained facilitators. Some of these programs are identified in HHS's Teen Pregnancy Prevention Evidence Review as being effective in improving behaviors related to (1) sexual activity, (2) the number of sexual partners, (3) contraceptive use, (4) STIs or HIV, and/or (5) pregnancies.[563](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn563 "The Teen Pregnancy Prevention Evidence Review was managed by the Assistant Secretary for Planning and Evaluation (ASPE) in collaboration with the Administration for Children and Families' (ACF 's ) Family and Youth Services Bureau (FYSB), and the former Office of Adolescent Health (OAH) within OASH. HHS has contract ed with Mathematica Policy Research, Inc., a social policy research organization, to review studies of teen pregnancy prevention programs. Note that the review was active fr...")

Do Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs Provide Contraceptive Services?

As noted, grantees that use a broader approach to providing sexual health education may use program models that provide information about contraceptives, including education on the proper use of contraceptives. Some grantees may potentially provide contraceptives such as condoms given there is no identified prohibition on distributing them in statute or program guidance.

Do Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs Provide Abortions or Abortion Counseling?

As discussed above, the Hyde Amendment has routinely been added to the annual appropriations measure for HHS to restrict federal funds to pay for abortions, except in cases of rape, incest, or endangerment of a mother's life.[564](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn564 "For more information about the Hyde Amendment, see \" Can Federal Funds Be Used to Pay for Abortions or Abortion Counseling? \" in this report.") Two of the four adolescent pregnancy prevention programs, the GDM Sexual Risk Avoidance Education program and the TPP program, are funded via annual appropriations measures for HHS; therefore, the Hyde Amendment applies to these programs.

The other two programs, Title V Sexual Risk Avoidance Education program and PREP, are funded via mandatory appropriations through their authorizing statutes under SSA Title V. These authorizing provisions do not address abortion. However, in a 2024 funding announcement for the Title V Sexual Risk Avoidance Education program, HHS specified that "Referrals cannot be made to family planning organizations that provide abortions." HHS further specified that "referral resources should include, but not be limited to, substance use and abuse and mental health services."[565](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn565 "See, HHS, ACF, Administration on Children, Youth, and Families (ACYF), FYSB, Standing Announcement for Title V State Sexual Risk Avoidance Education , HHS-2024-ACF-ACYF-SRAE-0044, 2024. The funding announcement for the Title V Competitive Sexual Risk Avoidance Education program (HHS-2024-ACF-ACYF-TS-0040, 2024) does not appear to address abortion.") The PREP grant announcements do not appear to address abortion.[566](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn566 "See, for example, HHS, ACF, FYSB, Competitive Personal Responsibility Education Program (PREP ) , HHS-2026-ACF-ACYF-AK-0014, 2026.") In the absence of program guidance on the topic, general HHS guidance on prohibiting funding for abortions applies.[567](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn567 "HHS, Office of the Assistant Secretary for Resources and Technology, Office of Grants, HHS Grants Policy Statement , January 1, 2007.")

Do Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs Provide Infertility Services?

The adolescent pregnancy prevention programs do not provide infertility services.

Do Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs Provide Maternity Services?

The adolescent pregnancy prevention programs do not provide maternity services.

Do Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs Provide Reproductive Health Prevention and Treatment Services?

The adolescent pregnancy prevention programs do not provide reproductive health screening or treatment services. The programs do address preventive services to prevent pregnancy, STDs, and related sexual risk outcomes.

Do Adolescent Pregnancy Prevention Programs Provide Gender-Affirming Services?

The adolescent pregnancy prevention programs do not provide gender-affirming services.

What Federal Grant Programs Address Sexually Transmitted Infections (STIs)?

Both CDC and HRSA provide funding to address STIs. CDC's programs seek to prevent and control multiple STIs, while HRSA programs focus on HIV/AIDS specifically.

What Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) Programs Address STIs?

A number of programs administered by CDC address STIs, including several grant programs administered by the CDC National Center for HIV, Viral Hepatitis, STD, and TB Prevention (NCHHSTP).[568](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R46785.html#fn568 "CDC, NCHHSTP , \" NCHHSTP Partners and Programs , \" June 13, 2026, https://www.cdc.gov/nchhstp/partners-programs/index.html . CDC, \"CDC's HIV, Viral Hepatitis, STD, and Tuberculosis Prevention Budget,\" June 22, 2026, https://www.cdc.gov/nchhstp/budget-funding/index.html .") For example

* **HIV Prevention and Control:**CDC provides technical and funding assistance to community-based organizations and state/local health departments on many aspects of planning, implementing, and evaluation of HIV prevention programs.
